The L p L^{p} regularity problem for parabolic operators Thanks: The first author has been supported in part by EPSRC grant EP/Y033078/1.
Martin Dindoš, Linhan Li, Jill Pipher

Abstract
. In this paper, we fully resolve the question of whether the Regularity problem for the parabolic PDE − ∂ t u + div ( A ∇ u ) = 0 -\partial_{t}u+\mathrm{div}(A\nabla u)=0 on a Lipschitz cylinder 𝒪 × ℝ \mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R} is solvable for some p ∈ ( 1 , ∞ ) p\in(1,\infty) under the assumption that the matrix A A is elliptic, has bounded and measurable coefficients and its coefficients satisfy a natural Carleson condition (a parabolic analog of the so-called DKP-condition). We prove that for some p 0 > 1 p_{0}>1 the Regularity problem is solvable in the range ( 1 , p 0 ) (1,p_{0}) . We note that answer to this question was not known even in the small Carleson case , that is, when the Carleson norm of coefficients is sufficiently small. In the elliptic case the analogous question was only fully resolved recently independently by two groups, with two very different methods: one involving two of the authors and S. Hofmann, the second by M. Mourgoglou, B. Poggi and X. Tolsa. Our approach in the parabolic case is motivated by that of the first group, but in the parabolic setting there are significant new challenges.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
2020 Mathematics Subject Classification: 35K20, 35K10

1.
Introduction

Inspired by recent breakthroughs (
[
14
]
,
[
49
]
) that answered some longstanding questions concerning elliptic Regularity boundary value problems,
this paper proves sharp solvability of the Regularity problem for
parabolic differential equations in divergence form
where the coefficients
satisfy a natural and well-studied minimal smoothness assumption.

The minimal smoothness condition we impose on the coefficients of
A
has a long history, first formulated in a conjecture
of Dahlberg concerning solvability of the Dirichlet problem for elliptic divergence form equations. It arises from a
particular change of
variable that is relevant to the study of smooth equations, both elliptic and parabolic, defined in Lipschitz domains (Dahlberg, Kenig, Nečas, Stein
[
9
,
46
]
).
This smoothness assumption
is expressed as a
Carleson measure
condition of arbitrary magnitude that is satisfied by the coefficients of the matrix
associated to the operator.
Up to now, only the Dirichlet problem has been studied for parabolic operators in this general regime.

The operators we consider
have the form

(1.1)

\mathcal{L}u:=-\partial_{t}u+\divg(A\nabla u)=0\quad\text{in }\Omega,

and will be defined in a domain
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
where
\mathcal{O}=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
or
\mathcal{O}
is a bounded or unbounded Lipschitz domain (Definition
2.10
).

The matrix
A=[a_{ij}(X,t)]
is a
n\times n
matrix satisfying the uniform ellipticity condition with
X\in\mathcal{O}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
That is, there exists positive constants
\lambda
and
\Lambda
such that

(1.2)

\lambda|\xi|^{2}\leq\sum_{i,j}a_{ij}(X,t)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\leq\Lambda|\xi|^{2}

for almost every
(X,t)\in\Omega
and all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Specifically for parabolic equations, we assume that
the measure defined by

(1.3)

d\mu=\left(\delta(X,t)|\nabla A|^{2}+\delta(X,t)^{3}|\partial_{t}A|^{2}\right)dXdt

is the density of a Carleson measure on
\Omega
with Carleson norm
\|\mu\|_{C}
and, moreover,

(1.4)

\delta(X,t)|\nabla A|+\delta(X,t)^{2}|\partial_{t}A|\leq K<\infty.

Here and in the sequel,
\delta(X,t)
denotes the parabolic distance to the boundary of
\Omega
, which is defined as:

\delta(X,t)=\inf_{(Y,\tau)\in\partial\Omega}\left(\left|X-Y\right|^{2}+\left|t-\tau\right|\right)^{1/2}.

The condition (
1.3
) can further be viewed as imposing a constraint on the oscillation of the coefficients of
A
. This related oscillation condition was first formulated and investigated
in
[
18
]
, and is especially useful as it does not require differentiability of the coefficients of
A
. We shall also provide solvability results here under this weaker condition.

The boundary value problems we consider are posed for boundary data and/or certain derivatives of data belonging to an
L^{p}
space, for
1<p<\infty
.
This inquiry has classical
roots in the study of harmonic functions or solutions to the heat equation in smooth domains, where, as in the classical theory, the objective is to prove nontangential convergence of solutions to
their boundary data. (See
2.26
and
2.31
for definitions.)
The
L^{p}
Dirichlet problem when
p=2
can be thought of as being
1/2
derivative below the natural class of
energy solutions
, the weak solutions that arise from applying the Lax-Milgram lemma. These Lax-Milgram solutions have finite energy, meaning that
\iint_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}<\infty
, and also have traces in the Besov-Sobolev space
\dot{B}^{2,2}_{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
. This trace space is an
L^{2}
-based space of functions having
1/2
-derivative on
\partial\Omega
,
appropriately understood. Such a class of energy solutions can also be defined in the parabolic setting, and this will be done in subsection
2.2
.

We now provide some details which will set the context for this paper’s study of the solvability of the parabolic Regularity problem, beginning with a brief review of what’s known for elliptic equations.
The study of elliptic divergence form operators under the analogous assumption - that the gradient of the coefficients of the matrix satisfies a Carleson condition - has
been well developed over more than two decades by numerous authors, and even enjoys fruitful connections
to the study of geometric properties of the boundary of the domain such as uniform rectifiability (see
[
2
,
4
,
39
,
37
,
38
,
30
]
).
In general these smoothness assumptions fall into two different regimes: one where the norm bound
\|\mu\|_{C}
in (
1.3
) is of arbitrary magnitude and a second where it is assumed to be
sufficiently small. In the elliptic setting, the “large Carleson norm” condition implies that the elliptic measure belongs to the Muckenhoupt weight class
A^{\infty}

with respect to surface measure, and this in turn implies solvability of the Dirichlet
problem with data belonging to
L^{p}
for some (possibly large) value of
p
. This was shown in
[
43
]
in 2001. In the “small Carleson norm” regime, the elliptic measure is provably
much better behaved with respect to surface measure,
yielding solvability of the
L^{p}
Dirichlet problem for all
1<p<\infty
. This was the main result of
[
18
]
in 2007, completing the program of solving these particular
elliptic Dirichlet boundary value problems.

The Regularity problem imposes an additional smoothness assumption on the Dirichlet data, namely, existence of “tangential derivatives” in
L^{p}
, while solvability requires a nontangential estimate on the gradient of solutions. When the elliptic Regularity problem is solvable with data in some
L^{p}
space, there is a remarkable duality between the range of solvability of the Dirichlet and Regularity problems; namely, the Dirichlet problem is solvable in the range
(p_{0},\infty)
if and only if the Regularity problem for its adjoint is solvable in the range
(1,p_{0}^{\prime})
, where
1/p_{0}+1/p_{0}^{\prime}=1
(
[
41
,
52
]
). It is an open question whether this duality of ranges holds without the assumption that Regularity is solvable at some point in the
L^{p}
scale. This highlights one of the sources of interest in the Regularity problem. Another source is interest in the companion boundary value problem, namely, the Neumann problem, which prescribes normal (or “co-normal”) derivatives of the solution on the boundary, as opposed to tangential derivatives. While apparently closely connected,
solvability of the Neumann problem is much less well understood. Solvability of both the elliptic
L^{p}
Regularity and Neumann
problem in the small Carleson norm regime (on coefficients) was first established for domains
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
in
[
21
]
, and then in all dimensions in
[
20
]
.
Finally, the Regularity problem in
L^{p}
for some (possibly small) value of
p
was established in the large Carleson norm regime recently and independently in
[
14
]
and
[
49
]
, by very different methods. We note that the approach of
[
49
]
solves this Regularity problem in a larger class of domains, beyond Lipschitz. However, the Neumann problem is still open in the large Carleson norm regime, except for dimension two (
[
14
]
). A survey of current state of the art for elliptic PDE can be found in
[
17
]
. Even in the elliptic setting, there are questions that have yet to be answered; for example, solvability of Regularity in the presence of drift terms.

Moving to the parabolic setting, the first results for solvability of the Dirichlet problem for the small Carleson norm regime were obtained in
[
15
]
, for

p\geq 2
. This was later improved to
p>1
in
[
13
]
. The solvability of the Dirichlet problem for the large Carleson norm regime was
established in
[
19
]
. A broader history of solvability of parabolic boundary value problems with coefficients satisfying various mild regularity conditions can be
found in, for example, the introduction of
[
11
]
.

Similar to the elliptic setting, the
L^{p}
Regularity problem in the parabolic setting for
p=2
can be thought of as being
1/2
derivative above the energy class of solutions, where the homogeneity of the parabolic setting entails that solutions restricted to boundary should have one full spatial gradient in
L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
. The time variable
of a parabolic PDE enjoys a different scaling from that of the spatial variables since the equation tells us that, roughly speaking,
\partial_{t}u\sim\nabla^{2}u
. That is, one spatial gradient of
u
corresponds to
1/2
derivative in time.
Hence the class of energy solutions (c.f.
[
3
]
) for parabolic PDEs is the space with
finite energy
:

\iint_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}+\iint_{\Omega}|D_{t}^{1/2}u|^{2}<\infty.

Finite energy solutions will have traces on the boundary with
1/2
-spatial and
1/4
-time derivatives. All of these considerations will
be explained with care in
subsection
2.2
.

Therefore, in order to define the parabolic
L^{p}
-based Regularity problem, one looks for solutions of the parabolic equation with prescribed data having one full spatial derivative and a half-time derivative on
\partial\Omega
.
The initial formulation of the Regularity problem for the heat equation is from
[
26
]
, where it was formulated for
C^{1}
cylinders. This was then reformulated and solved in
bounded Lipschitz cylinders in
[
5
,
6
]
. Constant coefficient systems were studied in
[
48
]
.
The parabolic Regularity problem with time-independent variable coefficients has been formulated and solved in bounded Lipschitz cylinders in
[
45
]
and

[
7
]
, but only for data in
L^{p}
with
p
close to 2.

1.1.
Main Results and Methodology

There are several optimal results in this paper for parabolic equations whose
coefficients satisfy a Carleson measure condition. In
the large Carleson norm regime, we show that the Regularity problem for an operator is solvable in the range dual to solvability of the Dirichlet problem for the
adjoint operator. This is the main goal of the paper, and will allow us to show solvability of Regularity in
L^{p}
for some interval
(1,p_{0})
.
Moreover, when the Carleson norm is assumed sufficiently small, this result will also give the first and best possible results for solvability of parabolic Regularity in this setting.

The approach taken in this paper essentially follows the elliptic roadmap of
[
14
]
; however, nearly every single step of that roadmap was missing from the
body of known results in the parabolic setting. Some of these steps represent significant advances in their own right, with
multiple further applications, and have appeared only recently as separate papers. Specifically, this program requires: (1) the parabolic perturbation results of
[
53
]
, (2) the parabolic Dirichlet-Regularity duality results of
[
22
]
, and (3) the
nontangential estimates for the half derivative in time in
[
11
]
.

Here, we establish
two key steps to obtain sharp solvability of the parabolic Regularity problem: first, reducing the general problem to a subclass of operators with a special block-form structure on the upper half-space; and second, leveraging the unique structure of such block-form operators—particularly the presence of a special transversal direction in which the operator exhibits well-behaved properties—to establish the solvability of the
L^{2}
Regularity problem for these block-form operators.

We encountered significant new challenges in both steps of the proof. For the reduction to the block-form case, the parabolic strategy required
an interesting technical refinement on known methodology of estimating the
L^{p}
norm of the nontangential maximal function by duality.
To solve the Regularity problem for the block-form parabolic operator, we had to overcome major additional challenges that arose
in handling the new appearance of a non-local operator, the half-derivative in time
D_{t}^{1/2}u
. We introduce and study the PDE satisfied by
D_{t}^{1/2}u
, which involves a non-local term. The results of this study constitute a novel contribution of this paper.
It turns out that the complexity of the mechanisms and the intricacy of the computations required in both of these steps necessitate the length of this paper.

We briefly address our assumption on the domain of the form
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
, i.e., the domain does not vary in time. This means that we do not include (unlike the elliptic case
[
14
]
),
the class of operators arising from the pull-backs of the heat equation from time varying domains. The reason for this omission is the current lack of theory of solvability of the Regularity problem for operators that include a singular drift (as in the work of Hofmann-Lewis
[
36
]
). This theory does not exist even in the elliptic case (as noted in
[
20
]
) and it currently prevents making any inroads in the time-varying case. We expect that when/if any advance is made on the solvability of the elliptic Regularity problem
with singular drift terms, a similar advance will be possible on the parabolic Regularity problem on time varying domains satisfying the usual assumptions as in
[
36
]
. This distinguishes the Regularity problem from the Dirichlet one, where the presence of drift terms can be usually handled.

One important aspect of the methodology here is the existence of a preferred direction - an essential feature of the upper half
space, or the domain above a Lipschitz graph.
This enables the use of a variant of the measure (
1.3
) in directions parallel to the boundary, namely,

(1.5)

d\mu_{||}(X,t)=\left(\delta(X,t)|\nabla_{x}A|^{2}+\delta(X,t)^{3}|\partial_{t}A|^{2}\right)dXdt.

The only difference between
\mu
and
\mu_{||}
is that
\mu_{||}
does not contain
\partial_{x_{n}}A
, which is important because the Carleson norm of the measure
\mu_{||}
can be
made small, unlike that of
\mu
.

We state our main results below.

Theorem 1.6
.

Consider the parabolic PDE
\mathcal{L}u=-\partial_{t}u+\divg(A\nabla u)=0
on the domain
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bounded or unbounded Lipschitz domain, the matrix
A
is uniformly elliptic (
1.2
) with bounded and measurable coefficients. Assume that in addition one of the the following two conditions holds:

(1)

matrix A satisfies (
1.3
) and (
1.4
), or

(2)

the measure

d\nu=\delta(X,t)^{-1}\left(\sup_{B((X,t),\delta(X,t)/2)}\mbox{\rm osc }A\right)^{2}\,dX\,dt

satisfies the Carleson measure condition.

Then there exists
p_{0}>1
such that for all
1<p<p_{0}
the
L^{p}
Regularity problem for the equation
\mathcal{L}u=0
in
\Omega
is solvable (c.f. Definition
2.26
). Moreover, this interval of solvability
(1,p_{0})
is dual to the interval of solvability of the
L^{q}
Dirichlet problem (c.f. Definition
2.31
) for the adjoint equation

(1.7)

\mathcal{L}^{*}u:=\partial_{t}u+\divg(A^{T}\nabla u)=0

in
\Omega
which is solvable for all
q>p_{0}^{\prime}
.

A special case of Theorem
1.6
is the following result for block-form operators.

Theorem 1.8
.

Assume that
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
and the matrix
A
is of the block-form, that is
A=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}A_{\parallel}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&1\\
\end{array}\right]
, for some
(n-1)\times(n-1)
matrix
A_{\parallel}
and that the matrix
A
is uniformly elliptic with bounded measurable coefficients.
Then the
L^{p}
Dirichlet problem
\mathcal{L}u=0
for the operator
\mathcal{L}=-\partial_{t}+\divg A\nabla
is solvable for all
1<p<\infty
.

Furthermore, assume that the matrix
A_{\parallel}

satisfies the condition (1) or (2) as in Theorem
1.6
. Then the
L^{p}
Regularity problem
\mathcal{L}u=0

is solvable for all
1<p<\infty
.

The elliptic analog of the first part statement in Theorem
1.8
concerning solvability of the Dirichlet problem is well known (and was
observed by S. Mayboroda). However we are not aware that such a result is known for the parabolic case. Because we need this result as a component
in solving the Regularity problems addressed in this paper, the proof of this fact is given in Appendix
A
. The second part of Theorem
1.8
, the solvability of the Regularity problem,
is the aforementioned key step in the proof of Theorem
1.6
.

The proof of Theorems
1.6
and
1.8
is presented in Section
4
, building on results established throughout the rest of the paper.

Acknowledgment:
We would like to thank Joseph Feneuil for some helpful discussions, especially for explaining their argument regarding the ‘
N<S
’ estimate in
[
10
]
.

2.
Formulation of the Regularity problem

2.1.
Parabolic Sobolev Spaces on
\partial\Omega

When considering the appropriate function space for our boundary data we want it to have the same homogeneity as the PDE.
As a rule of thumb, one derivative in time behaves like two derivatives in space and so the correct order of our time derivative should be
1/2
if we impose data with one derivative in spatial variables.
This has been studied previously in
[
34
,
35
,
48
]
, who have followed
[
23
]
in defining the homogeneous parabolic Sobolev space
\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}
in the following way.

Definition 2.1
.

The homogeneous parabolic Sobolev space
\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, for
1<p<\infty
, is defined to consist of equivalence classes of functions
f
with distributional derivatives satisfying
\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty
, where

(2.2)

\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|\mathbb{D}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

and

(2.3)

(\mathbb{D}f)\,\widehat{\,}\,(\xi,\tau):=\|(\xi,\tau)\|\widehat{f}(\xi,\tau).

Here
\|(\xi,\tau)\|
on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
is defined as the unique positive solution
\rho
to the following equation

(2.4)

\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{\rho^{2}}+\frac{\tau^{2}}{\rho^{4}}=1.

One can easily show that
\|(\xi,\tau)\|\sim(|\xi|^{2}+|\tau|)^{1/2}
and that this norm scales correctly according to the parabolic nature of the PDE.

Remark.

In the definition above we consider
(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
. This is due to the fact that the boundary
\partial\Omega
is a lower dimensional set and our aim is to define the parabolic Sobolev space on
\partial\Omega
.

In addition, following
[
25
]
, we define a parabolic half-order time derivative by

(2.5)

(\mathbb{D}_{n}f)\,\widehat{\,}\,(\xi,\tau):=\frac{\tau}{\|(\xi,\tau)\|}\widehat{f}(\xi,\tau).

If
0<\alpha\leq 2
, then for
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
the
one-dimensional fractional differentiation operators

D_{\alpha}
are defined by

(2.6)

(D^{\alpha}g)\,\widehat{\,}\,(\tau):=|\tau|^{\alpha}\widehat{g}(\tau).

It is also well known that if
0<\alpha<1
then

(2.7)

D^{\alpha}g(s)=c\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{g(s)-g(\tau)}{|s-\tau|^{1+\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau

whenever
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
h(x,t)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
then by
D^{\alpha}_{t}h:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we mean the function
D^{\alpha}h(x,\cdot)
defined a.e. for each fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.
We now recall the established connections between
\mathbb{D}
,
\mathbb{D}_{n}
and
D_{t}^{1/2}
. From
[
24
,
25
,
12
]
we have that

(2.8)

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{D}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\sim\|\mathbb{D}_{n}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|\nabla f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\sim\|D_{t}^{1/2}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|\nabla f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

for all
1<p<\infty
. Here
\nabla
denotes the usual gradient in the variables
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.

Recall the definition of Lipschitz domain.

Definition 2.9
.

{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an
\ell
-cylinder of diameter
d
if there
exists an orthogonal coordinate system
(x^{\prime},x_{n})
with
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

{\mathbb{Z}}=\{(x^{\prime},x_{n})\;:\;|x^{\prime}|\leq d,\;-(\ell+1)d\leq x_{n}\leq(\ell+1)d\}

and for
s>0
,

s{\mathbb{Z}}:=\{(x^{\prime},x_{n})\;:\;|x^{\prime}|\leq sd,-(\ell+1)sd\leq x_{n}\leq(\ell+1)sd\}.

Definition 2.10
.

\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a Lipschitz domain with Lipschitz
‘character’
(\ell,N,C_{0})
if there exists a positive scale
r_{0}\in(0,\infty]
and
at most
N

\ell
-cylinders
\{{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
of diameter
d
, with

\frac{r_{0}}{C_{0}}\leq d\leq C_{0}r_{0}
such that

(i)
8{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{j}\cap{\partial\mathcal{O}}
is the graph of a Lipschitz
function
\phi_{j}
,
\|\nabla\phi_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq\ell\,;\phi_{j}(0)=0
,

(ii)
\displaystyle{\partial\mathcal{O}}=\bigcup_{j}({{\mathbb{Z}}}_{j}\cap{\partial\mathcal{O}})
,

(iii)
\displaystyle{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{j}\cap\mathcal{O}\supset\left\{(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in\mathcal{O}\;:\;|x^{\prime}|<d,\;\mathrm{dist}\left((x^{\prime},x_{n}),{\partial\mathcal{O}}\right)\leq\frac{d}{2}\right\}
.

(iv) Each cylinder
\displaystyle{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{j}
contains points from
\mathcal{O}^{c}={\mathbb{R}^{n}}\setminus\mathcal{O}
.

(v) If
r_{0}<\infty
the domain
\mathcal{O}
is a bounded set.

Remark.
If the scale
r_{0}
is finite, then the domain
\mathcal{O}
from the definition above is a bounded Lipschitz domain. However, we shall also allow both
r_{0},\,d
to be infinite, and in this case, since
\displaystyle{{\mathbb{Z}}}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
we have that
\mathcal{O}
can be written in some coordinate system as

\mathcal{O}=\{(x^{\prime},x_{n}):x_{n}>\phi(x^{\prime})\}\quad\mbox{ where $\phi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}$ is a Lipschitz function.}

The set
\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
will be called a parabolic Lipschitz cylinder with Lipschitz base
\mathcal{O}
.
We will need an extension of the definition of spaces
\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}
to Lipschitz cylinders.

Definition 2.11
.

The homogeneous parabolic Sobolev space.
Let
1<p<\infty
and
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
where
\mathcal{O}
is a Lipschitz domain as in Definition
2.10
. The space
\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)
consists of an equivalence class of functions
f
with distributional derivatives satisfying
\|\nabla_{T}f\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+\|D_{t}^{1/2}f\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty
, where

\nabla_{T}f
denotes the tangential spatial gradient of
f
(defined for a.e. point of
(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}
) by
\nabla f(q,\tau)\Big|_{\partial\Omega}-\nu(\nabla f(q,\tau)\cdot\nu(q))
, where
\nu
is the outer normal vector to
\partial\mathcal{O}
.
The
\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)
norm of
f
is defined as

\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}=\|\nabla_{T}f\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+\|D_{t}^{1/2}f\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}.

In light of the equivalences of norms in (
2.8
) for
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
, this coincides with our previous definition for
\partial\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
.

2.2.
Reinforced weak and Energy solutions

We recall the paper
[
3
]
that neatly presents the concept of reinforced weak solutions for the parabolic problem of interest here. In
[
11
]
, it was shown that the definition given in
[
3
]
can be weakened a bit further by only asking for the “local
1/2
derivative” in the time variable. We explain below.

If
\mathcal{O}
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we let
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{W}^{1,2}(\mathcal{O})
be the standard Sobolev space of real valued functions
v
defined on
\mathcal{O}
, such that
v
and
\nabla v
are in
L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathbb{R})
and
L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, respectively. A subscripted ‘
\loc
’ will indicate that these conditions hold locally.

We shall say that
u
is a
reinforced weak solution
of
-\partial_{t}u+\divg(A\nabla u)=0
on
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
if

\displaystyle u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{\loc}(\Omega):=\mathcal{H}_{\loc}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R};L^{2}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O}))\cap L_{\operatorname{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{W}^{1,2}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O}))

and if for all
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)
,

(2.12)

\iint_{\Omega}\left[A\nabla u\cdot{\nabla(\phi\psi)}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}(u\psi)\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}\phi}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}(u\phi)\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}\psi}\right]\,\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

Here,
D_{t}^{1/2}
is the half-order derivative and
H_{t}
is the Hilbert transform with respect to the
t
variable, normalized so that
\partial_{t}=D_{t}^{1/2}H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}
. The space
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
is the homogeneous Sobolev space of order 1/2 - the completion of
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
in the norm
\|D_{t}^{1/2}(\cdot)\|_{2}
- and it embeds into the space
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{C}
of tempered distributions, modulo constants).
The local space
\mathcal{H}_{\loc}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
consists of functions
u
such that
u\phi\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
for all
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)
. As shown in
[
11
]
the space
\mathcal{H}_{\loc}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
is larger than
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
.
For
u\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R};L^{2}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O})),
(
2.12
) simplifies to

\iint_{\Omega}\left[A\nabla u\cdot{\nabla\phi}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}u\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}\phi}\right]\,\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

Our definition has the advantage that, in taking a cut-off of the function
u
, we might potentially improve the decay of
D^{1/2}_{t}u
at infinity. In particular, this matters when considering
u\big|_{\partial\Omega}\in L^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)
for
p>2
as such
u
might not decay fast enough to belong to

\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R};L^{2}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O}))
.

At this point we remark that for any
u\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
the formula

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}(u\psi)\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}\phi}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}(u\phi)\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}\psi}\right]\,\mathrm{d}t=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}u\cdot{\partial_{t}(\phi\psi)}\,\mathrm{d}t

holds, where on the right-hand side we use the duality form extension of the complex inner product of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, between
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
and its dual
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{-1/2}(\mathbb{R})
. By taking
\psi=1
on the set where
\phi
is supported, it follows that a reinforced weak solution is a weak solution in the usual sense on
\Omega
since it satisfies

u\in L_{\operatorname{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{W}^{1,2}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O}))
and for all
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle\iint_{\Omega}A\nabla u\cdot{\nabla\phi}\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t-\iint_{\Omega}u\cdot{\partial_{t}\phi}\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

This implies
\partial_{t}u\in L_{\operatorname{loc}}^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{W}^{-1,2}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O}))
. Conversely, any weak solution
u
in
\mathcal{H}_{\loc}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R};L^{2}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O}))
is a reinforced weak solution.

Specializing to the case
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathcal{O}
is either bounded or unbounded Lipschitz domain,
we say that a
reinforced weak solution
v\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{\loc}(\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})
belongs to the
energy class

\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})
if

\displaystyle\|v\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}}:=\bigg(\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\|H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}<\infty.

Consequently, these are called
energy solutions
. When considered modulo constants,
\dot{\mathsf{E}}
is a Hilbert space and it is in fact the closure of
\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}\!\big(\,\overline{\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}\,\big)
for the homogeneous norm
\|\cdot\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}}
. As shown in
[
3
]
(with a small generalization), functions from
\dot{\mathsf{E}}
have well defined traces with values in
the
homogeneous parabolic Sobolev space

\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})
. Here,
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{s}_{\pm\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined as the closure of Schwartz functions
v\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
with Fourier support away from the origin in the norm
\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}((|\xi|^{2}\pm i\tau)^{s}\mathcal{F}v)\|_{2}
. This yields a space of tempered distributions modulo constants in
L_{\operatorname{loc}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})
if
0<s\leq 1/2
.
Conversely, any
g\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}
can be extended to a function
v\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}
with trace
v\big|_{\partial\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{+}}=g
. For this via partition of unity we can define

\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})
for
\mathcal{O}
Lipschitz.

Hence, by the energy solution to
-\partial_{t}u+\divg(A\nabla u)=0
with Dirichlet boundary datum
u\big|_{\partial\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}=f\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}
(understood in the trace sense) we mean
u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}
such that

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}\left[A\nabla u\cdot{\nabla v}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}u\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}v}\right]\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t=0,

holds for all
v\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{0}
, the subspace of
\dot{\mathsf{E}}
with zero boundary trace.

By
[
3
]
the key to solving these problems is the introduction of the modified sesquilinear form

(2.13)

a_{\delta}(u,v):=\iint_{\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}\left[A\nabla u\cdot{\nabla(1-\delta H_{t})v}+H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}u\cdot{D_{t}^{1/2}(1-\delta H_{t})v}\right]\,\mathrm{d}X\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
\delta
is a real number yet to be chosen. The Hilbert transform
H_{t}
is a skew-symmetric isometric operator with inverse
-H_{t}
on both
\dot{\mathsf{E}}
and
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}
. Hence,
1-\delta H_{t}
is invertible on these spaces for any
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence for a fixed
\delta>0
small enough,
a_{\delta}
is coercive on
\dot{\mathsf{E}}
since

(2.14)

a_{\delta}(u,u)\geq(\lambda-\Lambda\delta)\|\nabla u\|_{2}^{2}+\delta\|H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}u\|_{2}^{2}.

In particular
\delta=\lambda/(\Lambda+1)
would work.
To solve the Dirichlet problem we take an extension
w\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}
of the data
f
and apply the Lax-Milgram lemma to
a_{\delta}
on
\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{0}
to obtain some
u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{0}
such that

\displaystyle a_{\delta}(u,v)=-a_{\delta}(w,v)\qquad(v\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{0}).

Hence,
u+w
is an energy solution with data
f
. Should there exist another solution
v
, then
a_{\delta}(u+w-v,u+w-v)=0
and hence by coercivity
\|u+w-v\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}}=0
. Thus the two solutions only differ by a constant. It means that Dirichlet problem associated with our parabolic PDE is
well-posed
in the energy class.

2.3.
(R)_{p}
boundary value problem

Let
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a Lipschitz domain. Assume that
A:\Omega\to M_{n\times n}(\mathbb{R})
is a bounded uniformly elliptic matrix-valued function. Let
\mathcal{L}=-\partial_{t}+\divg A\nabla
.

To give the definition of the parabolic Regularity problem, we introduce notation for parabolic balls and cubes, and then define nontangential parabolic cones and parabolic nontangential maximal functions.

Definition 2.15
.

A parabolic cube on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
centered at
(X,t)
with sidelength
r
as

Q_{r}(X,t):=\{(Y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}:|x_{i}-y_{i}|<r\ \text{ for }1\leq i\leq n,\ |t-s|^{1/2}<r\}.

When writing a lower case point
(x,t)
we shall mean a boundary parabolic cube on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}

which has an analogous definition but in one less spatial dimension:

(2.16)

Q_{r}(x,t):=\{(y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}:|x_{i}-y_{i}|<r\ \text{ for }1\leq i\leq n-1,\ |t-s|^{1/2}<r\}.

A parabolic ball on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
centered at
(X,t)
with radius
r
is the ball

(2.17)

B_{r}(X,t):=\{(Y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}:\|(X-Y,t-s)\|<r\},

where
\|\cdot\|
has been defined earlier by (
2.4
). Recall that
\|\cdot\|
scales as the parabolic distance function

d_{p}((X,t),(Y,s)):=\left(\left|X-Y\right|^{2}+\left|t-s\right|\right)^{1/2}\sim\|(X-Y,t-s)\|.

For parabolic balls at the boundary we use notation
\Delta_{r}(X,t)=B_{r}(X,t)\cap\partial\Omega
. In the special case
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
we drop the last coordinate and also write
\Delta_{r}(x,t)
with understanding that the ball is centered at
(X,t)=(x,0,t)
.

Definition 2.18
.

For
a>0
and
(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega
, unless otherwise defined, we denote the nontangential parabolic cones by

(2.19)

\Gamma_{a}(q,\tau):=\left\{(X,t)\in\Omega:d_{p}((X,t),(q,\tau))<(1+a)\delta(X,t)\right\},

and
\delta(\cdot)
is the parabolic distance to the boundary:

\delta(X,t)=\inf_{(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega}d_{p}((X,t),(q,\tau)).

We also use the truncated parabolic cones: for
r>0
,

\Gamma_{a}^{r}(q,\tau):=\left\{(X,t)\in\Omega:d((X,t),(q,\tau))<(1+a)\delta(X,t),\,\delta(X,t)<r\right\}

is the parabolic cone with vertex
(q,\tau)
truncated at height
r
.

Definition 2.20
.

For
w\in L^{\infty}_{\loc}(\Omega)
, we define the nontangential maximal function of
w
as

N_{a}(w)(q,\tau):=\sup_{(X,t)\in\Gamma_{a}(q,\tau)}\left|w(X,t)\right|\quad\text{for }(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega.

If
w\in L^{p}_{\loc}(\Omega)
,
p\in(0,\infty)
, we need the modified nontangential maximal function

(2.21)

\widetilde{N}_{a,p}(w)(q,\tau):=\sup_{(X,t)\in\Gamma_{a}(q,\tau)}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{\delta(X,t)/2}(X,t)}\left|w(Y,s)\right|^{p}dYds\right)^{1/p}\quad\text{for }(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega,

where
B_{r}(X,t)
is a parabolic ball centered at
(X,t)
with “radius”
r
, that is,

B_{r}(X,t):=\left\{(Y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}:d((Y,s),(X,t))<r\right\}.

We simply denote

(2.22)

\widetilde{N}_{a}(w):=\widetilde{N}_{a,2}(w)

when
p=2
in (
2.21
).

We now introduce the parabolic square and area functions.

Definition 2.23
.

For a function
w
with
\nabla w\in L^{2}_{\loc}(\Omega)
, define the square function of
w
as

(2.24)

S_{a}(w)(q,\tau):=\left(\iint_{\Gamma_{a}(q,\tau)}\left|\nabla w(X,t)\right|^{2}\delta(X,t)^{-n}dXdt\right)^{1/2}\quad\text{for }(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega.

We also need the following two versions of the area function defined as in
[
15
]
for a function
w:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

(2.25)

\displaystyle A_{a}(w)(q,\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\iint_{\Gamma_{a}(q,\tau)}|\partial_{t}w(X,t)|^{2}\delta(X,t)^{-n+2}\,dX\,dt\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{a}(w)(q,\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\iint_{\Gamma_{a}(q,\tau)}|\nabla^{2}w(X,t)|^{2}\delta(X,t)^{-n+2}\,dX\,dt\right)^{1/2}.

In virtue of the parabolic PDE heuristic that
\partial_{t}w\sim\nabla^{2}w
, the operators
A_{a}
and
\tilde{A}_{a}
are closely related, but for different purposes there is an advantage of using one over the other.

It is well-known (using level-sets argument) that for
p\in(0,\infty)
, the
L^{p}
norms of
N_{a}(w)
,
\widetilde{N}_{a}(w)
,
S_{a}(w)
,
A_{a}(w)
and
\tilde{A}_{a}(w)
are invariant under changes of
a
up to a constant multiple. For this reason, we omit the dependence on the aperture
a
of the cones when there is no need for the specificity.

We are now ready to formulate our definition of the parabolic Regularity problem
(R)_{p}
.

Definition 2.26
.

Let
1<p<\infty
.
The Regularity problem for the parabolic operator
\mathcal{L}
with boundary data in

\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)
is solvable (abbreviated
(R)_{p}
), if
for every
f\in\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)\cap\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)

the
energy solution

u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
(as defined above) to the problem

(2.27)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}u&=0\quad\text{ in }\Omega\\
u|_{\partial\Omega}&=f\quad\text{ on }\partial\Omega\end{cases}

satisfies

(2.28)

\displaystyle\quad\|\widetilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|\nabla_{T}f\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\approx\|f\|_{\dot{L}_{1,1/2}^{p}(\partial\Omega)}.

The implied constant depends only the matrix
A
,
p
and
n
.

When we need to specify the operator, we will use the abbreviation
(R)_{p}^{\mathcal{L}}
.

Observe that this definition does not require control of
\|\widetilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{p}}
or of
\|\widetilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}H_{t}u)\|_{L^{p}}
in
(
2.28
) because these bounds will follow from (
2.28
) and do not need to be established separately.

Theorem 2.29
.

(c.f.
[
11
]
) Let
\Omega
be an infinite Lipschitz cylinder of the form
\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{L}
be as above and assume that for some
1<p<\infty
the Regularity problem
(R)_{p}
for
\mathcal{L}
on the domain
\Omega
is solvable. Then in addition to (
2.28
) we also have the bounds

(2.30)

\displaystyle\quad\|\widetilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+\|\widetilde{N}(D^{1/2}_{t}H_{t}u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{L}_{1,1/2}^{p}(\partial\Omega)}.

Thus by Theorem
2.29
our definition coincides with the previous notions of solvability of the Regularity problem as in
[
5
,
6
,
48
,
45
,
7
]
which did include one or both of the terms on the left-hand side of (
2.30
). This theorem significantly simplifies our task as we need
only to focus on bounds for the spatial gradient of a solution
u
.

For completeness we also state the definition of the
L^{p}
Dirichlet problem
(D)_{p}
:

Definition 2.31
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
. We say that the Dirichlet problem for the operator
\mathcal{L}
with boundary data in

{L}^{p}(\partial\Omega)
is solvable (abbreviated
(D)_{p}
), if
for every
f\in{L}^{p}(\partial\Omega)\cap\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)

the
energy solution

u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
(as defined above) to the problem (
2.27
) satisfies the estimate

(2.32)

\displaystyle\quad\|\widetilde{N}(u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}.

The implied constant again depends only the matrix
A
,
p
and
n
.

We will also denote it by
(D)_{p}^{\mathcal{L}}
when we need to specify the operator.

3.
Preliminaries

3.1.
Overview of basic parabolic results

We recall some foundational estimates that will be used.

Lemma 3.1

(Caccioppoli’s inequality, see
[
1
]
)
.

Let
A
satisfy (
1.2
) and suppose that
u
is a weak solution of (
1.1
) in
Q_{4r}(X,t)\subset\Omega
.
Then there exists a constant
C=C(\lambda,\Lambda,n)
such that

r^{n}\left(\sup_{Q_{r/2}(X,t)}u\right)^{2}\leq C\sup_{t-r^{2}\leq s\leq t+r^{2}}\int_{Q_{r}(X,t)\cap\{t=s\}}u^{2}(Y,s)dY\\
+C\iint_{Q_{r}(X,t)}|\nabla u|^{2}dYds\leq\frac{C^{2}}{r^{2}}\iint_{Q_{2r}(X,t)}u^{2}(Y,s)dYds.

Lemma 3.2

(Reverse Hölder inequality for gradient)
.

Let
u
and
Q_{4r}(X,t)\subset\Omega
be as in Lemma
3.1
. Then there exists a constant
C=C(\lambda,\Lambda,n)
such that

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{Q_{r}(X,t)}\left|\nabla u(Y,s)\right|^{2}dYds\right)^{1/2}\leq C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{Q_{2r}}\left|\nabla u(Y,s)\right|dYds.

The theorem as stated is proven in
[
22
, Lemma 3.6]
and is a consequence of higher
p>2
regularity of gradients together with a real variable argument of Shen
[
52
]
.

Lemmas 3.4 and 3.5 in
[
36
]
give the following estimates for weak solutions of (
1.1
).

Lemma 3.3

(Interior Hölder continuity)
.

Let
A
satisfy (
1.2
) and suppose that
u
is a weak solution of (
1.1
) in
Q_{4r}(X,t)\subset\Omega
with
0<r<\delta(X,t)/8
.
Then for any
(Y,s),(Z,\tau)\in Q_{2r}(X,t)

\left|u(Y,s)-u(Z,\tau)\right|\leq C\left(\frac{|Y-Z|+|s-\tau|^{1/2}}{r}\right)^{\alpha}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{Q_{4r}(X,t)}|u|,

where
C=C(\lambda,\Lambda,n)
,
\alpha=\alpha(\lambda,\Lambda,n)
, and
0<\alpha<1
.

Lemma 3.4

(Maximum Principle, see
[
47
, Theorem 1.4]
,
[
36
, Lemma 3.38]
)
.

Let
A
satisfy (
1.2
), and let
u
,
v
be bounded continuous weak solutions to (
1.1
) in
\Omega
.
If
|u|,|v|\to 0
uniformly as
t\to-\infty
and

\limsup_{(Y,s)\to(X,t)}(u-v)(Y,s)\leq 0

for all
(X,t)\in\partial\Omega
, then
u\leq v
in
\Omega
.

If
\Omega^{\prime}
is a bounded subdomain of
\Omega
, and if
u-v\leq 0
on
\partial_{p}\Omega^{\prime}
, then
u\leq v
in
\Omega^{\prime}
.

Lemma 3.5

(Harnack inequality)
.

Let
A
satisfy (
1.2
) and suppose that
u
is a weak non-negative solution of (
1.1
) in
Q_{4r}(X,t)\subset\Omega
, with
0<r<\delta(X,t)/8
.
Suppose that
(Y,s),(Z,\tau)\in Q_{2r}(X,t)
then there exists
C=C(\lambda,\Lambda,n)
such that, for
\tau<s
,

u(Z,\tau)\leq u(Y,s)\exp\left[C\left(\frac{|Y-Z|^{2}}{|s-\tau|}+1\right)\right].

If
u\geq 0
is a weak solution to (
1.7
), then the inequality is valid when
\tau>s
.

The continuous Dirichlet problem for (
1.1
) is solvable for any compactly supported, continuous function
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)
(
[
36
]
)
and that solution is given by

(3.6)

u(X,t)=\int_{\partial\Omega}f(y,s)d\omega(X,t,y,s),

where
d\omega(X,t,y,s)
denotes the boundary parabolic measure.

Under the assumption (
1.2
), it is known (see e.g.
[
36
]
) that there exists a Green’s function
G
for (
1.1
) in
\Omega
with corresponding
parabolic, adjoint parabolic measures
\omega(X,t,\cdot)
,
\omega^{T}(X,t,\cdot)
, satisfying for
each
(X,t)\in\Omega
the condition:

(3.7)

\phi(X,t)=\iint_{\Omega}\left(A\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla_{Y}G(X,t,Y,s)+G(X,t,Y,s)\partial_{s}\phi\right)dYds+\int_{\partial\Omega}\phi(y,s)d\omega(X,t,y,s),

\phi(X,t)=\iint_{\Omega}\left(A^{T}\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla_{Y}G(Y,s,X,t)-G(Y,s,X,t)\partial_{s}\phi\right)dYds+\int_{\partial\Omega}\phi(y,s)d\omega^{T}(X,t,y,s),

for any
\phi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
.
The Green’s function for (
1.1
) has the following properties.

(1)

G(X,t,Y,s)=0
for
s>t
,
(X,t)
,
(Y,s)\in\Omega
.

(2)

For fixed
(Y,s)\in\Omega
,
G(\cdot,Y,s)
is a solution to (
1.1
) in
\Omega\setminus\{(Y,s)\}
.

(3)

For fixed
(X,t)\in\Omega
,
G(X,t,\cdot)
is a solution to (
1.7
),
the adjoint equation in
\Omega\setminus\{(X,t)\}
.

(4)

If
(X,t)
,
(Y,s)\in\Omega
then
G(X,t,\cdot)
and
G(\cdot,Y,s)
extend continuously to
\overline{\Omega}
provided both functions are defined to be zero on
\partial\Omega
.

It was proven in
[
1
]
that Green’s function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
satisfies the bounds

(3.8)

G(X,t,Y,s)\leq C(t-s)^{-n/2}\exp\left(-\frac{\left|X-Y\right|^{2}}{C(t-s)}\right)\quad\text{ for all }t>s,

for some
C=C(\lambda,\Lambda,n)\geq 1
.

Lemma 3.9

(Boundary Caccioppoli’s inequality)
.

Let
A
satisfy (
1.2
) and suppose that
u
is a weak solution of (
1.1
) or (
1.7
) in
(0,2r)\times Q_{2r}
, where
Q_{2r}=Q_{2r}(y,s)
is a parabolic cube on
\partial\Omega
defined as in (
2.16
). If
u
vanishes continuously on
Q_{2r}
, then there exist constant
C=C(\lambda,\Lambda,n)
such that

\int_{x_{n}=0}^{r}\int_{Q_{r}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}dtdX\leq Cr^{-2}\int_{x_{n}=0}^{2r}\int_{Q_{2r}}u^{2}dtdX.

The proof is standard; see for example
[
47
]
Remark 2.3.

A consequence of Caccioppoli’s inequality on the boundary is the following:

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
u
,
Q_{2r}
be as in Lemma
3.9
.
Then

(3.11)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{r}\int_{Q_{r}}|\nabla u|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq Cr^{-\frac{n+2}{2}}\int_{0}^{2r}\int_{Q_{2r}}|\nabla u|.

Lemma 3.12

(Boundary Hölder continuity, see
[
36
]
Lemma 3.9)
.

Let
A
,
u
,
Q_{2r}
be as in Lemma
3.9
. If
u
vanishes continuously on
Q_{2r}
, then there exist constants
C=C(\lambda,\Lambda,n)
and
\alpha=\alpha(\lambda,\Lambda,n)\in(0,1)
, such that for any
(X,t)\in(0,r/2)\times Q_{r/2}
,

u(X,t)\leq C\left(\frac{\delta(X,t)}{r}\right)^{\alpha}\sup_{(0,r)\times Q_{r}}u.

If in addition,
u\geq 0
in
(0,2r)\times Q_{2r}
, then there exist constants
C=C(\lambda,\Lambda,n)
and
\alpha=\alpha(\lambda,\Lambda,n)\in(0,1)
, such that for any
(X,t)\in(0,r/2)\times Q_{r/2}
,

(3.13)

u(X,t)\leq C\left(\frac{\delta(X,t)}{r}\right)^{\alpha}u(A_{r}^{\pm}(y,s)),

where the plus sign is taken when
u
is a weak solution to (
1.1
) and the minus sign
for
u
satisfying (
1.7
).

We now introduce the definition of Carleson measures.

Definition 3.14
.

A measure
\mu
is a Carleson measure on
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
if there exists a constant
M
such that for
(X,t)\in\partial\Omega
and

0<r<\diam(\Omega)
,

(3.15)

\mu(B_{r}(X,t)\cap\Omega)\leq Mr^{n+1}.

The infimum over all
M
such that (
3.15
) holds is the Carleson norm,
\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}
, of
\mu
.

Lemma 3.16
.

[
53
, Theorem 1.1]

Let
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Lipschitz domain, and let
\mathcal{L}_{0}=\partial_{t}-\divg(A_{0}\nabla)
and
\mathcal{L}_{1}=\partial_{t}-\divg(A_{1}\nabla)
be two parabolic operators such that
A_{i}
satisfies (
1.2
) on
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,2
. Set

d\nu=\sup_{Q(X,t,\delta(X,t)/2)}|A_{0}-A_{1}|^{2}\frac{dXdt}{\delta}.

Assume that the Regularity problem
(R)_{q}
for
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is solvable for
q>1
, and that
\left\|\nu\right\|_{C}<\infty
. Then the Regularity problem
(R)_{p}
for
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is solvable for some
1<p<q
.

Remark 3.17
.

(1)

[
53
, Theorem 1.1]
also includes a stronger result in the setting of small Carleson constants.

(2)

The theorem still holds if one replaces
d\nu
with

(3.18)

d\nu^{\prime}:=|A_{0}(X,t)-A_{1}(X,t)|^{2}\frac{dXdt}{\delta(X,t)}

and assumes in addition that
A_{0}
satisfies
x_{n}\left|\nabla A_{0}(X,t)\right|\leq C
for
(X,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. This is justified in
[
53
]
; the reasoning is similar to that of the elliptic case (see
[
29
]
).

Lemma 3.19

(
[
12
]
)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{O}
is a bounded Lipschitz domain and
\mathcal{L}
is a
parabolic operator of the form (
1.1
) on the domain
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
1
1

1

[
12
]
treats
Lip(1,1/2)
domains, which is more general.
with
A
satisfying the ellipticity condition (
1.2
). Suppose
(R)^{\mathcal{L}}_{p}
is solvable for some
p\in(1,\infty)
, then
(D)^{\mathcal{L}^{*}}_{p^{\prime}}
is solvable for
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
.

Lemma 3.20

(
[
22
, Theorem 1.5]
)
.

Assume that
\mathcal{O}
is Lipschitz (either bounded or unbounded) and
\mathcal{L}
is a
parabolic operator of the form (
1.1
) on the domain
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
, with
A
satisfying the ellipticity condition (
1.2
).
Suppose that
(D)^{\mathcal{L}^{*}}_{p^{\prime}}
is solvable for some
p\in(1,\infty)
. Then either
(R)^{\mathcal{L}}_{p}
is solvable
or
(R)^{\mathcal{L}}_{q}
is not solvable for all
1<q<\infty
.

Lemma 3.21

(
[
22
, Theorem 1.4]
)
.

Let
\Omega
and
\mathcal{L}
be as in Lemma
3.20
. Suppose that
(R)_{p}^{\mathcal{L}}
is solvable for some
1<p<\infty
. Then
(R)_{q}^{\mathcal{L}}
is solvable for
1<q<p
.

In order to use the averaged version
\tilde{N}
of the nontangential maximal function instead of
{N}
in (
3.27
)-(
3.28
), we will use the following estimate for nontangential maximal functions of solutions
u
as well as gradients
\nabla u
of solutions.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
u
be a solution to
\partial_{t}-\mbox{\rm div}(A\nabla u)=0
in
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. Then for any
a>0
there exists
\tilde{a}>0
such that we have pointwise for all
(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega
:

N_{a}(u)(q,\tau)\leq C\tilde{N}_{\tilde{a}}(u)(q,\tau).

Additionally, if
\delta(X,t)|\nabla A(X,t)|\leq K
then also

(3.23)

\displaystyle N_{a}(\nabla u)(q,\tau)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(1+K)\tilde{N}_{\tilde{a}}(\nabla u)(q,\tau),

for some
C=C(n,\lambda,\Lambda)
.

Proof.

The first inequality is a consequence of the interior Hölder inequality (Lemma
3.3
): notice that the aperture of the cone
\tilde{N}_{\tilde{a}}
)
has been enlarged. The second inequality follows from the interior Hölder inequality for gradients
[
44
]
, which requires the extra assumption that
\delta(X,t)|\nabla A(X,t)|\leq K
.
∎

3.2.
Some real analysis results

We introduce some special notation for a class of Carleson measures whose density is given by a function.

Definition 3.24
.

For a function
f
defined on
\Omega
, we say that
f\in CM_{\Omega}(M)
when

|f|^{2}\delta^{-1}
is the density of a Carleson measure, that is, if there exists
M>0
such that for any
(X,t)\in\partial\Omega
and
0<r<\diam(\Omega)
,

(3.25)

\iint_{B_{r}(X,t)\cap\Omega}|f(Y,\tau)|^{2}\delta(Y,\tau)^{-1}dYd\tau\leq Mr^{n+1}.

We now recall some well known properties of these measures.

Lemma 3.26
.

Let
\mathfrak{c}\in CM(M)
and let
f
and
g
be functions on
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. Then

(3.27)

\iint_{\Omega}f(X,t)\mathfrak{c}^{2}(X,t)\frac{dXdt}{\delta(X,t)}\leq C_{n}M\int_{\partial\Omega}N(f)\,dq\,dt,

and

(3.28)

\iint_{\Omega}\mathfrak{c}(X,t)f(X,t)g(X,t)\frac{dXdt}{\delta(X,t)}\leq C_{n}M\int_{\partial\Omega}N(f)\mathcal{A}(g)\,dq\,dt,

where

\mathcal{A}(g)(q,t):=\left(\iint_{\Gamma(q,t)}\left|g(Y,s)\right|^{2}\delta(Y,s)^{-n-2}dYds\right)^{1/2}\quad\text{for }(q,t)\in\partial\Omega.

Proof.

The first inequality (
3.27
) is classical, which is originally due to Carleson; see for example
[
33
, Corollary 3.3.6]
. The second inequality can be obtained by a stopping time argument; see for instance the proof of (3.33) in
[
14
]
. Note that
\mathcal{A}(\delta\nabla g)=S(g)
. See also
[
8
, Theorem 1]
or
[
28
, Proposition 2.10]
.
∎

Lemma 3.29
.

Let
f
be a continuous function defined on
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. For any
(X,t)\in\Omega
, let
F(X,t):=M(f(X,\cdot))(t)
be the maximal function of
f
in
t
. Then for any
p\in(1,\infty)
,

\int_{\partial\Omega}N_{a}(F)(x,t)^{p}dtdx\leq C\int_{\partial\Omega}N_{a}(f)(x,t)^{p}dtdx,

where the constant
C
depends only on
n
and
p
.

Proof:
We claim that for
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
,

(3.30)

N_{a}(F)(x,t)\leq N_{2a}(f)(x,t)+2M(N_{a}(f)(x,\cdot))(t).

Then the lemma would follow from the
L^{p}
boundedness of the maximal function.
To show (
3.30
), we fix
(x,t)
and write

(3.31)

N_{a}(F)(x,t)=\sup_{(Y,s)\in\Gamma_{a}(x,t)}\sup_{r>0}\fint_{s-r}^{s+r}\left|f(Y,\tau)\right|d\tau\\
=\sup_{(Y,s)\in\Gamma_{a}(x,t)}\left(\sup_{0<r<(ay_{n})^{2}}\fint_{s-r}^{s+r}\left|f(Y,\tau)\right|d\tau+\sup_{r\geq(ay_{n})^{2}}\fint_{s-r}^{s+r}\left|f(Y,\tau)\right|d\tau\right).

Fix any
(Y,s)\in\Gamma_{a}(x,t)
; that is,
\left|y-x\right|+\left|s-t\right|^{1/2}\leq ay_{n}
.
In the case when
r\geq(ay_{n})^{2}
,
s+r\leq t+\left|t-s\right|+r\leq t+(ay_{n})^{2}+r\leq t+2r
, and similarly,
s-r\geq t-2r
. So

\int_{s-r}^{s+r}\left|f(Y,\tau)\right|d\tau\leq\int_{t-2r}^{t+2r}\left|f(Y,\tau)\right|d\tau\leq\int_{t-2r}^{t+2r}N(f)(x,\tau)d\tau,

as
(Y,\tau)\in\Gamma(x,\tau)
. Therefore, for
r\geq(ay_{n})^{2}
,

\fint_{s-r}^{s+r}\left|f(Y,\tau)\right|d\tau\leq 2\fint_{t-2r}^{t+2r}N(f)(x,\tau)d\tau\leq 2M(N(f)(x,\cdot))(t).

When
0<r<(ay_{n})^{2}
, we observe that
(Y,\tau)\in\Gamma_{2a}(x,t)
for any
\tau\in[s-r,s+r]
. This entails that

\fint_{s-r}^{s+r}\left|f(Y,\tau)\right|d\tau\leq N_{2a}(f)(x,t),

which together with the first case proves (
3.30
).

\square

Lemma 3.32
.

If the matrix
A
satisfies (
1.2
) and

(3.33)

\left|\nabla A\right|\delta+\left|\partial_{t}A\right|\delta^{2}\in CM_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n+1}}(M),

then there exist matrices
B
and
D
such that
A=B+D
, where
B
satisfies (
1.2
) with the same constants
\lambda
and
\Lambda
. In addition, the following estimates hold:

(3.34)

\left|\nabla B\right|\delta+\left|\partial_{t}B\right|\delta^{2}+\left|D\right|\in CM_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n+1}}(CM),

(3.35)

\left|\partial_{t}\nabla B\right|\delta^{3}\in CM_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n+1}}(CM),

and

(3.36)

\left|x_{n}\nabla B(X,t)\right|+\left|x_{n}^{2}\partial_{t}B(X,t)\right|+\left|x_{n}^{3}\partial_{t}\nabla B(X,t)\right|\leq C,

where the constant
C
depends only on
n
and
\Lambda
(and not on the Carleson constant
M
).

Remark 3.37
.

(1)

An analog of this lemma in the elliptic case was given in
[
31
, Lemma 2.1]
. We note that as in
[
31
]
, the condition (
3.33
) can be weakened and the (analogous) result holds for a general domain
\Omega
. The idea of the lemma in the elliptic case appeared in
[
18
]
and various weaker versions of the result have been used in the literature (e.g.
[
15
]
).

(2)

One can derive estimates for higher derivatives on
B
, but (
3.35
) and (
3.36
) are all that is needed in the rest of the paper.

(3)

It is easy to see from the proof that if
A
is in block form (
8.1
), then
B
is in block form too.

Proof:
Let
\theta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
be a nonnegative function such that
\supp\theta\subset B(0,\frac{1}{10})
,
\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\theta(X,t)dXdt=1
. Define

\theta_{X,t}(Y,s):=\frac{1}{x_{n}^{n+2}}\theta\left(\frac{Y-X}{x_{n}},\frac{s-t}{x_{n}^{2}}\right),

(3.38)

B(X,t):=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}A(Y,s)\,\theta_{X,t}(Y,s)\,dYds,\quad\text{ and }\quad C:=A-B.

We compute the derivatives of
\theta_{X,t}
and get the following.

(3.39)

\partial_{x_{k}}\theta_{X,t}(Y,s)=\frac{1}{x_{n}}(\partial_{k}\theta)_{X,t}(Y,s)=-\partial_{y_{k}}\theta_{X,t}(Y,s)\quad\text{for }k=1,\dots,n-1,

(3.40)

\partial_{x_{n}}\theta_{X,t}(Y,s)=-\frac{n+2}{x_{n}}\theta_{X,t}(Y,s)+\frac{1}{x_{n}}\left(\partial_{n}\theta\right)_{X,t}(Y,s)-\frac{1}{x_{n}^{2}}(X-Y)\cdot(\nabla\theta)_{X,t}(Y,s)\\
-\frac{2(t-s)}{x_{n}^{3}}(\partial_{t}\theta)_{X,t}(Y,s)\\
=\frac{1}{x_{n}}\left[\divg_{Y}\left((X-Y)\cdot\theta_{X,t}(Y,s)\right)+\partial_{s}\left(2(t-s)\theta_{X,t}(Y,s)\right)\right]-\partial_{y_{n}}\theta_{X,t}(Y,s),

and

(3.41)

\partial_{t}\theta_{X,t}(Y,s)=\frac{1}{x_{n}^{2}}(\partial_{t}\theta)_{X,t}(Y,s)=-\partial_{s}\theta_{X,t}(Y,s).

Then it is easy to see that

x_{n}^{n+3}\left|\nabla_{X}\theta_{X,t}\right|+x_{n}^{n+4}\left|\partial_{t}\theta_{X,t}\right|+x_{n}^{n+5}\left|\partial_{t}\nabla_{X}\theta_{X,t}\right|\lesssim{\mathds{1}}_{B_{x_{n}/4}(X,t)},

and thus,

\left|x_{n}\nabla B(X,t)\right|++\left|x_{n}^{2}\partial_{t}B(X,t)\right|+\left|x_{n}^{3}\partial_{t}\nabla B(X,t)\right|\leq\frac{C}{x_{n}^{n+2}}\iint_{B_{x_{n}/4}(X,t)}\left|A\right|dYds\leq C\Lambda,

which is (
3.36
). Here and in the sequel,
B_{r}(X,t)=\left\{(Y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}:\left|Y-X\right|^{2}+\left|t-s\right|<r^{2}\right\}
is the parabolic ball.

By (
3.39
), we can move
\partial_{x_{k}}
from
\theta_{X,t}
in (
3.38
) for
k=1,\dots,n-1
to get that

(3.42)

\partial_{k}B(X,t)=\iint\partial_{y_{k}}A(Y,s)\theta_{X,t}(Y,s)dYds.

By (
3.40
),

(3.43)

\partial_{n}B(X,t)=\iint\partial_{y_{n}}A(Y,s)\theta_{X,t}(Y,s)dYds-\frac{1}{x_{n}}\iint\nabla_{Y}A(Y,s)(X-Y)\theta_{X,t}(Y,s)dYds\\
-\frac{2}{x_{n}}\iint\partial_{s}A(Y,s)(t-s)\theta_{X,t}(Y,s)dYds.

So by the support property of
\theta_{X,t}
,

\left|x_{n}\nabla B(X,t)\right|\lesssim x_{n}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{x_{n}/4}(X,t)}\left|\nabla A(Y,s)\right|dYds+x_{n}^{2}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{x_{n}/4}}\left|\partial_{s}A(Y,s)\right|dYds.

By Fubini’s theorem and our assumption (
3.33
), we get

\iint_{B\cap\Omega}\left|x_{n}\nabla B(X,t)\right|^{2}\frac{dXdt}{x_{n}}\leq CM\sigma(2B\cap\partial\Omega)

for any parabolic ball
B
centered at
\partial\Omega
. Using (
3.41
) and a similar argument as above one can get that

\iint_{B\cap\Omega}\left|x_{n}^{2}\partial_{t}B(X,t)\right|^{2}\frac{dXdt}{x_{n}}\leq C\iint_{2B\cap\Omega}x_{n}^{3}\left|\partial_{t}A\right|^{2}dXdt\leq CM\sigma(2B\cap\partial\Omega).

This proves the desired estimate for the first two terms in (
3.34
).

To show (
3.35
), we take
\partial_{t}
to (
3.42
) and (
3.43
). We obtain

\partial_{t}\partial_{k}B(X,t)=\frac{1}{x_{n}^{2}}\iint\partial_{y_{k}}A(Y,s)\left(\partial_{t}\theta\right)_{X,t}(Y,s)dYds\quad\text{for }k=1,\dots,n-1,

and so

\left|x_{n}^{3}\partial_{t}\nabla_{x}B(X,t)\right|\leq C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{x_{n}/4}(X,t)}y_{n}\left|\nabla_{y}A(Y,s)\right|dYds.

A similar computation shows that

\left|x_{n}^{3}\partial_{t}\nabla_{n}B(X,t)\right|\leq C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{x_{n}/4}(X,t)}y_{n}\left|\nabla A(Y,s)\right|dYds+C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{x_{n}/4}(X,t)}y_{n}^{2}\left|\nabla_{s}A(Y,s)\right|dYds.

Then (
3.35
) follows from Fubini’s theorem and (
3.33
).

We turn to the estimate for the matrix
D=A-B
. We can write

\left|D(X,t)\right|=\left|\iint(A(X,t)-A(Y,s))\theta_{X,t}(Y,s)dYds\right|\\
\leq C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{x_{n}/4}(X,t)}\left|A(X,t)-A(Y,s)\right|dYds\quad\text{ for any }(X,t)\in\Omega.

Fix
(Z,\tau)\in B\cap\Omega
, we have

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{z_{n}/10}(Z,\tau)}\left|D(X,t)\right|^{2}dXdt\leq C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{z_{n}/10}(Z,\tau)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{x_{n}/4}(X,t)}\left|A(X,t)-A(Y,s)\right|^{2}dYds\\
\leq C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{z_{n}/10}(Z,\tau)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{z_{n}/2}(Z,\tau)}\left|A(X,t)-A(Y,s)\right|^{2}dYds

as
B_{x_{n}/4}(X,t)\subset B_{z_{n}/2}(Z,\tau)
for
(X,t)\in B_{z_{n}/10}(Z,\tau)
.
By the Poincaré inequality, one can see that the above is bounded by

C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{z_{n}/2}(Z,\tau)}x_{n}^{2}\left|\nabla A(X,t)\right|^{2}dXdt+C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{z_{n}/2}(Z,\tau)}x_{n}^{4}\left|\partial_{t}A(X,t)\right|^{2}dXdt,

and hence, by Fubini’s theorem,

\iint_{B\cap\Omega}\left|D\right|^{2}dXdt\leq C\iint_{B\cap\Omega}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{z_{n}/10}(Z,\tau)}\left|D\right|^{2}dXdt\\
\leq C\iint_{2B\cap\Omega}\left(\left|\nabla A\right|^{2}x_{n}+\left|\partial_{t}A\right|^{2}x_{n}^{3}\right)dXdt\leq CM\sigma(B\cap\partial\Omega),

which completes the proof of (
3.34
), and the lemma.

\square

Lemma 3.44
.

Let
p>1
,
f
be a non-negative function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
, and
g
be a
t
-independent non-negative function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
. Assume that the measure
\nu
given by
d\nu=f(X,t)^{p}g(X)dXdt
is a Carleson measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. Then for any
\kappa>0
, the measure
\widetilde{\nu}
given by
d\widetilde{\nu}=M_{\kappa x_{n}^{2}}(f(X,\cdot))(t)^{p}g(X)dXdt
is a Carleson measure, where

(3.45)

M_{R}(f)(t):=\sup\limits_{t\in I,\ell(I)<R}\fint_{I}\left|f(s)\right|ds

is the localized uncentered Hardy-Littlewood maximal function at scale at most
R
. Moreover, there exists
C>0
such that

\left\|\widetilde{\nu}\right\|_{C}\leq C\max\left\{1,\kappa\right\}\left\|\nu\right\|_{C}
.

Proof:
Let
p>1
,
R>0
, and
(a,b)
be any interval in
\mathbb{R}
. One can show that for any
L^{p}
integrable function
h
on
(a-R,b+R)
,

(3.46)

\left\|M_{R}(h)\right\|_{L^{p}(a,b)}\leq\left\|M_{R}(h{\mathds{1}}_{(a-R,b+R)})\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C\left\|h\right\|_{L^{p}(a-R,b+R)}.

Let
Q_{r}=Q_{r}(x_{0},t_{0})
be a parabolic cube on
\partial\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
. Then by (
3.46
),

\int_{x_{n}=0}^{r}\int_{(x,t)\in Q_{r}}M_{\kappa x_{n}^{2}}(f(X,\cdot))(t)^{p}g(X)dxdtdx_{n}\\
\leq C\int_{x_{n}=0}^{r}\int_{x\in Q_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{t_{0}-(\kappa+1)x_{n}^{2}}^{t_{0}+(\kappa+1)x_{n}^{2}}f(X,t)^{p}g(X)dtdxdx_{n}\\
\leq C\int_{x_{n}=0}^{r}\int_{x\in Q_{r}(x_{0})}\int_{t_{0}-(\kappa+1)r^{2}}^{t_{0}+(\kappa+1)r^{2}}f(X,t)^{p}g(X)dtdxdx_{n}=:T.

If
\kappa<1
, then

T\leq\int_{x_{n}=0}^{2r}\int_{(x,t)\in Q_{2r}}f(X,t)^{p}g(X)dtdxx_{n}\leq C_{n}\left\|\nu\right\|_{C}r^{n+1}.

If
\kappa\geq 1
, then we can cover
Q_{r}(x_{0})\times(t_{0}-(\kappa+1)r^{2},t_{0}+(\kappa+1)r^{2})
by
[\kappa]+1
parabolic cubes
Q_{i}=Q_{r}(x_{0},t_{i})
of equal size, and therefore,

T\leq\sum_{i=1}^{[\kappa]+1}\int_{x_{n}=0}^{r}\int_{(x,t)\in Q_{i}}f(X,t)^{p}g(X)dtdxx_{n}\leq C\kappa\left\|\nu\right\|_{C}r^{n+1}.

This proves that
\left\|\widetilde{\nu}\right\|_{C}\leq C\max\left\{1,\kappa\right\}\left\|\nu\right\|_{C}
.

\square

With Lemma
3.44
in hand, we can construct many Carleson measures on
\Omega
if we assume that our coefficients satisfy the Carleson condition (
1.3
). In particular, we have the following.

Corollary 3.47
.

Let
K\geq 1
and
\nu
given by

d\nu=x_{n}^{3}M_{K^{2}x_{n}^{2}}(\left|\partial_{t}A(X,\cdot)\right|)(t)^{2}dXdt
. Then
\nu

is a Carleson measure on
\Omega
if
x_{n}^{3}\left|\partial_{t}A(X,t)\right|^{2}dXdt
is so. Moreover, there exists
C>0
such that

\left\|\nu\right\|_{C}\leq CK^{2}\left\|x_{n}^{3}\left|\partial_{t}A(X,t)\right|^{2}dXdt\right\|_{C}\leq CK^{2}\left\|\mu_{||}\right\|_{C}.

Proof:
Simply take
p=2
,
\kappa=K^{2}
,
f(X,t)=\left|\partial_{t}A(X,t)\right|
, and
g(X)=x_{n}^{3}
in Lemma
3.44
.

\square

4.
Proof of the main results

In this section, we outline how it all fits together and then prove our main theorems, relying on the results of Sections 5 - 11.

4.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.6
assuming Theorem
1.8

We first prove Theorem
1.6
for
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Step 1
. It suffices to prove Theorem
1.6
under assumption (1), that is that (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) hold. If, instead, we assume condition (2) of Theorem
1.6
, then, as in

[
18
]
, we construct a new matrix
B
such that
A=B+D
where
B
and
D
are as in Lemma
3.32
. In fact the proof is analogous to the one given in Lemma
3.32
. The matrix
B
will satisfy (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) (and also ellipticity when
A
is elliptic). Thus, if we can prove Theorem
1.6
for
B
, then by Lemma
3.16
we also obtain solvability of the
Regularity problem
(R)^{\mathcal{L}}_{q}
for the original matrix
A
for some
q>1
. This is sufficient to conclude that

(R)_{p}^{\mathcal{L}}
holds if and only if
(D)_{p^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
by Lemma
3.20
.

Step 2
. Hence for the remainder of this section we assume that
A
is elliptic and (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) hold.
By Theorem
5.1
,
(R)_{p}^{\mathcal{L}}
will hold if and only if
(D)_{p^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
holds, assuming that

(i)

(R)_{q}^{\mathcal{L}_{0}}
holds for all
q\in(1,\infty)
, where
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is the parabolic operator in block form given by (
5.2
).

(ii)

For any
q\in(1,\infty)
and any
f\in\dot{L}^{q}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)
, the energy solution
\widetilde{u}
to
\mathcal{L}_{0}\widetilde{u}=0
in
\Omega
with
\widetilde{u}|_{\partial\Omega}=f
satisfies

\left\|S(\nabla\widetilde{u})\right\|_{L^{q}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{\dot{L}^{q}_{1,1/2}}.

Theorem
1.8
(proven below) ensures that (i) holds. For (ii), the estimate
\left\|S(\nabla\widetilde{u})\right\|_{L^{q}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{\dot{L}^{q}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}
follows from a combination of Theorem
9.1
, which shows that
\left\|S(\nabla\widetilde{u})\right\|_{L^{q}}\leq C\left\|N(\nabla\widetilde{u})\right\|_{L^{q}}
, and solvability of
(R)^{\mathcal{L}_{0}}_{q}
, which implies that
\left\|N(\nabla\widetilde{u})\right\|_{L^{q}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{\dot{L}^{q}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}
.

Step 3
. Assume now
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
for a Lipschitz domain
\mathcal{O}
as defined in Definition
2.10
. We distinguish two cases:

(i)

\mathcal{O}
is unbounded,

(ii)

\mathcal{O}
is bounded.

In both cases, it suffices to show that
(R)_{p}^{\mathcal{L}}
holds at least one
p\in(1,\infty)
, as the rest of our claim then follows from Lemma
3.20
.

In case (i),
\mathcal{O}
can be written as
\left\{(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:x_{n}>\varphi(x^{\prime})\right\}
where
\varphi
is a Lipschitz function. Define the map
\rho(x^{\prime},x_{n},t)=(\Psi(x^{\prime},x_{n}),t)
as in (
11.22
), where
\Psi(x^{\prime},x_{n})=(x^{\prime},c_{0}x_{n}+(\theta_{x_{n}}*\phi)(x^{\prime}))
,
where
(\theta_{s})_{s>0}
is smooth compactly supported approximate identity and
c_{0}
can be chosen large enough (depending only on

\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n-1})}
) so that
\Psi
is one to one. One can show that
\rho
is bi-Lipschitz and that the pull-back of the operator
\mathcal{L}
under the map
\rho
(denoted by
\mathcal{L}_{1}
) is an elliptic operator on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
that satisfies the Carleson condition (1) or (2) with respect to the domain
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
with a different constant (see the discussion below (
11.22
)). Therefore, we can apply Step 1 to conclude that the
L^{p}
Regulairty problem is solvable for some
p>1
for
\mathcal{L}_{1}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
, which yields that
(R)^{\mathcal{L}}_{p}
is solvable on
\Omega
as
\rho
is bi-Lipschitz.

The case (ii) is carried out in detail in Section 10. More precisely, it is shown there that matters can be reduced to to proving Theorem
11.12
. The proof of Theorem
11.12
consists of a similar argument as in case (i), as well as a comparison between solutions with boundary data with compact support.

We remark that in both cases, it is possible to track the exponent
p
in the argument to show directly using Step 1 that the range of
p
for which
(R)^{\mathcal{L}}_{p}
is solvable is the same as that of
(D)^{\mathcal{L}^{*}}_{p^{\prime}}
, but thanks to Lemma
3.20
, this is not necessary. In particular, we only need to establish solvability of the Regularity problem
(R)^{\mathcal{L}}_{q}
for one value of
q>1
.

4.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.8

Let
\Omega
,
A
, and
\mathcal{L}
be as in Theorem
1.8
. Let us denote by
\mu^{A}
the measure given by the density (
1.3
)- we emphasize the matrix
A
in the notation because we will need to introduce more matrices. Let
M:=\left\|\mu^{A}\right\|_{C}
. Since
(D)_{p^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{L}^{*}}
holds for all
p\in(1,\infty)
(c.f. Appendix

A
), it suffices to show that
(R)_{q_{0}}^{\mathcal{L}}
is solvable for some
q_{0}\in(1,\infty)
and then by Lemma
3.20
we can deduce solvability of
(R)_{p}^{\mathcal{L}}
for all
p\in(1,\infty)
.

Let
B
be the matrix found in Lemma
3.32
, and so the measure
\mu^{B}
given by

d\mu^{B}:=\left(x_{n}\left|\nabla B\right|^{2}+x_{n}^{3}\left|\partial_{t}B\right|^{2}+x_{n}^{5}\left|\partial_{t}\nabla B\right|^{2}\right)dxdx_{n}dt

is a Carleson measure on
\Omega
, with
\left\|\mu^{B}\right\|_{C}\leq C\left\|\mu^{A}\right\|_{C}=CM
. Also,
B\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
satisfies the bound (
3.36
) where the constant
C
depends only on
n
and
\Lambda
.
Moreover, by Remark
3.37
(3),
B
is in block form, and we write
B=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}B_{\parallel}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&1\\
\end{array}\right]
.

For
k>1
, we define a new matrix,
B^{k}(x,x_{n},t):=B(x,kx_{n},t)
for
(x,x_{n},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. This dilation in
x_{n}
is a key step in the proof, and
appeared at a similar point in the proof of the elliptic Regularity problem in
[
14
]
. As we shall see in a moment, the important observation is that this matrix gives rise
to a new Carleson measure that has small norm for sufficiently large
k
. Let us denote

\mathcal{L}_{0}:=\divg(B\nabla\cdot)-\partial_{t}=\divg_{x}(B_{\parallel}\nabla_{x}\cdot)+\partial^{2}_{nn}-\partial_{t},

and

(4.1)

\mathcal{L}_{k}:=\divg(B^{k}\nabla\cdot)-\partial_{t}=\divg_{x}(B^{k}_{\parallel}\nabla_{x}\cdot)+\partial^{2}_{nn}-\partial_{t}\qquad\text{for }k>0.

We observe that
B^{k}
enjoys the following properties: for
k>1
,
B^{k}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
, and

(a)

for all
(x,x_{n},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
,

x_{n}\left|\nabla_{x}B^{k}(x,x_{n},t)\right|+x_{n}^{2}\left|\partial_{t}B^{k}(x,x_{n},t)\right|\leq C\,k^{-1},\quad x_{n}\left|\partial_{n}B^{k}(x,x_{n},t)\right|\leq C

for some
C
depending only on
n
and
\Lambda
;

(b)

the measure
\mu_{||}^{k}
given by

d\mu_{||}^{k}:=\left(x_{n}\left|\nabla_{x}B^{k}\right|^{2}+x_{n}^{3}\left|\partial_{t}B^{k}\right|^{2}\right)dxdx_{n}dt

is a Carleson measure on
\Omega
, with
\left\|\mu_{||}^{k}\right\|_{C}\leq\left(\left\|\mu^{B}\right\|_{C}+C\log k\right)k^{-2}\leq C(M+\log k)k^{-2}
;

(c)

the measure
\mu^{k}
given by

d\mu^{k}:=\left(x_{n}\left|\nabla B^{k}\right|^{2}+x_{n}^{3}\left|\partial_{t}B^{k}\right|^{2}+x_{n}^{5}\left|\partial_{t}\nabla B^{k}\right|^{2}\right)dxdx_{n}dt

is a Carleson measure on
\Omega
, with
\left\|\mu^{k}\right\|_{C}\leq\left\|\mu^{B}\right\|_{C}+C\log k\leq C(M+\log k)
;

(d)

\mathcal{L}_{k}
is a Carleson perturbation of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
, and more precisely, the measure
\nu_{k}
given by

d\nu_{k}:=\left|B^{k}(x,x_{n},t)-B(x,x_{n},t)\right|^{2}\frac{dxdx_{n}dt}{x_{n}}

is a Carleson measure on
\Omega
, with
\left\|\nu_{k}\right\|_{C}\leq Ck(M+\log k)
.

Property (a) is a direct consequence of the bound (
3.36
). For (b) and (c), we only show the computation of the Carleson norm of
\delta\left|\partial_{n}B^{k}\right|^{2}
to illustrate the idea. Fix any
R>0
and any parabolic cube
Q_{R}
with sidelength
R
on
\partial\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\right)
. We write

\int_{x_{n}=0}^{R}\int_{Q_{R}}\left|\partial_{n}B^{k}(x,x_{n},t)\right|^{2}x_{n}dxdtdx_{n}=k^{2}\int_{x_{n}=0}^{R}\int_{Q_{R}}\left|(\partial_{n}B)(x,kx_{n},t)\right|^{2}x_{n}dxdtdx_{n}\\
=\int_{0}^{kR}\int_{Q_{R}}\left|\partial_{y_{n}}B(x,y_{n},t)\right|^{2}y_{n}dxdy_{n}dt=\int_{0}^{R}\int_{Q_{R}}+\int_{R}^{kR}\int_{Q_{R}}.

Using the Carleson condition on
\mu^{B}
, the first integral on the right-hand side is bounded by
\left\|\mu^{B}\right\|_{C}\sigma(Q_{R})
. For the second integral, we use the bound
\left|\partial_{y_{n}}B(x,y_{n},t)\right|\leq Cy_{n}^{-1}
to get that

\int_{R}^{kR}\int_{Q_{R}}\left|\partial_{y_{n}}B(x,y_{n},t)\right|^{2}y_{n}dxdy_{n}dt\leq C\sigma(Q_{R})\log k.

From this it follows that
\left\|\delta\left|\partial_{n}B^{k}\right|^{2}\right\|_{C}\leq C(M+\log k)
.

The smallness of the Carleson norm for the expression
x_{n}\left|\nabla_{x}B^{k}(x,x_{n},t)\right|
in (b) comes from the fact that
the
k^{2}
factor that multiplies
\left|\partial_{n}B^{k}(x,x_{n},t)\right|^{2}x_{n}dxdtdx_{n}
above will not appear in that computation.

Property (d) can be verified using the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus and Fubini’s theorem: fix
R
and
Q_{R}
as before, we write

\int_{x_{n}=0}^{R}\int_{Q_{R}}\left|B^{k}-B\right|^{2}\frac{dxdtdx_{n}}{x_{n}}=\int_{x_{n}=0}^{R}\int_{Q_{R}}\left|\int_{x_{n}}^{kx_{n}}\partial_{s}B(x,s,t)ds\right|^{2}\frac{dxdtdx_{n}}{x_{n}}\\
\leq k\int_{x_{n}=0}^{R}\int_{Q_{R}}\int_{x_{n}}^{kx_{n}}\left|\partial_{s}B(x,s,t)\right|^{2}ds\,dxdtdx_{n}\leq k\int_{s=0}^{kR}\int_{Q_{R}}\left|\partial_{s}B(x,s,t)\right|^{2}s\,dxdtds.

Then we can obtain the estimate for
\left\|\nu_{k}\right\|_{C}
by splitting the integral in
s
into
\int_{0}^{R}
and
\int_{R}^{kR}
.

We are now done with all the preparations and ready to prove Theorem
1.8
.

Step 1.
We check that the matrix
B^{k}
meets the conditions of Theorem
8.2
, and so there exist constants
C>0
and
K_{0}\geq 1
depending only on the dimension and the ellipticity constants, such that for any
f\in\dot{L}^{2}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)
and any
K\geq K_{0}
,
the energy solution
u
to
\mathcal{L}_{k}u=0
in
\Omega
,
u|_{\partial\Omega}=f
satisfies

\left\|S(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}\leq C\left(K^{5}\left\|\mu^{k}_{||}\right\|_{C}+K^{-1}\left\|\mu^{k}\right\|_{C}\right)\left\|N(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}+C\left\|f\right\|_{\dot{L}_{1,1/2}^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}\\
\leq C(M+\log k)\left(K^{5}k^{-2}+K^{-1}\right)\left\|N(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}+C\left\|f\right\|_{\dot{L}_{1,1/2}^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}

by using properties (b) and (c) in the second inequality.

Step 2.
Let
\delta>0
be as in Theorem
10.1
, and let
k>1
to be large enough so that

(4.2)

\left(\left\|\mu_{||}^{k}\right\|_{C}+\left\|\delta\nabla_{x}B^{k}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{2}\right)\left(1+\left\|\delta\nabla B^{k}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{2}\right)\leq\frac{C(1+M+\log k)}{k^{2}}<\delta.

With this requirement on
k
,
B^{k}
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
10.1
, and so if we a priori know that
\left\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty
then

(4.3)

\left\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\left(1+\|\mu^{k}\|_{C}+\left\|\delta|\nabla B^{k}|\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\right)\left[\left\|S(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}\right]\\
+C\left\|A(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}.

where
u
is as in Step 1, and
C
depends only on
n
,
\delta
, and the ellipticity constants.
By (
6.4
), we have

\|A(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|S(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}}+C\left\|\mu_{||}^{k}\right\|_{C}^{1/2}\|N(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}}.

Plugging this into (
4.3
), and then using properties (a) - (c) of
B^{k}
, one obtains that

\left\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C(1+M+\log k)\left(\left\|S(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla_{x}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}\right)\\
+\frac{C(M+\log k)^{1/2}}{k}\left\|N(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}.

Step 3.
We combine the estimates in Steps 1 and 2. Note that
\left\|N(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left\|\widetilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}}
with an implicit constant depending only on
n
and
\Lambda
thanks to Lemma
3.22
and property (a) of the matrix
B^{k}
. We get that for
k
satisfying (
4.2
) and for
K\geq K_{0}
,

(4.4)

\left\|\widetilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{1}(K^{5}k^{-2}+K^{-1})\left\|\widetilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C_{2}\left\|f\right\|_{\dot{L}^{2}_{1,1/2}}^{2},

where
C_{1}=O((\log k)^{3})
and
C_{2}=O((\log k)^{2})
.

We take
K=k^{1/5}
and let
k
be sufficiently large so that all the following estimates are satisfied: (
4.2
),
k^{1/5}\geq K_{0}
, and
C_{1}(k^{-1}+k^{-1/5})\leq 1/2
. With this choice of
K
and
k
, we can hide the first term on the right-hand side of (
4.4
) to obtain that

\left\|\widetilde{N}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{\dot{L}^{2}_{1,1/2}}^{2}
, which implies that
(R)_{2}^{\mathcal{L}_{k}}
is solvable.

Step 4.
By property (d) of
\mathcal{L}_{k}
and Lemma
3.16
, we deduce that
(R)_{q}^{\mathcal{L}_{0}}
is solvable for some
1<q<2
. By our definition of
B
and Lemma
3.32
,
\left|A-B\right|^{2}\frac{dxdx_{n}dt}{x_{n}}
is a Carleson measure on
\Omega
. Thus, by Lemma
3.16
again, we conclude that
(R)_{q_{0}}^{\mathcal{L}}
is solvable for some
1<q_{0}<2
