. Appendix
A
shows that the Dirichlet problem for operators with a block form matrix is solvable for all
1<p<\infty
, so from
this and Lemma
3.20
and we obtain

(R)_{p}^{\mathcal{L}}
is also solvable for all
1<p<\infty
.

This completes the proof, modulo an argument that
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty
in the first place, since we used finiteness of this term in the above inequalities.

Step 5. Further approximation.
Consider a matrix
B^{k}
as in step 3. Let us drop dependence of this matrix on
k
, as we have already fixed
k
and call this matrix just
B
. We approximate
B
by a sequence of matrices
B^{i}
,
i=1,2,3,\dots
as follows.

Let
\eta^{i}
be a smooth non-negative cutoff function in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
that is equal to
1
on a parabolic ball
B(0,i)

and vanishing outside the ball
B(0,2i)
. Consider

(4.5)

B^{i}(x^{\prime},x_{n},t)=\eta^{i}(x,x_{n},t)B(x^{\prime},x_{n}+1/i,t)+(1-\eta^{i})I,

where
I
is the
n\times n
identity matrix. It follows that all matrices
B^{i}
on the domain
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
are uniformly elliptic with the same ellipticity constants, each
B^{i}
is the identity matrix outside a large ball centered at zero and that
\|\nabla B^{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}B^{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}<\infty
. This latter
bound is a consequence of the fact that
|\nabla B|\leq Cx_{n}^{-1}
and thus
|\nabla B^{i}|\leq C(x_{n}+1/i)^{-1}\leq Ci
, with similar argument for
\partial_{t}B
.
Furthermore each
B^{i}
satisfies the same Carleson measure conditions as
B
does and thus we have properties (a)-(c) as stated just below (
4.1
)
and with same constants as
B
. Finally, we observe that on all compact subsets
K\subset\Omega
we have that
B^{i}\to B
uniformly in the
L^{\infty}
norm as
i\to\infty
.

Consider the approximate energy solutions
u^{i}
to the PDE problems

\mathcal{L}^{i}u^{i}=\mathrm{div}(B^{i}\nabla u^{i})-\partial_{t}u^{i}=0\quad\mbox{in }\Omega\quad\mbox{with}\quad u^{i}\Big|_{\partial\Omega}=f,

where
f
is in the trace space

\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)
. As for all
i
the ellipticity constants have the same bounds we can conclude that the energy norm
\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
of all
u^{i}
is uniformly bounded by the same constant which only depends on the
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)
norm of
f
.

As the solutions
u^{i}
are determined up to a constant, and enjoy interior Hölder continuity, we may without loss of generality assume that

u^{i}(0,1,0)=0
for all
i
. With this in hand, and the fact that
\sup_{i}\|u^{i}\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)}<\infty

we conclude that there exists a subsequence of the sequence
(u^{i})
such that this subsequence

u^{i_{n}}
converges weakly in
\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
to a function
u\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
with

u(0,1,0)=0
. With the normalization
u^{i}(0,1,0)=0
in place,
for any
R>0

the space
L^{2}(\Omega\cap B(0,R))
is compactly embedded in
\dot{\mathsf{E}}
with uniformly bounded

\sup_{i}\|u^{i}\|_{L^{2}(B(0,R))}
(by the Sobolev embedding theorem), allowing us to conclude that we have the convergence

u^{i_{n}}\to u\quad\mbox{as }n\to\infty\quad\mbox{in the $L^{2}$ norm on all balls $B(0,R)$, $R>0$}.

Fix now a compact subset
K\subset\Omega
and consider a test function
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(K)
. Since each
u^{i_{n}}
is a weak solution of its corresponding PDE we get that:

\iint_{\Omega}u^{i_{n}}\partial_{t}\varphi-\iint_{\Omega}B\nabla u^{i_{n}}\cdot\nabla\varphi=\iint_{\Omega}(B^{i_{n}}-B)\nabla u^{i_{n}}\cdot\nabla\varphi.

As
n\to\infty
we obtain that the RHS converges to zero as
B^{i}\to B
uniformly on
K
and hence
the weak limit
u
solves the PDE

\mathcal{L}u=\mathrm{div}(B\nabla u)-\partial_{t}u=0\quad\mbox{in }\Omega.

Because the
\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
norm of
u
is bounded by
\sup_{i}\|u^{i}\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)}<\infty
, we conclude that
u
is also an energy solution in
\Omega
.
The PDE for the difference
u^{i_{n}}-u
, which is

\mathcal{L}(u^{i_{n}}-u)=\mathrm{div}(B\nabla(u^{i_{n}}-u))-\partial_{t}(u^{i_{n}}-u)=\mathrm{div}f^{i}:=\mathrm{div}((B-B^{i_{n}})\nabla u^{i_{n}})

yields a Caccioppoli inequality of the form:

\iint_{B((X,t),r)}|\nabla(u^{i_{n}}-u)|^{2}\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}}\iint_{B((X,t),2r)}|u^{i_{n}}-u|^{2}+C\iint_{B((X,t),2r)}\left(f^{i}\right)^{2}.

Since
f^{i}\to 0
in
L^{2}
norm on compact subsets of
\Omega
, from weak convergence
\nabla u^{i_{n}}\to\nabla u
in
L^{2}
, we actually have strong convergence in
L^{2}
on compact subsets of
\Omega
.

We will prove below in step 6 that for the approximate solutions
u^{i}
we have the a priori bound
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u^{i})\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty
, as required above for
f
with finite
\dot{L}^{2}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)
norm.
Assuming that for the moment, by step 3 we have that for each
i
, the estimate

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u^{i})\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{2}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)},

holds with a constant
C
which is independent of
i
, depending only on ellipticity of the matrix and the Carleson norm of its coefficients.
Fix now a compact subset
K\subset\Omega
and let
\tilde{N}_{K}
be a variant of the nontangential maximal
function
\tilde{N}
defined with respect to the modified nontangential cones
\Gamma_{K}(q,\tau)=\Gamma(q,\tau)\cap K
. Because of the strong convergence
\nabla u^{i_{n}}\to\nabla u
in
L^{2}
on compact sets we have
that
\tilde{N}_{K}(\nabla u^{i})\to\tilde{N}_{K}(\nabla u)
uniformly on
\partial\Omega
and furthermore the functions
\tilde{N}_{K}(\nabla u^{i})
and
\tilde{N}_{K}(\nabla u)
vanish outside of a compact subset of the boundary. Hence
\tilde{N}_{K}(\nabla(u^{i_{n}}-u))\to 0
in
L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
. Finally, this implies that

\left\|\tilde{N}_{K}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\tilde{N}_{K}(\nabla u^{i_{n}})\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u^{i_{n}})\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{2}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.

Taking the supremum over all compact subsets
K\subset\Omega
then implies that

(4.6)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{2}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.

To see that
u
attains the boundary value
f
we reason as follows. Consider an extension
\tilde{f}
of
f
into
\Omega
(for example, the
solution of the heat equation in
\Omega
with datum
f
). Then each
u^{i}
can be written also as
\tilde{f}+v^{i}
where
v^{i}\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{0}(\Omega)
solves the inhomogeneous PDE:

\mathcal{L}^{i}v^{i}=\mathrm{div}(B^{i}\nabla v^{i})-\partial_{t}v^{i}=-\mathrm{div}(B^{i}\nabla\tilde{f}).

As before, the
\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{0}(\Omega)
norms are uniformly bounded (by
C\|\nabla\tilde{f}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
) and hence there is a subsequence (which we may again take to be
(i_{n})
) such that
v^{i}\to v
weakly in
\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{0}(\Omega)
and
v\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}_{0}(\Omega)
. Given that
u^{i_{n}}\to u
strongly on compact sets we must have that
u=\tilde{f}+v
and therefore Tr
\,u=f
. Thus
u
is the energy solution of our PDE with boundary datum
f
as desired. It follows that such
u
solves the
L^{2}
Regularity problem for the operator with matrix
B
and boundary data
f
thanks to (
4.6
).

Step 6. An a priori estimate for
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u^{i})\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}
.
It follows that we only need to prove that
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u^{i})\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty

for solutions
u^{i}
, as defined above associated to matrices
B^{i}

having the properties listed in step 5. We fix
i\in\mathbb{N}
and consider a matrix
B^{i}
as above. Dropping the index
i
we may assume that
B
is uniformly elliptic, satisfies the Carleson measure properties listed as (a)-(c) under (
4.1
), equals the identity matrix outside a large ball centered at zero, and also has the property
that
\|\nabla B\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}B\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}<\infty
.
Let
u
be an energy solution to
\mathcal{L}u=0
where
\mathcal{L}=\mathrm{div}(B\nabla\cdot)-\partial_{t}
. Without loss of generality assume also that
u\Big|_{\partial\Omega}=f
for some compactly supported Lipschitz function
f
(in all variables).
It suffices to establish solvability for a class of function that is dense in our underlying space since the solvability operator then extends continuously to the whole space thanks to the estimate (
4.6
).

As explained in section
10
we have for such
u
:
\nabla_{x}u=\vec{V}+\vec{\eta}
, where components of
\vec{V}
solve the Dirichlet problem (for which nontangential estimates are proven in Appendix
A
) and
\vec{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\dots,\eta_{n-1})
solves the PDE system (
10.22
).
Our first objective is to prove that
\eta
is bounded in
\Omega
. This can be proven as follows: Initially we prove that
\|\vec{\eta}\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)}<\infty
. This follows from (
10.22
), provided we show that we can apply Lax-Milgram to this system in the spirit of subsection
2.2
. This requires establishing coercivity of the system (
10.22
), as well as proving bounds for the right-hand side terms of the form

\partial_{i}((\partial_{m}b_{ij})v_{j}),

where for
v_{j}
we have an
L^{\infty}
bound by the maximum principle. Also, since
B=I
outside a bounded set, we have
\nabla B=0
outside a bounded set and hence
(\partial_{m}b_{ij})v_{j}
belongs to
L^{p}(\Omega)
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
. It follows this term is not problematic. The second term on the righthand side of
(
10.22
) includes components of
\vec{\eta}
and when paired with
\eta_{m}
enjoys the estimate:

\left|\iint_{\Omega}\partial_{i}((\partial_{m}b_{ij})\eta_{j})\eta_{m}\right|=\left|\iint_{\Omega}((\partial_{m}b_{ij})\eta_{j})\partial_{i}\eta_{m}\right|\leq C\left(\iint_{\Omega}|\nabla\vec{\eta}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\iint_{\Omega}\frac{|\vec{\eta}|^{2}}{x_{n}^{2}}\right)^{1/2},

by Cauchy-Schwarz for some small
C
(c.f. assumption (a) stated under (
4.1
)). Finally, by the Hardy-Sobolev inequality, which applies here as
\vec{\eta}
vanishes on the boundary, the right hand side of the above inequality is bounded by
C\iint_{\Omega}|\nabla\vec{\eta}|^{2}
.
Thus this term can be absorbed (thanks to smallness of
C
) by the coercivity the lefthand side enjoys (c.f. subsection
2.2
).
It follows that
\vec{\eta}\in\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega)
. Given that
\vec{\eta}|_{\partial\Omega}=0
we get by the embedding theorem
and the fact that
\nabla B
vanishes outside bounded sets
that
(\partial_{m}b_{ij})\eta_{j}\in L^{2n/(n-2)}(\Omega)
. By a bootstrap argument we may then further improve regularity of
\vec{\eta}
, via a Moser type iteration argument, eventually obtaining that
\vec{\eta}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
.
Hence we may conclude that
\nabla_{x}u=\vec{V}+\vec{\eta}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. In particular this implies that

\tilde{N}(\nabla_{x}u)
is
L^{p}
integrable on bounded subsets of
\partial\Omega
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
.

Consider a ball
B(0,R)
large enough such that the operator
B
equals
I
outside this set. It follows that on the set
\Omega\setminus B(0,R)
the PDE for
\vec{\eta}
is just the heat equation

\Delta\vec{\eta}-\partial_{t}\vec{\eta}=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\vec{\eta}\Big|_{\partial\Omega\cap B(0,R)^{c}}=0.

Hence on this set components of
\vec{\eta}
are comparable with the Green’s function for the heat equation in the upper half-space. We pick the pole
(Y,s)
with time
s

in the past of the ball
B(0,R)
. We can find a large constant
M>0
for which
0\leq|\vec{\eta}|\leq MG

on
\Omega\cap\partial B(0,R)
and hence by the comparison principle

0<|\vec{\eta}|(x,x_{n},t)\leq MG((x,x_{n},t),(Y,s)),\qquad\mbox{for all }(x,x_{n},t)\notin B(0,R).

with
G
being the Green’s function of the whole space given by (
3.7
). By (
3.8
),

G
has sufficient decay - exponential in spatial variables, and polynomial in
t
of order at least
C(2R+t)^{-1}
- so
which is
L^{p}
integrable, for
p>1
on an interval
(-R,\infty)
,
we conclude that
\tilde{N}(\vec{\eta})
will be integrable in
L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
for all
p>1
.
Thus we have established the required a priori bound for
\tilde{N}(\nabla_{x}u)
.

It remains to establish a bound for
\tilde{N}(\partial_{n}u)
.
The PDE satisfied by
w_{n}
is (using that
B
is in block form)

(4.7)

-\partial_{t}w_{n}+\divg(B\nabla w_{n})=-\divg_{x}\left((\partial_{n}B_{||})\nabla_{x}u\right):=\mathrm{div}_{x}\vec{F}.

Given what we know about
\nabla_{x}u
, the righthand side of the PDE has the form
\mathrm{div}_{x}\vec{F}
for a bounded function
\vec{F}
with bounded support, and hence such
\vec{F}\in L^{q}(\Omega)
for all
q>n
. It follows that
we can write
w_{n}
as
w^{0}+\widetilde{w_{n}}
, where
w^{0}
solves the above PDE with zero boundary data
and (by results of, say,
[
32
, Theorem 8.17]
) is therefore bounded on
\Omega
. The other term,
\widetilde{w_{n}}
,
solves the homogeneous PDE
\mathcal{L}\widetilde{w_{n}}=0
with boundary datum
w_{n}\big|_{\partial\Omega}
.
If
w_{n}\big|_{\partial\Omega}\in L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
, then by results of Appendix
A
we have the required nontangential bounds for it. For
w^{0}
the rest of the argument continues as above for
\vec{\eta}
, since once again outside a bounded set this function is comparable to the Green’s function of the heat equation.

Hence it remains to establish that
w_{n}\big|_{\partial\Omega}\in L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
. The argument is similar to that given in (
10.25
),
but is a bit easier since (
10.25
) is a local estimate and in this calculation we need to take
\theta\hbar=0
and
c=0
. It follows that for any
h>0
we have:

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(h):

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w_{n}^{2}(x,0,t)\,dx\,dt-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w_{n}^{2}(x,h,t)\,dx\,dt+h\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\partial_{x_{n}}w_{n}^{2})(x,h,t)\,dx\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,h)\times\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{x_{n}}w_{n}|^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt+2\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,h)\times\mathbb{R}}w_{n}\partial^{2}_{x_{n}x_{n}}w_{n}\,x_{n}\,dX\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,h)\times\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{x_{n}}w_{n}|^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt-2\sum_{i,j<n}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,h)\times\mathbb{R}}w_{n}\partial_{i}(b_{ij}\partial_{j}w_{n})\,x_{n}\,dX\,dt

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle 2\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,h)\times\mathbb{R}}w_{n}\,\mathrm{div}_{x}\vec{F}\,x_{n}\,dX\,dt-\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,h)\times\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}(w_{n}^{2})\,dX\,dt.

Here we have used (
4.7
). Integration by parts,
together with the properties of
\vec{F}
, yields

(4.9)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{A}(h)|\lesssim\|S(\nabla_{x}u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,h)\times\mathbb{R}}|\partial^{2}_{x_{n}x_{n}}u|^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt.

Hence by (
8.7
) the first term of the righthand side of (
4.9
) is finite because we know that
\|N(\nabla_{x}u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}<\infty
.
For the second term, we make use of the PDE that
u
satisfies. This yields another term,
\|S(\nabla_{x}u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
, that we have already handled, as well as the term
\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,h)\times\mathbb{R}}|\nabla_{x}u|^{2}x_{n}

which is bounded by
h^{2}\|N(\nabla_{x}u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

and lastly the term

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,h)\times\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{t}u|^{2}x_{n}
, which we consider now. Setting
v=\partial_{t}u
,
differentiating the PDE for
u
gives

(4.10)

-\partial_{t}v+\divg(B\nabla v)=-\divg_{x}\left((\partial_{t}B_{||})\nabla_{x}u\right):=\mathrm{div}_{x}\vec{F}^{1},

with
v\big|_{\partial\Omega}=\partial_{t}f
, where
f
has bounded derivatives (including the
t
-variable) and
\partial_{t}f

is compactly supported. As with
w_{n}
the solution
v
can be written as
v^{0}+\tilde{v}
with
v^{0}
solving the inhomogeneous problem with zero boundary data and
\tilde{v}
solving
\mathcal{L}\tilde{v}=0
with boundary datum
\partial_{t}f
. Again, Appendix
A
applies to
\tilde{v}
since, by our assumptions on
f
,
\partial_{t}f\in L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
), and so
\|N(\tilde{v})\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}
is finite.
The same argument for
w^{0}
applies to
v^{0}
since the functions
\vec{F}
and
\vec{F}^{1}
have similar properites. Thus,

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,h)\times\mathbb{R}}|\partial_{t}u|^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt\leq h^{2}\left[\|N(v^{0})\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}+\|N(\tilde{v})\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}\right]<\infty.

Hence, by combining all terms of (
4.9
) we have proven that
|\mathcal{A}(h)|\leq C(1+h^{2})
. Fix now
H>0
. We average
\mathcal{A}(h)
over the interval
(O,H)
and see that:

H^{-1}\int_{0}^{H}\mathcal{A}(h)dh=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w_{n}^{2}(x,0,t)\,dx\,dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w_{n}^{2}(x,H,t)\,dx\,dt-H^{-1}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,H)\times\mathbb{R}}w_{n}^{2}\,dX\,dt.

Combining this with our earlier estimate we see that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}w_{n}^{2}(x,0,t)\,dx\,dt\leq C+CH^{2}+H^{-1}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,H)\times\mathbb{R}}w_{n}^{2}\,dX\,dt\leq C+\iint_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\,dX\,dt,

when
H=1
. Finally, recall that
u
is the energy solution, and therefore
\iint_{\Omega}|\nabla u|^{2}\,dX\,dt<\infty
, so that

w_{n}\in L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
. This completes the proof.∎

In the remainder of the paper we establish the results we have used in this section, starting with Theorem
5.1
.

5.
Solvability of the Regularity problem - reduction to the block form case

The following section establishes a result that reduces the question of solvability of the Regularity problem for parabolic PDEs with coefficients satisfying a large Carleson condition to solvability of the same problem for elliptic
matrices of special block form (see below).

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=\mathrm{div}(A\nabla\cdot)-\partial_{t}
be an operator in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
where matrix
A
is uniformly elliptic, with bounded real coefficients such both (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) hold.
Suppose that for all
1<p<\infty
the
L^{p}
, the Regularity problem for the block form operator

(5.2)

{\mathcal{L}}_{0}u=-\partial_{t}u+\mbox{\rm div}_{\parallel}(A_{\parallel}\nabla_{\parallel}u)+\partial^{2}_{nn}u,

is solvable in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. Here
A_{\parallel}
is the matrix
(a_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n-1}
.
In addition, assume that for any
p\in(1,\infty)
, any
f\in\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})
, the energy solution
\widetilde{u}
to
\mathcal{L}_{0}\widetilde{u}=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
with
\widetilde{u}=f
on
\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
satisfies

(5.3)

\left\|S(\nabla\widetilde{u})\right\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}}.

Then we have the following: For any
1<q<\infty
the
L^{q}
Regularity problem for the operator
\mathcal{L}
is solvable in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
if and only if the
L^{q^{\prime}}
Dirichlet problem for the adjoint operator
\mathcal{L}^{*}
is solvable in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

We start by adapting useful results from
[
42
]
to parabolic settings. Let us denote by
\tilde{N}_{1,\varepsilon}
the
L^{1}
-averaged version of the nontangential maximal function for
doubly truncated
parabolic cones. That is, for
\vec{u}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we set

\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q):=\Gamma(Q)\cap\{(X,t):\varepsilon<\delta(X,t)<1/\varepsilon\}
,
and

\tilde{N}_{1,\varepsilon}(\vec{u})(Q)=\sup_{(X,t)\in\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{(Z,\tau)\in B_{\delta(X,t)/4}(X,t)}|\vec{u}(Z,\tau)|dZd\tau.

Lemma 2.8 of
[
42
]
, stated below, provides a way to estimate the
L^{q}
norms of
\tilde{N}_{1,\varepsilon}(\nabla F)(Q)
via duality (based on tent-spaces). We state these results in the following subsection.

5.1.
Test function
\vec{h}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

There exist
\vec{\alpha}(X,Z)
with
\vec{\alpha}(X,\cdot):B(X,\delta(X)/2)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and

\|\vec{\alpha}(X,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(B(X,\delta(X)/2))}=1
, a nonnegative scalar function
\beta(X,Q)\in L^{1}(\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q))
with

\int_{\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q)}\beta(X,Q)\,dX=1
and a nonnegative
g\in L^{q^{\prime}}(\partial{(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})},d\sigma)
with
\|g\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}=1
such that

(5.5)

\left\|\tilde{N}_{1,\varepsilon}(\nabla F)\right\|_{L^{q}(\partial{(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})},d\sigma)}\lesssim\iint_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\nabla F(Z)\cdot\vec{h}(Z)\,dZ,

where

\vec{h}(Z)=\int_{\partial{(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}}\iint_{\Gamma(Q)}g(Q)\vec{\alpha}(X,Z)\beta(X,Q)\frac{\chi_{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}(Z)}{\delta(X)^{n+2}}\,dX\,dQ,

and
\chi_{A}
is the characteristic function of the set
A
.

Moreover, for any
G:{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\tilde{N}_{1}(\nabla G)
we also have an upper bound

(5.6)

\left|\iint_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\nabla G(Z)\cdot\vec{h}(Z)\,dZ\right|\lesssim\left\|\tilde{N}_{1}(\nabla G)\right\|_{L^{q}(\partial({\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}),d\sigma)},

where
\widetilde{N}_{1}
is the modified nontangential maximal function with
p=1
in (
2.21
).
The implied constants in (
5.5
)-(
5.6
) do not depend on
\varepsilon
, only on the dimension
n
.

The function
\vec{h}
defined above works perfectly for the purposes outlined in the Lemma, but unfortunately has one flaw which will become important later. Namely, at one point we will need to differentiate
\vec{h}
(since the derivatives on
\vec{h}
will not be removable by the usual methods such as integration by parts). Thus, we claim that under certain mild assumptions we can improve this function and make it differentiable with
|\nabla\vec{h}(Z)|\lesssim\delta(Z)^{-1}{h_{1}}(Z)
, where
{h_{1}}
is a function of same type as
\vec{h}
.

Proposition 5.7
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
.
Assume that
\vec{u}
is a real vector (or scalar) valued function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
and that the interior Hölder continuity condition holds. That is for some
C>0
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
we have for any interior ball
B_{4r}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
the estimate:

(5.8)

\sup_{Z,Z^{\prime}\in B_{r}}|\vec{u}(Z)-\vec{u}(Z^{\prime})|\leq C\left(\frac{\|Z-Z^{\prime}\|}{r}\right)^{\alpha}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{2r}}|\vec{u}|.

Here
\|\cdot\|
is as in the Definition
2.1
.
Fix a non-negative function
\eta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
supported in
B(0,1)
with
\iint\eta=1
.
Then there exists
\rho=\rho(n,\alpha,C)\in(0,1/4)
(independent of
\vec{u}
),

\vec{\alpha}(\cdot)
with
|\vec{\alpha}(X)|\leq 1
, a nonnegative scalar function
\beta(X,Q)\in L^{1}(\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q))
with
\int_{\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q)}\beta(X,Q)\,dX=1
,
a mapping
Y:\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
,
X\mapsto Y(X)\in B(X,\delta(X)/4)
where
B(X,R)
is a parabolic ball centered at
X
with “radius”
R
,
and a nonnegative
g\in L^{q^{\prime}}(\partial{(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})})
with
\|g\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}=1
such that
for

(5.9)

\vec{h}(Z)=\int_{\partial{(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}}\iint_{\Gamma(Q)}g(Q)\vec{\alpha}(X)\beta(X,Q)\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}(Z-Y(X))\,dX\,dQ,

we have that

(5.10)

\left\|\tilde{N}_{1,\varepsilon}(\vec{u})\right\|_{L^{q}(\partial{(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})},d\sigma)}\approx\iint_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\vec{u}(Z)\cdot\vec{h}(Z)\,dZ,

where (
5.10
) holds with the implied constant independent of
\varepsilon>0
and
\vec{u}
. Here
\eta_{\delta}
denotes the parabolic rescaling of the function
\eta
defined by

\eta_{\delta}(Z)=\eta_{\delta}(z,\tau)=\delta^{-n-2}\eta(z/\delta,t/\delta^{2}).

Moreover, for any
\vec{G}:{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\tilde{N}_{1}(\vec{G})
we also have that the upper bound

(5.11)

\left|\iint_{{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\vec{G}(Z)\cdot\vec{h}(Z)\,dZ\right|\lesssim\left\|\tilde{N}_{1}(\vec{G})\right\|_{L^{q}(\partial({\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}),d\sigma)}.

This estimate holds even if we drop the assumption that
\eta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is non-negative, as the assumed non-negativity plays no role in the upper bound.

Proof.

Clearly, (
5.11
) must hold as the comparison between
\vec{h}
in Lemma
5.4
and (
5.9
) reveals that the only difference between these functions is that
\vec{\alpha}
not does not depend on
Z
and we have replaced the function
\frac{\chi_{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}(Z)}{\delta(X)^{n+2}}
by its smoothened-out version
\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}
shifted to the point
Y(X)
(and thus supported in
B(Y(X),\rho\delta(X))
). Hence we have not changed the nature of our function in any way. For the upper bound we also can take absolute values inside the integral. Therefore,

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}|\vec{G}(Z)||\vec{h}(Z)|\,dZ\leq

\int_{\partial{(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}}g(Q)\iint_{\Gamma(Q)}|\beta(X,Q)|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}|\vec{\alpha}(X)||\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}(Z-Y(X))||\vec{G}(Z)|\,dZ\,dX\,dQ.

As
\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}
is supported in
B(Y(X),\rho\delta(X))\subset B(X,\delta(X)/4)
and

|\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}|\leq\sup|\eta|(\rho\delta(X))^{-n-2}
the innermost integral has an upper bound:

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}|\vec{\alpha}(X)||\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}(Z-Y(X))||\vec{G}(Z)|\,dZ\leq\sup|\eta|\omega_{n}^{-1}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(Y(X),\rho\delta(X))}|\vec{G}|

\leq\sup|\eta|\omega_{n}^{-1}C_{n}^{-\log_{2}(\rho/4)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}|\vec{G}|\leq\sup|\eta|\omega_{n}^{-1}C_{n}^{-\log_{2}(\rho/4)}\tilde{N}_{1}(\vec{G})(Q).

Here
\omega_{n}
is the volume of the
n
-dimensional unit ball,
C_{n}
is the doubling constant and
X
is an arbitrary point in
\Gamma(Q)
. As a result, the second innermost integral has an upper bound of:

\sup|\eta|\omega_{n}^{-1}C_{n}^{-\log_{2}(\rho/4)}\tilde{N}_{1}(\vec{G})(Q)\iint_{\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q)}|\beta(X,Q)|\,dX=\sup|\eta|\omega_{n}^{-1}C_{n}^{-\log_{2}(\rho/4)}\tilde{N}_{1}(\vec{G})(Q).

Finally, it follows that the outside integral enjoys, by Hölder’s inequality, an upper bound of

\sup|\eta|\omega_{n}^{-1}C_{n}^{-\log_{2}(\rho/4)}\|\tilde{N}_{1}(\vec{G})\|_{L^{q}}\|g\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}.

From this the conclusion follows since
\|g\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}=1
. Observe that the constant in our estimate only depends on the dimension
n
,
\rho>0
and
\sup|\eta|
which is finite as
\eta
is a compact
C^{\infty}
function. We have not used any other property of
\eta
apart from its boundedness and the fact that it is supported in the unit ball.

We now focus on (
5.10
).
Assume for now that
u
is a scalar function in
L^{1}_{loc}
. We will explain the modification needed for our argument to apply in the vector valued case later. Let us define the two versions of average functions

(5.12)

W(X)=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{Z\in B(X,\delta(X)/2)}|{u}|dZ,\quad w(X)=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{Z\in B(X,\delta(X)/8)}|{u}|dZ.

Let
C_{n}
be a doubling constant on the space
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
, that is we have for any ball

|B_{2r}|\leq C_{n}|B_{r}|.

Clearly,

w(X)=|B_{\delta(X)/8}|^{-1}\iint_{B(X,\delta(X)/8)}|u|\leq(C_{n})^{2}|B_{\delta(X)/2}|^{-1}\iint_{B(X,\delta(X)/2)}|u|=(C_{n})^{2}W(X).

What about the reverse inequality? With
N
defined by

N(v)(Q)=\sup_{X\in\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q)}|v(X)|,

it follows by the level-set argument that for all
1\leq q<\infty
we have

\|N(w)\|_{L^{q}}\approx\|N(W)\|_{L^{q}},

where the implied constants are independent of the function
u
and of
\epsilon>0
. Hence, let
K
be a universal constant
such that

\|N(W)\|_{L^{q}}\leq K\|N(w)\|_{L^{q}}.

Fix
\gamma>0
(to be determined later) and define the sets

B_{good}=\{Q\in\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}):\,N(W)(Q)\leq\gamma N(w)(Q)\},\qquad B_{bad}=\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})\setminus B_{good}.

We have that

\int_{B_{bad}}N(w)^{q}\leq\gamma^{-q}\int_{B_{bad}}N(W)^{q}.

Therefore if

\int_{B_{bad}}N(w)^{q}>\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}N(w)^{q},

it would follow that

\int_{\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}N(w)^{q}<2\int_{B_{bad}}N(w)^{q}\leq 2\gamma^{-q}\int_{B_{bad}}N(W)^{q}\leq 2\gamma^{-q}K^{q}\int_{\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}N(w)^{q},

which is a contradiction if we choose
\gamma>0
such that
2\gamma^{-q}K^{q}=1
. Let us make such choice of
\gamma
. Notice that this choice is independent of
u
and
\varepsilon
.

With
\gamma
chosen as above, we must therefore have

\|N(w)\|_{L^{q}(\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}))}\leq 2^{-q}\|N(w)\|_{L^{q}(B_{good})}.

We make our choice of the function
g
. We pick
g
supported on the set
B_{good}
such that
\|g\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}=1
and

\|N(w)\|_{L^{q}(B_{good})}=\int_{\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}N(w)g.

Clearly we can pick
g
nonnegative (since
N(w)\geq 0
) and we also have

\|N(W)\|_{L^{q}(\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}))}\leq K\|N(w)\|_{L^{q}(\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}))}\leq 2^{-q}K\int_{\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}N(w)g.

Fix now
Q\in\mbox{supp }g
. We have for such
Q
the two inequalities:

\gamma^{-1}N(W)(Q)\leq N(w)(Q)\leq(C_{n})^{2}N(W)(Q)

and hence the qualities
N(w)(Q)
and
N(W)(Q)
are comparable. By the definition of
N
there exists a point

X\in\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q)
such that

w(X)\geq\frac{1}{2}N(w)(Q)\geq(2\gamma)^{-1}N(W)(Q).

For such point
X
consider the ball used to define
w(X)
, i.e.
B(X,\delta(X)/8)
. Simple geometric
considerations imply that for all points

Z\in B(X,\delta(X)/16)\cap\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q)\quad\mbox{ we have that }\quad B(X,\delta(X)/8)\subset B(Z,\delta(Z)/4)

and therefore

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(Z,\delta(Z)/4)}|{u}|\geq C_{n}^{-1}w(X)\geq(2C_{n}\gamma)^{-1}N(W)(Q),

and trivially
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(Z,\delta(Z)/4)}|{u}|\leq C_{n}N(W)(Q)
. It follows that
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(Z,\delta(Z)/4)}|{u}|\approx N(W)(Q)
.
Given the geometry of the set
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q)
, clearly
B(X,\delta(X)/16)\cap\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q)

is a set of positive measure. We now choose the function
\beta(\cdot,Q)
to be

\beta(\cdot,Q)=\frac{1}{|B(X,\delta(X)/16)\cap\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q)|}\chi_{B(X,\delta(X)/16)\cap\Gamma_{\varepsilon}(Q)}.

Observe that it follows that such
\beta
satisfies all properties stated in our Proposition. To make our choice complete for
Q\in B_{bad}
(and thus not in the support of
g
), we may pick
\beta
completely arbitrarily.

In summary, we have so far defined
g
and
\beta
such that whenever
Q\in\mbox{supp }g
and
X\in\mbox{supp }\beta(\cdot,Q)
we must have that

(2C_{n}\gamma)^{-1}N(W)(Q)\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}|{u}|,

and therefore also

(5.13)

(2C_{n}\gamma)^{-1}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(X,\delta(X)/2)}|{u}|\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}|{u}|.

Up to this point there is no difference in the argument for
u
that is vector valued.

Because of the choices we have made above, we only care about the points
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
for which (
5.13
) holds. Thus for points
X
where (
5.13
) does not hold we simply choose

Y(X)=X
and
\vec{\alpha}(X)=0
. Observe that this means that

\vec{\alpha}(X)\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}(\cdot-Y(X))=0,

and thus there is no contribution from this term in the definition of
\vec{h}
.

It remains to define
Y
and
\alpha
when (
5.13
) holds for a point
X
. We start with scalar
u
.
We apply our assumption of Hölder regularity of
u
. As follows from (
5.8
) and (
5.13
) that on the ball
B(X,\delta(X)/4)
we have

(5.14)

\sup_{Z,Z^{\prime}\in B(X,\delta(X)/4)}|{u}(Z)-{u}(Z^{\prime})|\leq C^{\prime}\left(\frac{\|Z-Z^{\prime}\|}{\delta(X)}\right)^{\alpha}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(X,\delta(X)/2)}|{u}|

\leq 2C^{\prime}C_{n}\gamma\left(\frac{\|Z-Z^{\prime}\|}{\delta(X)}\right)^{\alpha}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}|{u}|\leq 2C^{\prime}C_{n}\gamma\left(\frac{\|Z-Z^{\prime}\|}{\delta(X)}\right)^{\alpha}\sup_{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}|u|.

Consider a point
Y^{\prime}\in\overline{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}
where
\sup_{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}|u|
is attained (possibly at the boundary of the ball). Applying (
5.14
) to this point we see that for all
Z\in B(Y^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}\delta(X))\cap B(X,\delta(X)/4)
we have that

|u(Z)-u(Y^{\prime})|\leq 2C^{\prime}C_{n}\gamma(\rho^{\prime})^{\alpha}|u(Y^{\prime})|.

We choose
\rho^{\prime}
so that
2C^{\prime}C_{n}\gamma(\rho^{\prime})^{\alpha}=1/2
which ensures that

|u(Z)-u(Y^{\prime})|\leq\frac{1}{2}|u(Y^{\prime})|,\qquad\mbox{for all }Z\in B(Y^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}\delta(X))\cap B(X,\delta(X)/4).

As
u
is scalar, this inequality ensures that
u(Z)
has the same sign as
u(Y^{\prime})
.
Let
\alpha(X)=1
when
u(Y^{\prime})>0
and
\alpha(X)=-1
otherwise, that is,
\alpha(X)=\sgn u(Y^{\prime})
.
The geometry of the situation implies that there exits some
\rho=\rho(\rho^{\prime},n)>0
(the worst-case is when
Y^{\prime}
is on the boundary of
B(X,\delta(X)/4)
) such that for some point

Y\in B(Y^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}\delta(X))\cap B(X,\rho(X)/4)
we have that

B(Y,\rho\delta(X))\subset B(Y^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}\delta(X))\cap B(X,\delta(X)/4).

This point
Y
is the point we set to be the outcome of the mapping
Y(X)
and the
\rho
we have found above is the one used to define
\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}
.

We note that for all
Z\in B(Y,\rho\delta(X))
the scalar function
u(Z)
has the same sign and thus

u(Z)\alpha(X)>0
. Furthermore since
|u(Z)|\geq\frac{1}{2}|u(Y^{\prime})|
and the function

\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}(\cdot-Y(X)))
is supported in the ball
B(Y,\rho\delta(X))
and has integral equal to
1
, we have that

|u(Y^{\prime})|\geq\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}u(Z)\alpha(X)\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}(Z-Y(X)))\,dZ\geq\frac{1}{2}|u(Y^{\prime})|=\frac{1}{2}\sup_{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}|u|.

Since also

\sup_{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}|u|\leq 2\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(Y,\rho\delta(X))}|{u}|\leq 2C_{n}^{-\log_{2}(\rho/4)}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}|{u}|,

this is only possible if

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}u(Z)\alpha(X)\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}(Z-Y(X)))\,dZ\approx\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}|{u}|.

Thus integrating this portion of the function
h
against
u
captures a good portion of the average of
|u|
on
B(X,\delta(X)/4)
.

There are minor differences in the definition if
\vec{u}
is a vector.
Suppose that
\vec{u}
has
k
components. We choose
Y^{\prime}
in the way same as above. By (
5.14
) there will be
j\in\{1,2,\dots,k\}
such that

|{u_{j}}(Z)-{u_{j}}(Y^{\prime})|\leq|\vec{u}(Z)-\vec{u}(Y^{\prime})|\leq 2C^{\prime}C_{n}k^{-1/2}\gamma(\rho^{\prime})^{\alpha}|u_{j}(Y^{\prime})|,

since, for at least one
j
,
|u_{j}(Y^{\prime})|\geq\sqrt{\frac{1}{k}}|u(Y^{\prime})|
. We choose
\alpha_{j}(X)=\mbox{sgn }u_{j}(Y^{\prime})
and

\alpha_{i}(X)=0
for
i\neq j
. The rest of the argument follows as above replacing
u
by
u_{j}
which is a scalar function. We choose
\rho^{\prime}>0
such that
2C^{\prime}C_{n}k^{-1/2}\gamma(\rho^{\prime})^{\alpha}=1/2
, i.e., now
\rho^{\prime}
also depends on
k
- the dimension of our vector.
We again obtain the desired conclusion that

(5.15)

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}\vec{u}(Z)\vec{\alpha}(X)\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}(\cdot-Y(X)))\approx\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B(X,\delta(X)/4)}|{\vec{u}}|.

In summary, we have chosen
g
such that

\|N_{1,\varepsilon}(\vec{u})\|_{L^{q}(\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}))}\approx\int_{\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}N(w)g

and for any
Q\in\mbox{supp }g
we have that for all
X\in\mbox{supp }\beta(\cdot,Q)
(
5.15
) holds.
Thus the function
\vec{h}
integrated against
\vec{u}
picks up a sufficiently large portion of the
L^{q}
norm of

\|N_{1,\varepsilon}(\vec{u})\|_{L^{q}}
.
∎

Remark on differentiability of
\vec{h}
:
Given the definition of
\vec{h}
, if we take a partial (spatial derivative
\partial_{i}
) of this function we see that

\partial_{i}[\vec{\alpha}(X)\beta(X,Q)\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}(\cdot-Y(X))]=\vec{\alpha}(X)\beta(X,Q)\partial_{i}[\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}(\cdot-Y(X))],

since the derivative only acts on the smooth function. Given the scaling we have applied to
\eta
,
differentiating in Z gives

\partial_{i}[\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}(Z-Y(X))]=(\rho\delta(X))^{-n-3}\partial_{i}\eta((z-y(X))/(\rho\delta(X)),(t-\tau(X))/(\rho\delta(X))^{2}).

Recall that the point
Z\in B(X,\delta(X)/4)
and hence
\delta(Z)\approx\delta(X)
, implying that

\left|\partial_{i}[\vec{\alpha}(X)\beta(X,Q)\eta_{\rho\delta(X)}(\cdot-Y(X))]\right|\lesssim\delta(Z)^{-1}|\vec{\alpha}(X)|\beta(X,Q)(|\partial_{i}\eta|)_{\rho\delta(X)}(\cdot-Y(X)),

which is a function of the same type as
\vec{h}
which we shall call
{h_{1}}
.
After integrating, we conclude that

(5.16)

|\partial_{i}\vec{h}(Z)|\lesssim\delta^{-1}(Z){h_{1}}(Z),\qquad\forall Z,

and similarly for the time derivative

(5.17)

|\partial_{t}\vec{h}(Z)|\lesssim\delta^{-2}(Z){h_{2}}(Z),\qquad\forall Z.

Here both
{h_{1}}
and
{h_{2}}
enjoy the estimates (
5.11
), as they are of the same type as
\vec{h}
. Similar estimates hold for higher order derivatives with each spatial derivative bringing an extra
\delta^{-1}(Z)
and the time derivative

\delta^{-2}(Z)
.

Let
u
be the solution of the following boundary value problem

(5.18)

\mathcal{L}u=-\partial_{t}u+\mathrm{div}(A\nabla u)=0\mbox{ in }{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+},\qquad u\Big|_{\partial{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}=f,

We find
\vec{h}
as in Proposition
5.7
for the vector
\nabla u
. The reason we can apply this result to
\nabla u
is that under our assumption
x_{n}|\nabla A|+x_{n}^{2}\left|\partial_{t}A\right|\lesssim 1
the gradient of
u
does enjoy interior Hölder estimates (
[
44
]
), and given the scaling
|\nabla A|\lesssim x_{n}^{-1}
the constant in the condition (
5.8
) does not depend on the size of the ball
B_{r}
- the condition
B_{4r}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
ensures we stay in the interior of the domain of at least distance
r
away from the boundary.

5.2.
The adjoint inhomogenous Dirichlet problem

We let
v:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be the solution of the inhomogenous Dirichlet problem for the operator
{\mathcal{L}}^{*}
(adjoint to
\mathcal{L}
) which is a backward in time parabolic operator:

(5.19)

{\mathcal{L}}^{*}v=\partial_{t}v+\mathrm{div}(A^{*}\nabla v)=\mathrm{div}(\vec{h})\mbox{ in }{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R},\qquad v\Big|_{\partial({\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}=0.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Our aim is to estimate
N_{1,\varepsilon}(\nabla u)
in
L^{q}
using Proposition
5.9
. Then since
\vec{h}\big|_{\partial({{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}})}=0
and
\vec{h}
vanishes as
x_{n}\to\infty
, we have by integration by parts

(5.20)

\|\widetilde{N}_{1,\varepsilon}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{q}}\lesssim\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\nabla u\cdot\vec{h}\,dZ=-\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}u\,\mathrm{div}\vec{h}\,dZ=-\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}u\,{\mathcal{L}}^{*}v\,dZ

We now move
u
inside the divergence operator and apply the divergence theorem.

\mbox{RHS of \eqref{e6}}=-\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\mathrm{div}(uA^{*}\nabla v)\,dZ+\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}A\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\,dZ+\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}(\partial_{t}u)v\,dZ

=\int_{\partial{{(\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}}u(\cdot,0,\cdot)a^{*}_{nj}\partial_{j}v\,d\sigma.

since

\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}A\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\,dZ+\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}(\partial_{t}u)v\,dZ=-\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{L}u\,v\,dZ=0.

Here there is no boundary integral since
v
vanishes on the boundary of
{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}
. It follows that

(5.21)

\|N_{1,\varepsilon}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{q}}\lesssim\int_{\partial{{(\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}}u(\cdot,0,\cdot)a^{*}_{nj}\partial_{j}v\,d\sigma,

where the implied constant in (
5.21
) is independent of
\varepsilon>0
. Now, we use the fundamental theorem of calculus and the decay of
\nabla v
at infinity to write (
5.21
) as

(5.22)

\|N_{1,\varepsilon}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{q}}\lesssim-\int_{\partial({{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R})}u(x,0,t)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d}{ds}\left(a^{*}_{nj}(x,s,t)\partial_{j}v(x,s,t)\right)ds\right)dx\,dt.

Recall that
\mathrm{div}(A^{*}\nabla v)=\mathrm{div}(\vec{h})-\partial_{t}v
and hence the righthand side of (
5.22
) equals

(5.23)

\int_{\partial{{(\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R})}u(x,0,t)\Bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big[\sum_{i<n}\partial_{i}(a^{*}_{ij}(x,s,t)\partial_{j}v(x,s,t))-\mathrm{div}\vec{h}(x,s,t)

+\partial_{t}v(x,s,t)\Big]ds\Bigg)dx\,dt.

We integrate by parts moving
\partial_{i}
for
i<n
onto
u(\cdot,0,\cdot)
. We also peel off half-derivative from
\partial_{t}v
onto
u
.

The integral term containing
\partial_{n}h_{n}(x,s,t)
does not need to be considered as it equals to zero by the fundamental theorem of calculus since
\vec{h}(\cdot,0,\cdot)=\vec{0}
and

\vec{h}(\cdot,s,\cdot)\to\vec{0}
as
s\to\infty
.
It follows that

(5.24)

\displaystyle\|N_{1,\varepsilon}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{\partial({{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R})}\nabla_{\parallel}f(x,t)\cdot\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\vec{h}_{\parallel}(x,s,t)-(A^{*}\nabla v)_{\parallel}(x,s,t)\right]ds\right)dx\,dt

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int_{\partial({{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R})}D^{1/2}_{t}f(x,t)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}v(x,s,t)\,ds\right)dx\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I+II+III.

Here
I
is the term containing
\vec{h}_{\parallel}
and
II
contains
(A^{*}\nabla v)_{\parallel}
and
III
term
H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}v
. Here
H_{t}
is the Hilbert transform in the
t
-variable.
The notation we are using here is that, for a vector
\vec{w}=(w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{n})
, the vector
\vec{w}_{\parallel}
denotes the first
n-1
components of
\vec{w}
, that is
(w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{n-1})
, and
\nabla_{||}=\nabla_{x}
.

As shall see below we do not need worry about term
I
. This is because what we are going to do next is essentially undo the integration by parts we have done above but we swap the function
u
with another (better behaving) function
\tilde{u}
with the same boundary data. Doing this we eventually arrive at
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}
plus some error terms (solid integrals) that arise from the fact that
u
and
\tilde{u}
disagree inside the domain. This explains why we get the same boundary integral as
I
but with the opposite sign, as this “reverse process” will undo and eliminate this boundary term.

We solve a new auxiliary PDE problem to define
\tilde{u}
. Let
\tilde{u}
be the solution
of the following boundary value problem for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{0}
whose matrix
A_{0}
has the block-form

A_{0}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}A_{\parallel}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&1\\
\end{array}\right]
and

(5.25)

{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\tilde{u}=-\partial_{t}\tilde{u}+\mathrm{div}(A_{0}\nabla\tilde{u})=0\mbox{ in }\Omega,\qquad\tilde{u}\Big|_{\partial\Omega}=f.

Recall that we have assumed that the
L^{q}
Regularity problem for the operator

{L_{0}}
is solvable. We look the terms
II
and
III
.
Let

(5.26)

\vec{V}(x,x_{n},t)=-\int_{x_{n}}^{\infty}(A^{*}\nabla v)_{\parallel}(x,s,t)ds,\quad{W}(x,x_{n},t)=-\int_{x_{n}}^{\infty}H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}v(x,s,t)ds.

It follows that by the fundamental theorem of calculus and the observation that
\widetilde{u}=u
on
\partial\Omega
, where
\widetilde{u}
is same as in (
5.25
),

(5.27)

II=\int_{\partial{{(\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}}\nabla_{\parallel}\widetilde{u}(x,0,t)\cdot\vec{V}(x,0,t)dx\,dt\\
=-\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}\partial_{n}\left(\nabla_{||}\widetilde{u}(x,x_{n},t)\cdot\vec{V}(x,x_{n},t)\right)\partial_{n}(x_{n})dxdx_{n}dt\\
=\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial^{2}_{nn}\left[\nabla_{\parallel}\tilde{u}(x,x_{n},t)\cdot\vec{V}(x,x_{n},t)\right]x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt,

and therefore,

(5.28)

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial^{2}_{nn}(\nabla_{\parallel}\tilde{u})\cdot\vec{V}x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+2\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial_{n}(\nabla_{\parallel}\tilde{u})\cdot\partial_{n}(\vec{V})x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+

\displaystyle+\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\nabla_{\parallel}\tilde{u}\cdot\partial^{2}_{nn}(\vec{V})x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt=II_{1}+II_{2}+II_{3}.

Since
\partial_{n}\vec{V}(x,x_{n},t)=(A^{*}\nabla v)_{\parallel}
the term
II_{2}
is easiest to handle and can be estimated as a product of two square functions

(5.29)

|II_{2}|\leq C\int_{\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}\iint_{\Gamma(y,s)}\left|\partial_{n}(\nabla_{\parallel}\tilde{u})\right|\left|\partial_{n}(\vec{V})\right|x_{n}^{-n}dxdx_{n}dtdyds\leq C\|S(\partial_{n}\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}\|S(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}.

Next we look at
II_{1}
. We integrate by parts moving
\nabla_{\parallel}
from
\tilde{u}
. This gives us

(5.30)

II_{1}=\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u}\cdot\left(\int_{x_{n}}^{\infty}\mathrm{div}_{\parallel}(A^{*}\nabla v)_{\parallel}ds\right)x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt.

Using the PDE
v
satisfies we get that

\int_{x_{n}}^{\infty}\mathrm{div}_{\parallel}(A^{*}\nabla v)_{\parallel}ds=(a_{nj}\partial_{j}v)(x,x_{n},t)+\int_{x_{n}}^{\infty}[\mathrm{div}\vec{h}-\partial_{t}v]\,ds.

Using this in (
5.30
) we see that

(5.31)

II_{1}=\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}(\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u})(a_{nj}\partial_{j}v)x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u}\cdot\left(\int_{x_{n}}^{\infty}[\mathrm{div}\vec{h}-\partial_{t}v]\,ds\right)x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt.

Here the first term enjoys the same estimate as
II_{2}
, namely (
5.29
). We work more with the second term which we call
II_{12}
. We integrate by parts in
\partial_{n}
.

\displaystyle II_{12}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}(\partial_{n}\tilde{u})[\mathrm{div}\vec{h}-\partial_{t}v]x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle\qquad-

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\partial_{n}\tilde{u}\cdot\left(\int_{x_{n}}^{\infty}[\mathrm{div}\vec{h}-\partial_{t}v]\,ds\right)\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}(\partial_{n}\tilde{u})[\mathrm{div}\vec{h}]x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt-\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}(\partial_{n}\tilde{u})\partial_{t}vx_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle\qquad-

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\partial_{n}\tilde{u}\cdot\left(\int_{x_{n}}^{\infty}[\mathrm{div}\vec{h}-\partial_{t}v]\,ds\right)\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle II_{121}+II_{122}-\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\partial_{n}\tilde{u}\cdot\left(\int_{x_{n}}^{\infty}[\mathrm{div}\vec{h}-\partial_{t}v]\,ds\right)\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt.

For the last term, we separate it into two integrals and apply integration by parts to the first integral to get that

-\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\partial_{n}\tilde{u}\cdot\left(\int_{x_{n}}^{\infty}[\mathrm{div}\vec{h}-\partial_{t}v]\,ds\right)\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt=\int_{\partial({\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R})}\tilde{u}(x,0,t)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{div}\vec{h}\right)dx\,dt\\
-\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\nabla\tilde{u}\cdot\vec{h}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial_{n}\tilde{u}\left(\int_{x_{n}}^{\infty}\partial_{t}v\,ds\right)\,\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt\\
=:\int_{\partial({\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R})}\tilde{u}(x,0,t)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{div}\vec{h}\right)dx\,dt+II_{123}+II_{124}.

Note that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\divg\vec{h}(x,s,t)ds=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\divg_{||}\vec{h}_{||}(x,s,t)+\partial_{s}\vec{h}(x,s,t)\right)ds=\int_{0}^{\infty}\divg_{||}\vec{h}_{||}(x,s,t)ds.

We integrate by parts again and get that

\int_{\partial({\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R})}\tilde{u}(x,0,t)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{div}\vec{h}\right)dx\,dt=-\int_{\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})}\nabla_{\parallel}\tilde{u}(x,0,t)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\vec{h}_{\parallel}\,ds\right)dx\,dt,

which is precisely the term
I
defined by (
5.24
) with an opposite sign. To summarize, we have obtained that

II_{12}=II_{121}+II_{122}-I+II_{123}+II_{124}.

We return to the term
II_{121}
later. As we will see below the term
II_{124}
appears again but with opposite sign and hence will cancel out. The term
II_{123}
thanks to (
5.6
) of Lemma
5.4
have the estimate:

|II_{123}|=\left|\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\nabla\tilde{u}\cdot\vec{h}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt\right|\leq\left\|\tilde{N}_{1}(\nabla\tilde{u})\right\|_{L^{q}(\partial({\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}),d\sigma)}.

We repeat this approach with the term
III
. By the same logic we have

\displaystyle III

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial^{2}_{nn}\left[D^{1/2}_{t}\tilde{u}(x,x_{n},t)W(x,x_{n},t)\right]x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial^{2}_{nn}(D^{1/2}_{t}\tilde{u})W\,x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+2\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial_{n}(D^{1/2}_{t}\tilde{u})\partial_{n}(W)x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+

\displaystyle+\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}D^{1/2}_{t}\tilde{u}\,\partial^{2}_{nn}(W)x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt=II_{122}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial_{n}(D^{1/2}_{t}\tilde{u})\left(\int_{x_{n}}^{\infty}H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}v\,ds\right)\,\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}D^{1/2}_{t}\tilde{u}\,\partial^{2}_{nn}(W)x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle II_{122}-\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial_{n}\tilde{u}\left(\int_{x_{n}}^{\infty}\partial_{t}v\,ds\right)\,\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}D^{1/2}_{t}\tilde{u}\,\partial^{2}_{nn}(W)x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle II_{122}-II_{124}+III_{1}.

Here
II_{122}
arises from the second term on the second line by shifting half-derivative from
\tilde{u}
.
Next we look at
II_{3}
and
III_{1}
together. We see that

\displaystyle II_{3}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle III_{1}=\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\nabla_{\parallel}\tilde{u}\cdot\,\partial_{n}(A^{*}\nabla v)_{\parallel}x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}D_{t}^{1/2}\tilde{u}\,\partial_{n}(H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}v)x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\nabla_{\parallel}\tilde{u}\cdot((\partial_{n}A^{*})\nabla v)_{\parallel}x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\nabla_{\parallel}\tilde{u}\cdot(A^{*}\nabla(\partial_{n}v))_{\parallel}x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

+\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}D_{t}^{1/2}\tilde{u}\,\partial_{n}(H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}v)x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt=IV_{1}+IV_{2}+IV_{3}.

In order to handle the term
IV_{1}
we will use the fact that the matrix
A
satisfies the Carleson measure condition, (
3.28
), and Hölder’s inequality to obtain

(5.32)

|IV_{1}|\lesssim\|S(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}\|N(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}.

For the term
IV_{2}
we separate the parallel and tangential parts of the gradient, to get

\displaystyle IV_{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle IV_{3}=\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\nabla_{\parallel}\tilde{u}\cdot(A_{\parallel}^{*}\nabla_{\parallel}(\partial_{n}v))x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\nabla_{\parallel}\tilde{u}\cdot(a_{in}^{*}\partial^{2}_{nn}v)_{i<n}x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}D_{t}^{1/2}\tilde{u}\,\partial_{n}(H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}v)x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt=-\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{div}_{\parallel}(A_{\parallel}\nabla\tilde{u})(\partial_{n}v)x_{n}dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}\tilde{u}(\partial_{n}v)x_{n}dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}\nabla_{\parallel}\tilde{u}\cdot(a_{in}^{*}\partial^{2}_{nn}v)_{i<n}x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}}(\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u})(\partial_{n}v)x_{n}dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+IV_{4}.

Here we have integrated the first and third term by parts and then used the equation that
\tilde{u}
satisfies.
The first term on the last line enjoys a square function estimate by
\|S(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}\|S(\partial_{n}\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}
.
For
IV_{4}

we write
\partial^{2}_{nn}v
as

\partial^{2}_{nn}v=\partial_{n}\left(\frac{a^{*}_{nn}}{a^{*}_{nn}}\partial_{n}v\right)=\frac{1}{a^{*}_{nn}}\partial_{n}(a^{*}_{nn}\partial_{n}v)-\frac{\partial_{n}a^{*}_{nn}}{a^{*}_{nn}}\partial_{n}v

=-\frac{1}{a^{*}_{nn}}\left[\mathrm{div}_{\parallel}(A^{*}_{\parallel}\nabla_{\parallel}v)+\sum_{i<n}\left[\partial_{i}(a^{*}_{in}\partial_{n}v)+\partial_{n}(a^{*}_{ni}\partial_{i}v)\right]+\partial_{n}(a^{*}_{nn})\partial_{n}v+\partial_{t}v-\mathrm{div}\vec{h}\right],

where the final line follows from the equation that
v
satisfies. It therefore follows that the term
IV_{4}
can be written as a sum of six terms (which we shall call
IV_{41},\dots,IV_{46}
).

Terms
IV_{41}
and
IV_{42}
are similar and we deal with then via integration by parts (in
\partial_{i}
,
i<n
):

(5.33)

|IV_{41}|+|IV_{42}|\leq C\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}|\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}||\nabla v|x_{n}+C\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}|\nabla A||\nabla\tilde{u}||\nabla v|x_{n}.

The first term of (
5.33
) can be seen to be a product of two square functions and hence by Hölder it has an estimate by
\|S(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}\|S(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}
. The second term of (
5.33
) is similar to the term
IV_{1}
with analogous estimate. For the third term
IV_{43}
we observe that
\partial_{n}(a^{*}_{ni}\partial_{i}v)=\partial_{i}(a^{*}_{ni}\partial_{n}v)+(\partial_{n}a^{*}_{ni})\partial_{i}v-(\partial_{i}a^{*}_{ni})\partial_{n}v
which implies that it again can be estimated by the right-hand side of (
5.33
).
The same is true for the term
IV_{44}
which has a bound by the second term on the right-hand side of (
5.33
).
It remains to consider the term
IV_{45}-IV_{46}
which are

(5.34)

IV_{45}+IV_{46}=\sum_{i<n}\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\frac{a_{ni}}{a_{nn}}(\partial_{i}\tilde{u})\,[-v_{t}+\mathrm{div}\vec{h}]\,x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt.

Notice the similarity of this term with
II_{121}
, hence the calculation below also applies to it. For the terms
containing
\mathrm{div}\vec{h}
, since
|\mathrm{div}\vec{h}|x_{n}\lesssim H
with
H
of the same type as
\vec{h}
we have that

\left|\sum_{i}\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\frac{a_{ni}}{a_{nn}}(\partial_{i}\tilde{u})\mathrm{div}\vec{h}\,x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt\right|\leq C\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}|\nabla\tilde{u}|H\lesssim\|N_{1}(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}},

by (
5.11
).

The remaining two terms to consider are the term
II_{122}
(twice) and the terms containing
\partial_{t}
in (
5.34
). Taken together these terms are equal to

(5.35)

-\sum_{i<n}\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\frac{a_{ni}}{a_{nn}}(\partial_{i}\tilde{u})v_{t}\,x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt-2\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}(\partial_{i}\tilde{u})v_{t}\,x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt:=V_{i<n}+V_{i=n}.

Starting with
i=n
we have

\displaystyle V_{i=n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial_{n}\tilde{u}\,\partial_{t}v\,\partial_{n}(x_{n}^{2})\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u}\,\partial_{t}v\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt-\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial_{n}\tilde{u}\,(\partial_{n}\partial_{t}v)\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u}\,\partial_{t}v\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}(\partial_{t}\partial_{n}\tilde{u})\,\partial_{n}v\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt.

Recalling the two versions of the Area function introduced in (
2.25
),
we see that for the second term of
V_{i=n}
we have a bound by

\left|\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}(\partial_{t}\partial_{n}\tilde{u})\,\partial_{n}v\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt\right|\lesssim\|A(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}\|S(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}.

We work a bit more on the first term. Using the PDE for
v
:

(5.36)

\displaystyle-\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u}\,\partial_{t}v\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u}\mbox{ div}(A^{*}\nabla v)x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt-\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}(\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u})\mbox{div}\vec{h}\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt.

For the second term we use the remark on differentiability of the function
\vec{h}
(namely (
5.16
)) which implies that

|\mbox{div}\vec{h}|x_{n}\lesssim{H}
, for some function
{H}
with the same properties as
\vec{h}
. Hence

\left|\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}(\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u})(\mbox{div }\vec{h})\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt\right|\lesssim\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}|\nabla^{2}\tilde{u}||H|x_{n}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt,

where this term enjoys the bound by
\|\tilde{N}(x_{n}\nabla^{2}\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}\lesssim\|\tilde{N}(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}
which follows from the Cacciopoli’s inequality for second gradient (Lemma 4.4 of
[
15
]
).
.
The first term on the last line of (
5.36
) can be further estimated by

\displaystyle\left|\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u}\mbox{ div}(A^{*}\nabla v)x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}|\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u}||\nabla A||\nabla v|x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}|\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u}||\nabla^{2}v|x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|S(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}\|S(v)\|_{L^{q}}+\|S(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}\|\tilde{A}(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}},

where for the penultimate term we have used boundedness of
|\nabla A|x_{n}
.

We proceed similarly for the terms
V_{i<n}
:

\displaystyle V_{i<n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\frac{a_{ni}}{a_{nn}}\partial_{n}\tilde{u}\,\partial_{t}v\,\partial_{n}(x_{n}^{2})\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\frac{a_{ni}}{a_{nn}}\partial^{2}_{nn}\tilde{u}\,\partial_{t}v\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt-\frac{1}{2}\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\frac{a_{ni}}{a_{nn}}\partial_{n}\tilde{u}\,(\partial_{n}\partial_{t}v)\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial_{n}\left(\frac{a_{ni}}{a_{nn}}\right)\partial_{n}\tilde{u}\,\partial_{t}v\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt.

The first term of the second row enjoys bounds identical to the term in (
5.36
), i.e.,
by
\|S(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}\|\tilde{A}(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}+\|\tilde{N}(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}
as above, and the last term is bounded by
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}(1+\|\tilde{A}(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}+\|S(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}})
using the Carleson condition on
\nabla A
, the PDE for
v_{t}
, and
\left|\divg\vec{h}\right|x_{n}\lesssim H
.
Meanwhile for the second term of the second row we have

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\frac{a_{ni}}{a_{nn}}\partial_{n}\tilde{u}\,(\partial_{n}\partial_{t}v)\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\partial_{t}\left(\frac{a_{ni}}{a_{nn}}\right)\partial_{n}\tilde{u}\,\partial_{n}v\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt+\frac{1}{2}\iint_{{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}}}\frac{a_{ni}}{a_{nn}}(\partial_{t}\partial_{n}\tilde{u})\,\partial_{n}v\,x_{n}^{2}\,dx\,dx_{n}\,dt.

Here the last term clearly is bounded by
\|A(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}\|S(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}
, while the penultimate term
(using the Carleson condition (
1.3
) for
|\partial_{t}A|^{2}x_{n}^{3}
) is bounded by
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}\|S(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}
.

We make one more observation before summing up the argument.
In section
6
we will establish (
6.5
) which provides bounds on
\|A(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}
.

Therefore, under the assumptions we have made (along with (
6.5
)), namely the solvability of Regularity problem
(R)_{q}^{\mathcal{L}_{0}}
for the block form-parabolic operator accompanied by the estimates:

(5.37)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}+\|S(\nabla\tilde{u})\|_{L^{q}}\leq C(\|\nabla_{\parallel}f\|_{L^{q}}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{q}}),

as well as the solvability of the inhomogeneous Dirichlet problem (
5.19
) with the estimates:

(5.38)

\|N(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}+\|S(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}+\|\tilde{A}(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}\leq C.

then:

II+III=\int_{\partial({{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R})}\nabla_{\parallel}u(x,0)\cdot\vec{V}(x,0)dx\,dt+\int_{\partial({{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R})}D^{1/2}_{t}f(x)W(x,0)dx\,dt

\leq C(\|\nabla_{\parallel}f\|_{L^{q}}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{q}})-I.

Pulling all the estimates together (since term
I
cancels out), we have established the following:

\|\tilde{N}_{1,\varepsilon}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{q}}\leq C(\|\nabla_{\parallel}f\|_{L^{q}}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{q}}).

An argument is required to demonstrate that the control of
\tilde{N}_{1,\varepsilon}(\nabla u)
of a solution
\mathcal{L}u=0
implies the control of
\tilde{N}(\nabla u)
(the
L^{2}
averaged version of the nontangential maximal function as defined in (
2.22
)). Firstly, as the established estimates are independent of
\varepsilon>0
we obtain

\|\tilde{N}_{1}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{q}}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\|\tilde{N}_{1,\varepsilon}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{q}}\leq C(\|\nabla_{\parallel}f\|_{L^{q}}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{q}}).

Secondly,
by Lemma
3.2
,

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B}|\nabla u|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{2B}|\nabla u|\right),

which implies a bound of
\tilde{N}(\nabla u)(\cdot)
defined using cones
\Gamma_{a}(\cdot)
of some aperture
a>0
by
\tilde{N}_{1}(\nabla u)(\cdot)
defined using cones
\Gamma_{b}(\cdot)
of some slightly larger aperture
b>a
.

5.3.
The area function of the adjoint Dirichlet problem

As our first task we find an estimate of the
L^{q^{\prime}}
norm of
\tilde{A}(v)
which is needed for (
5.38
). Fix a boundary point
P
and consider
the nontangential cone
\Gamma(P)
which can be covered by a collection of parabolic Whitney cubes
Q_{i}
with their enlargements
2Q_{i}
having finite overlap and
3Q_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Consider now one such cube
Q_{r}=Q_{i}
(with
r
being the sidelength of this cube).
We take the spatial gradient of this PDE. For simplicity, let
u_{i}=\partial_{i}v
and
w_{i}=u_{i}\zeta^{2}
,
1\leq i\leq n
where
0\leq\zeta\leq 1
is a smooth cutoff function equal to
1
on
Q_{r}
and supported in
Q_{2r}
satisfying
r|\nabla\zeta|+r^{2}|\zeta_{t}|\leq c
for some
c>0
. It follows that (summing over repeating indices)

\iint_{Q_{2r}}(u_{i})_{t}w_{i}\,dX\,dt=\iint_{Q_{2r}}\left(A^{T}\nabla u_{i}+(\partial_{i}A^{T})\nabla v\right)\cdot\nabla w_{i}+\iint_{Q_{2r}}\partial_{i}(\mbox{div }\vec{h})w_{i}\,dX\,dt.

which implies that

\iint A^{T}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla u_{i}\zeta^{2}dXdt=\iint\partial_{t}\left(\frac{u_{i}^{2}\zeta^{2}}{2}\right)dXdt-\iint\partial_{t}\left(\zeta^{2}\right)u_{i}^{2}dXdt\\
-2\iint A^{T}\nabla u_{i}\cdot\nabla\zeta\,u_{i}\zeta dXdt-\iint\left(\partial_{i}A^{T}\right)\nabla v\cdot\nabla u_{i}\,\zeta^{2}dXdt\\
-2\iint\left(\partial_{i}A^{T}\right)\nabla v\cdot\nabla\zeta\,u_{i}\,\zeta\,dXdt-\iint\partial_{i}(\divg\vec{h})u_{i}\zeta^{2}dXdt.

Using the ellipticity and boundedness of the coefficients and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, it follows that

\begin{split}&\lambda\iint_{Q_{r}}\left|\nabla^{2}v\right|^{2}\,dX\,dt\\
&\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}}\iint_{Q_{2r}}|\nabla v|^{2}\,dX\,dt+{C}\iint_{Q_{2r}}|\nabla A|^{2}|\nabla v|^{2}\,dX\,dt+Cr^{n}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{Q_{2r}}H|\nabla v|\,dX\,dt\\
&\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}}\iint_{Q_{2r}}|\nabla v|^{2}\,dX\,dt+C\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{Q_{2r}}|\nabla v|^{2}r^{2}\,dX\,dt\right)^{1/2}r^{n-1}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{Q_{2r}}|H|^{2}\,dX\,dt\right)^{1/2},\end{split}

for some constant
C=C(\lambda,\Lambda)>0
and
|\nabla^{2}\vec{h}|x_{n}^{2}\leq H
, where
H
is a function of the same type as
\vec{h}
.

We now multiply above inequality by
r^{-n+2}
, and sum over all indices
i
for
Q=Q_{i}
to obtain:

\tilde{A}^{2}(v)(P)\lesssim S^{2}(v)(P)+{\tilde{N}}(x_{n}\nabla v)(P)\iint_{\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)}W(H)x_{n}^{-n-1}\,dX\,dt.

Here
\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)
is a cone of wider aperture than the original cone so that
\bigcup_{i}2Q_{i}\subset\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)
and the square and nontangential maximal functions
S(v),\,{\tilde{N}}(x_{n}\nabla v)
are also defined using this wider cone.
W(H)
denotes the
L^{2}
average of
H
over Whitney balls (c.f. (
5.12
)).
And, we have used the estimate of
\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{Q_{2r}}|\nabla v|^{2}r^{2}\,dX\,dt\right)^{1/2}
by
{\tilde{N}}(x_{n}\nabla v)(P)
.
Recall the setting of the paper
[
42
]
which introduced the function
T_{p}(H)(P)
as follows:

T_{p}(H)(P)=\left(\iint_{\Gamma(P)}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B((X,t),\delta((X,t))/2)}|H|^{p}\right)^{1/p}x_{n}^{-n-1}\,dX\,dt.\right)^{1/2}.

In particular in
L^{q^{\prime}}
we have

(5.39)

\|\tilde{A}(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}\lesssim\|{S}(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}+\|N(x_{n}\nabla v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}+\|T_{2}(H)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}.

By the results of Ulmer
[
53
]
(see also
[
54
]
), Lemma 3.7 provides bounds
L^{q^{\prime}}
norms of
N(v)
and
N(x_{n}\nabla v)
, while Corollary 3.9 bounds
S(v)
and finally Lemma 3.10 established bounds for
T_{p}(H)
. In summary, we have that there exists
C>0
independent of
v
such that

\|N(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}+\|N(x_{n}\nabla v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}+\|{S}(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}+\|T_{2}(H)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}\leq C,

for all
q^{\prime}
for which the parabolic Dirichlet problem for the operator
\mathcal{L}^{*}
is solvable. Thanks to (
5.39
) we therefore can conclude that
\|\tilde{A}(v)\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}}\leq C^{\prime}
as well. This establishes all required bounds we have asked for in (
5.38
) for the inhomogeneous parabolic Dirichlet problem.

It follows we have reduced the problem of solvability of the Regularity problem for the parabolic PDE

u_{t}-\mbox{div}(A\nabla u)=0
with elliptic matrix
A
to a related problem of
solvability of the Regularity problem for the parabolic PDE

\tilde{u}_{t}-\mbox{div}(\tilde{A}\nabla\tilde{u})=0
where the matrix
\tilde{A}
has an additional feature, namely it is of the block form. It remain to establish (
5.37
) for such solutions
\tilde{u}
.
This concludes the proof of Theorem
5.1
.∎

6.
The area function of
\nabla u
for the block-form parabolic PDEs.

Let us recall the subsection
5.3
where we have established estimates for the area function of the adjoint inhomogenuous solution
v
. We now establish similar estimates for the area function of
u
; the solution of the block-form parabolic PDEs. Let us denote
w=\partial_{t}u
, then
w
will solve the PDE

(6.1)

-\partial_{t}w+\mbox{div}_{x}(A_{\parallel}(\nabla w)_{\parallel})+\partial^{2}_{x_{n}x_{n}}w=\mbox{div}_{x}((\partial_{t}A_{\parallel})(\nabla u)_{\parallel}),

where, consistent with our previous notation,
(\nabla w)_{\parallel}
and
A_{\parallel}
denotes the first
n-1
components of
\nabla w
and the
n-1\times n-1
subblock of
A
, respectively.
Mimicking the calculation for
\tilde{A}(v)
in subsection
5.3
we multiply both sides by
w\zeta^{2}

where
\zeta
is a cutoff function equal to one on
Q
for an interior Witney cube and zero outside
2Q
. After integration by parts we obtain:

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\iint_{2Q}\left[(w\zeta)^{2}\right]_{t}\,dX\,dt+\iint_{2Q}A\nabla(w\zeta)\cdot\nabla(w\zeta)\,dX\,dt\\
&=\iint_{2Q}w^{2}\zeta\zeta_{t}\,dX\,dt+\iint_{2Q}w^{2}A\nabla\zeta\cdot\nabla\zeta\,dX\,dt+\iint_{2Q}A^{T}\nabla(w\zeta)\cdot w\nabla\zeta\,dX\,dt\\
&\quad-\iint_{2Q}A\nabla(w\zeta)\cdot w\nabla\zeta\,dX\,dt-\iint_{2Q}(\partial_{t}A)\nabla u\zeta\cdot(\nabla w\zeta)\,dX\,dt\\
&\quad-\iint_{2Q}(\partial_{t}A)\nabla u(w\cdot(\zeta\nabla\zeta))\,dX\,dt.\end{split}

Using the ellipticity and boundedness of the coefficients and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, it follows that

\begin{split}&\lambda\iint_{Q}\left|\nabla w\right|^{2}\,dX\,dt\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}}\iint_{2Q}|w|^{2}\,dX\,dt+C\iint_{2Q}|\partial_{t}A|^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}\,dX\,dt.\end{split}

for some constant
C=C(\Lambda)
.

As before in
5.3
, we cover
\Gamma_{a}(P)
by a collection of Whitney cubes
(Q_{i})_{i}
,
multiply above inequality by
r_{i}^{-n+2}
, and sum over all indices
i
for
Q=Q_{i}
to obtain:

(6.2)

{A}^{2}(\nabla u)(P)\lesssim\iint_{\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)}|u_{t}|^{2}x_{n}^{-n}\,dX\,dt+\iint_{\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)}|\partial_{t}A|^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}x_{n}^{-n+2}\,dX\,dt.

Here
\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)
is a cone of wider aperture (smaller slope) than the original cone so that
\bigcup_{i}2Q_{i}\subset\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)
. We note that (
6.2
) also holds if the cones are truncated.

We note that the first term can be further estimated using the equation for
u
and hence

\iint_{\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)}|u_{t}|^{2}x_{n}^{-n}\,dX\,dt\leq S^{2}(\nabla u)(P)+\iint_{\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)}|\nabla_{\parallel}A|^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}x_{n}^{-n}\,dX\,dt,

and therefore

(6.3)

{A}^{2}(\nabla u)(P)\lesssim S^{2}(\nabla u)(P)+\iint_{\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)}(|\partial_{t}A|^{2}x_{n}^{2}+|\nabla_{\parallel}A|^{2})|\nabla u|^{2}x_{n}^{-n}\,dX\,dt.

Again, a truncated version of this inequality also holds. Using truncated cones at the height
r
and integrating over a boundary ball
\Delta_{r}
we obtain that:

\|A(\nabla u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Delta_{r})}\lesssim\|S(\nabla u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(2\Delta_{r})}+\iint_{T(2\Delta_{r})}(|\partial_{t}A|^{2}x_{n}^{2}+|\nabla_{\parallel}A|^{2})|\nabla u|^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt

\leq\|S(\nabla u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(2\Delta_{r})}+\|\mu_{\parallel}\|_{C}\|N(\nabla u)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(2\Delta_{r})},

using the Carleson condition for
\mu_{\parallel}
.
In particular after taking
r\to\infty
we obtain

(6.4)

\|A(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}\leq C\|S(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}+C\|\mu_{\parallel}\|_{C}^{1/2}\|N(\nabla u)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}.

We are also interested in an
L^{q}
version of this inequality, which will be obtained via a good-
\lambda
argument.

Denote by
S_{A}(u)(P)
the square root of the second term of (
6.3
); that is,

S_{A}(u)(P)=\left(\iint_{\Gamma_{a^{\prime}}(P)}(|\partial_{t}A|^{2}x_{n}^{2}+|\nabla_{\parallel}A|^{2})|\nabla u|^{2}x_{n}^{-n}\,dX\,dt\right)^{1/2}.

It follows that if we establish
\|S_{A}(u)\|_{L^{q}}\leq C\|N(\nabla u)\|_{L^{q}}
for all
q>1
it would follow from
(
6.3
) that

(6.5)

\|A(\nabla u)\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\|S(\nabla u)\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})}+\|\mu_{\parallel}\|_{C}^{1/2}\|N(\nabla u)\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R})},\quad\mbox{for }q>1.

Denote by
E_{\lambda}
the set
\{P:\,S_{A}(u)(P)>2\lambda,\,N(\nabla u)(P)\leq\gamma\lambda\}
for some
\gamma>0
to be determined later. We decompose the open set
\{P:\,S_{A}(u)(P)>\lambda\}
and write it as a union of countable collection of open balls
\Delta_{i}
of finite overlap such that each
\Delta_{i}
has the property that
2\Delta_{i}\cap E_{\lambda}^{c}\neq\emptyset
.

If we prove that for each
i
we have
|\Delta_{i}\cap E_{\lambda}|\leq C\gamma^{2}|\Delta_{i}|
it will follow that

|E_{\lambda}|\leq C\gamma^{2}|\{P:\,S_{A}(u)(P)>\lambda\}|.

From this the claim
