(10.13)

\left|\hbar_{\nu,a}(w)(x,t)-\hbar_{\nu,a}(w)(y,s)\right|\leq C_{n}a^{-1}\left(\left|x-y\right|+\left|t-s\right|^{1/2}\right)

for all
(x,t),(y,s)\in\partial\Omega
.

(iii)
Given an arbitrary
(x,t)\in E_{\nu,a}
, where
E_{\nu,a}
is defined in (
10.5
), let
x_{n}:=\hbar_{\nu,a}(w)(x,t)
. Then there exists a
point
(y,y_{n},s)\in\partial\gamma_{a}(x,x_{n},t)
such that
w(y,y_{n},s)=\nu
and
\hbar_{\nu,a}(w)(y,s)=y_{n}
.

Proof.

(i) and (ii).
The way the function
\hbar_{\nu,a}(w)
is defined implies the following observation.
Consider two points
(x,t)
,
(y,t)\in\partial\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and let
x_{n}=\hbar_{\nu,a}(w)(x,t)
and
y_{n}=\hbar_{\nu,a}(w)(y,s)
and let
(X,t)=(x,x_{n},t)
,
(Y,s)=(y,y_{n},s)
.

It is not possible to have
(Y,s)\in\gamma_{a}(X,t)
or vice versa
(X,t)\in\gamma_{a}(Y,s)
. Indeed, if for example
(Y,s)\in\gamma_{a}(X,t)
it would follow that

\overline{\gamma_{a}(Y,s)}\in\gamma_{a}(X,t)

and hence
\sup_{(Z,s)\in\gamma_{a}(Y,s)}w(Z,s)<\nu
which contradicts with the definition of

\hbar_{\nu,a}(w)(y,s)=y_{n}
. Therefore

(Y,s)\notin\gamma_{a}(X,t)
and
(X,t)\notin\gamma_{a}(Y,s)
. This implies that if
we
let
\phi(x,t):\partial\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be the function whose graph is the boundary of

\partial\gamma_{a}(X,t)
then

\hbar_{\nu,a}(w)(y,s)\leq\phi(x,t)(y,s)\qquad\forall(x,t),\,(y,s)\in\partial\Omega,

and thus

(10.14)

\hbar_{\nu,a}(w)=\inf\{\phi(x,t):\,(x,t)\in\partial\Omega\}.

Each function
\phi(x,t)
being a boundary of a cone described above enjoys the Lipschitz bounds
by
a^{-1}
in spatial and
\frac{a^{-2}}{2x_{n}}
is the time variable. From this both claims (i) and (ii) follow.

We omit the proof of part (iii) as it is identical to that of given in
[
16
]
.
∎

The next two statements appear in
[
16
]
and apply in our settings with analogous proofs.

Lemma 10.15
.

Assume that
w
is defined as in (
10.3
) and
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
.
Then for any
a>0
there exists
0<b_{0}=b_{0}(a)
and
\gamma_{0}=\gamma_{0}(a)>0
such that the following holds.
Having fixed an arbitrary
\nu>0
,
b\geq b_{0}
, and
\gamma\leq\gamma_{0}
for each point
(x,t)\in\partial\Omega
from the set

(10.16)

\big\{(x,t):\,N_{a}(w)(x,t)>\nu\mbox{ and }S_{b}(\nabla u)(x,t)+A_{b}(\nabla u)(x,t)\leq\gamma\nu\big\}

there exists a boundary ball
R
with
(x,t)\in 2R
and such that

(10.17)

\big|w\big(z,\hbar_{\nu,a}(w)(z,\tau),\tau\big)\big|>\nu/{2}\,\,\text{ for all }\,\,(z,\tau)\in R.

A new feature here (distinct from
[
16
]
) is the presence of the area function (
2.25
) that allows us to use Poincaré inequality in all variables (including
t
). (Since
[
16
]
deals with the elliptic case, the
t
-variable was not present there.)

Corollary 10.18
.

Let
w
be defined as in (
10.3
),
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
and fix
a>0
.
Associated with these, let
b_{0},\gamma_{0}
be as in Lemma
10.15
. Then there exists a finite
constant
C=C(n)>0
with the property that for any
\nu>0
,
b\geq b_{0}
,
\gamma\in(0,\gamma_{0}]
, and any point
(x,t)\in E_{\nu,a}

such that
S_{b}(\nabla u)((x,t))+{A_{b}(\nabla u)((x,t))}\leq\gamma\nu
, one has

(10.19)

(M_{\hbar_{\nu,a}}w)\big(x,\hbar_{\nu,a}(x,t),t\big)\geq\,C\nu,

where
M_{\hbar_{\nu,a}}
is the Hardy-Littlewood maximal function on the graph of
\hbar_{\nu,a}
.

Finally, we are ready to state the key lemma that allows us to formulate a good-
\lambda
inequality later.
From here we follow more closely the elliptic model case of
[
14
]
. Notice, that up to this point we have not used any property of
\nabla u
except a moderate decay
x_{n}\to\infty
and the fact that
\nabla u\in L^{2}_{loc}(\Omega)
. This changes now as we will use the PDE system it satisfies.

Consider the PDEs satisfied by each
w_{m}=\partial_{m}u
for
m=1,2,\dots,n-1
. Due to the block form nature of our operator
\mathcal{L}=-\partial_{t}+\mbox{div}(A\nabla\cdot)
we have the following:

(10.20)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}w_{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i,j=1}^{n-1}\partial_{i}((\partial_{m}a_{ij})w_{j})\quad\mbox{in }\Omega,\quad m=1,2,\dots,n-1,

\displaystyle w_{m}\Big|_{\partial\Omega}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{m}f.

Observe that only
w_{1},\dots,w_{n-1}
appears in these equations and hence (
10.20
) is a
weakly coupled
fully determined system of
n-1
equations for the unknown vector valued function

W:=(w_{1},w_{2}\dots,w_{n-1})

with boundary datum
W\big|_{\partial\Omega}=\nabla_{x}f\in L^{p}
. We call this system
weakly coupled
because each
\partial_{m}a_{ij}
appearing on the righthand side will be assumed to have a small Carleson measure norm,
\|\mu_{\parallel}\|_{C}
.

Hence, let us write
w_{m}=v_{m}+\eta_{m}
where each
v_{m}
solves the parabolic Dirichlet problem

\mathcal{L}v_{m}=0\mbox{ in }\Omega,\qquad v_{m}\big|_{\partial\Omega}=\partial_{m}f\in L^{q}(\partial\Omega).

We denote

V:=(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n-1}).

As
\mathcal{L}
is a block form matrix we know this Dirichlet problem is solvable for all
1<q<\infty
(c.f. Appendix
A
) and we have the following square and nontangential estimates:

(10.21)

\|S(v_{m})\|_{L^{q}}\approx\|\tilde{N}(v_{m})\|_{L^{q}}\lesssim\|\partial_{m}f\|_{L^{q}},\qquad m=1,2,\dots,n-1.

Thus each
\eta_{m}
solves

(10.22)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}\eta_{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i,j=1}^{n-1}\partial_{i}((\partial_{m}a_{ij})(v_{j}+\eta_{j}))\quad\mbox{in }\Omega,\quad m=1,2,\dots,n-1,

\displaystyle\eta_{m}\Big|_{\partial\Omega}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Our aim is to establish nontangential estimates for each
\eta_{m}
as well, and thus also for
w_{m}
.

Lemma 10.23
.

Let
\Omega={\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
and let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a block-form operator as above.
Suppose
\vec{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\dots,\eta_{n-1})
is a weak solution of (
10.22
) in
\Omega
. For a fixed (sufficiently large
a>0
determined below), consider an arbitrary function
\hbar:{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that it satisfies
the estimates (
10.11
), (
10.12
) and
\hbar\geq 0
.
Then
we have the following:

For any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0

such that if
\left[\|\mu_{||}\|_{C}+\|x_{n}\nabla_{x}A_{||}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}}\right]\Big(1+\|x_{n}\nabla A\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}}\Big)<\delta
(where
\mu_{||}
defined by (
1.5
)) then
for all arbitrary parabolic surface balls
\Delta_{r}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
of radius
r
such that at least one point of
\Delta_{r}

the inequality
\hbar(x,t)\leq 2r
holds we have the following estimate for all
m=1,2,\dots,n-1
and an arbitrary
\vec{c}=(c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{n-1})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
:

(10.24)

\sum_{m<n}\int_{1/6}^{6}\int_{\Delta_{r}}\big|\eta_{m}\big(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t\big)-c_{m}\big|^{2}\,dx\,dt\,d\theta\leq C(1+\varepsilon^{-1})\iint_{\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{r},\hbar)}\left|\nabla\eta_{m}\right|^{2}x_{n}\,dx_{n}dtdx\\
+\varepsilon\left(\|\tilde{N}_{a}\left((\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{r},\hbar)}\right)\|_{L^{2}(\Delta_{2r})}^{2}+\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}\,{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{r},\hbar)})\|_{L^{2}(\Delta_{2r})}^{2}+\|\tilde{N}_{a}(V{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{r},\hbar)})\|_{L^{2}(\Delta_{2r})}^{2}\right)\\
+\frac{C}{r}\iint_{\mathcal{K_{\varepsilon}}}|\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}|^{2}\,dX\,dt,

for some
C\in(0,\infty)
that only depends on
a,\Lambda,n
but not on
\vec{\eta}
,
\vec{c}
,
\varepsilon
or
\Delta_{r}
.
Here,

\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{r},\hbar):=\left\{(x,x_{n},t):\,(x,t)\in\Delta_{2r}\text{ and }\frac{\hbar(x,t)}{6}<x_{n}<c_{a}r\right\},

where
c_{a}
is a constant depending only on
a
(one can take
c_{a}=26(1+2a^{-1})
for instance), and
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{r},\hbar)\cap\left\{(x,x_{n},t):x_{n}>\varepsilon r\right\}
.

Also for
w_{n}=\partial_{x_{n}}u
we have for an arbitrary
c\in\mathbb{R}

(10.25)

\int_{1/6}^{6}\int_{\Delta_{r}}\big|w_{n}\big(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t\big)-c\big|^{2}\,dx\,dt\,d\theta\leq C(1+\varepsilon^{-1})\iint_{\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{r},\hbar)}\left|\nabla w_{n}\right|^{2}x_{n}\,dx_{n}dtdx\\
+C(\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}+\|x_{n}|\nabla A_{\parallel}|\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2})(1+\|x_{n}|\nabla A_{\parallel}|\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2})\|\tilde{N}_{a}(W{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{r},\hbar)})\|_{L^{2}(\Delta_{2r})}^{2}\\
+\varepsilon\|\tilde{N}_{a}\left((w_{n}-c){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{r},\hbar)}\right)\|_{L^{2}(\Delta_{2r})}^{2}+\frac{C}{r}\iint_{\mathcal{K_{\varepsilon}}}|w_{n}-c|^{2}\,dX\,dt,

The cones used to define the nontangential
maximal functions in this lemma have vertices on
\partial\Omega
.

Proof.

Let
\Delta_{r}
be as in the statement of our Lemma. and assume that
(q,\tau)
is the center of
\Delta_{r}
. Let
\zeta
be a smooth cutoff function of the form
\zeta(x,x_{n},t)=\zeta_{0}(x_{n})\zeta_{1}(x,t)
where

(10.26)

\zeta_{0}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ in }(-\infty,r_{0}+r],\\
0&\text{ in }[r_{0}+2r,\infty),\end{cases}\qquad\zeta_{1}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ in }\Delta_{r}(q,\tau),\\
0&\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Delta_{2r}(q,\tau)\end{cases}

and

(10.27)

r|\partial_{x_{n}}\zeta_{0}|+r|\nabla_{x}\zeta_{1}|+r^{2}|\partial_{t}\zeta_{1}|\leq c

for some constant
c\in(0,\infty)
independent of
r
. Here

r_{0}=6\sup_{(x,t)\in\Delta_{r}}\hbar(x,t)
. Observe that our assumptions imply that

0\leq r_{0}-\theta\hbar(x,t)\leq r_{0}\leq 24(1+a^{-1})r,\qquad\mbox{for all }(x,t)\in\Delta_{2r},

for
\theta\in(1/6,6)
.

We focus on (
10.24
) first.
Our goal is to control the
L^{2}
norm of
\eta_{m}\big(\cdot,\theta\hbar(\cdot)\big)-c_{m}
. We fix
m\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
and proceed to estimate

\displaystyle\hskip-14.45377pt\int_{\Delta_{r}(q,\tau)}(\eta_{m}(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t)-c_{m})^{2}\,dx\,dt\leq\mathcal{I}:=\int_{\Delta_{2r}(q,\tau)}(\eta_{m}(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t)-c_{m})^{2}\zeta(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t)\,dx\,dt

\displaystyle\hskip 50.58878pt=-\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}\partial_{x_{n}}\left[(\eta_{m}(x,x_{n},t)-c_{m})^{2}\zeta(x,x_{n},t)\right]\,dx_{n}\,dx\,dt

where
\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)=\{(x,x_{n},t):(x,t)\in\Delta_{2r}(q,\tau)\mbox{ and }\theta\hbar(x,t)<x_{n}<r_{0}+2r\}
. Hence:

\displaystyle\hskip 7.22743pt\mathcal{I}\leq-2\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})\partial_{x_{n}}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})\zeta\,dx_{n}\,\,dt\,dx

(10.28)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.58878pt\quad-\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})^{2}(x,x_{n},t)\partial_{x_{n}}\zeta\,dx_{n}\,dx\,dt=:\mathcal{A}+V.

We further expand the term
\mathcal{A}
as a sum of three terms obtained
via integration by parts with respect to
x_{n}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=-2\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})\partial_{x_{n}}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})(\partial_{x_{n}}x_{n})\zeta\,d_{n}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle=2\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}\left|\partial_{x_{n}}\eta_{m}\right|^{2}x_{n}\zeta\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle\quad+2\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})\partial^{2}_{x_{n}x_{n}}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})x_{n}\zeta\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle\quad+2\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})\partial_{x_{n}}\eta_{m}\,x_{n}\partial_{x_{n}}\zeta\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{\mathcal{\partial}S(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})\partial_{x_{n}}\eta_{m}\,x_{n}\zeta\,\nu_{n}dS

(10.29)

\displaystyle=:I+II+III+IV.

Here the boundary term
IV
is nonvanishing only on the graph of function
\theta\hbar
.

We start by analyzing the term
II
. As the
\eta_{m}
solve the PDE (
10.22
) then we have for
\eta_{m}-c_{m}
:

{\mathcal{L}}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})=-\sum_{i,j=1}^{n-1}\partial_{i}((\partial_{m}a_{ij})(v_{j}+\eta_{j}))

and thus using the block-form structure of our matrix:

(10.30)

\partial^{2}_{x_{n}x_{n}}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})=-\sum_{i,j=1}^{n-1}\partial_{i}((\partial_{m}a_{ij})(v_{j}+\eta_{j}))-\sum_{i,j=1}^{n-1}\partial_{i}(a_{ij}\partial_{j}(\eta_{m}-c_{m}))+\partial_{t}(\eta_{m}-c_{m}).

In turn, this permits us to write the term
II
as

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle=-2\sum_{i,j<n}\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})\partial_{i}\left({a}_{ij}\partial_{j}\eta_{m}\right)x_{n}\zeta\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle\quad-2\sum_{i,j<n}\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})\partial_{i}\left((\partial_{m}a_{ij})(v_{j}+\eta_{j})\right)x_{n}\zeta\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx

(10.31)

\displaystyle\quad+2\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})\partial_{t}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})x_{n}\zeta\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx=\mathcal{B}+\mathcal{C}+II_{8}.

Here we leave analysis of the last term
II_{8}
for later and continue with the first two terms which we both integrate by parts w.r.t.
\partial_{i}
. This then yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}+\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i,j<n}\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}a_{ij}\partial_{i}\eta_{m}\partial_{j}\eta_{m}\,x_{n}\zeta\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle+2\sum_{i,j<n}\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}a_{ij}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})\partial_{j}(\eta_{m})\,x_{n}\partial_{i}\zeta)\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle+2\sum_{i,j<n}\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\partial_{m}a_{ij})(\partial_{i}\eta_{m})(v_{j}+\eta_{j})x_{n}\zeta\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle+2\sum_{i,j<n}\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\partial_{m}a_{ij})(\eta_{m}-c_{m})(v_{j}+\eta_{j})x_{n}(\partial_{i}\zeta)\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle\quad-2\sum_{i>0}\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\mbox{boundary terms})x_{n}\zeta\nu_{i}\,dS

(10.32)

\displaystyle=:II_{1}+II_{2}+II_{3}+II_{4}+II_{5}.

The boundary integral (term
II_{5}
) vanishes everywhere except on the graph of the function
\theta\hbar
. Note that on the graph of
\theta\hbar
,

\nu_{i}=\frac{-\theta\partial_{i}\hbar}{J_{h}}dxdt,\quad dS=J_{h}\,dxdt\quad\text{for }i=1,\dots,n-1,

where
J_{h}:=\left(1+(\theta\nabla_{x}\hbar)^{2}+(\theta\partial_{t}\hbar)\right)^{1/2}
, and so

\left|\nu_{i}\right|dS\leq\theta\left|\partial_{i}\hbar\right|dxdt\leq\theta\,a^{-1}dxdt
. Hence,

\displaystyle|II_{5}|

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{i,j<n}\int_{\Delta_{2r}(q,\tau)}|(\eta_{m}-c_{m})(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t))\nabla_{x}(\eta_{m})(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t)\hbar(x,t)\zeta(x,\theta\hbar(x),t)|dt\,dx

\displaystyle\,+C\sum_{i,j<n}\int_{\Delta_{2r}(q,\tau)}|\partial_{m}a_{ij}||(\eta_{m}-c_{m})(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t))(\eta_{j}+v_{j})(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t)\hbar(x,t)\zeta(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t)|dt\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{6}\int_{\Delta_{2r}(q,\tau)}(\eta_{m}(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t)-c_{m})^{2}\zeta(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t)\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle\quad+C^{\prime}\int_{\Delta_{2r}(q,\tau)}|\nabla_{x}\eta_{m}(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t)|^{2}|\hbar(x,t)|^{2}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle\quad+\|x_{n}\nabla_{x}A_{||}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\int_{\Delta_{2r}(q,\tau)}(|\vec{\eta}+V|^{2})(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t)\,dt\,dx=\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{I}+II_{6}+II_{7}.

Here we have used the Cauchy-Schwarz for the first two terms.
We can hide the term
\frac{1}{6}\mathcal{I}
on the left-hand side of (
10.28
), while the second term after integrating
II_{6}
in
\theta
becomes:

\displaystyle\int_{1/6}^{6}|II_{6}|\,d\theta

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{1/6}^{6}\int_{\Delta_{2r}(q,\tau)}|\nabla\eta_{m}(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t)|^{2}|\hbar(x,t)|^{2}dt\,dxd\theta.

(10.33)

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}|\nabla\eta_{m}(x,x_{n},t)|^{2}x_{n}\,dt\,dxdx_{n}.

The term
II_{7}
can be estimated using the nontangential maximal function: for
(x,t)\in\Delta_{2r}
, we have
\left|\vec{\eta}+\vec{V}\right|(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t)\leq N((\vec{\eta}+\vec{V})\,{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)})(x,t)
, and thus, using Lemma
3.22

|II_{7}|\leq\|x_{n}\nabla_{x}A_{\parallel}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\Big(1+\|x_{n}\nabla A_{\parallel}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}}\Big)\\
\cdot\left(\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}\,{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)})\|_{L^{2}(\Delta_{2r})}^{2}+\|\tilde{N}_{a}(V{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)})\|_{L^{2}(\Delta_{2r})}^{2}\right).

It follows that this term will appear in (
10.24
) with an
\varepsilon
, if we have
\|x_{n}\nabla_{x}A_{||}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\Big(1+\|x_{n}\nabla A\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}}\Big)
sufficiently small.
Observe that the boundary term
IV
enjoys a similar estimate. Then
\nu_{n}dS=-\,dt\,dx
on the graph of
\theta\hbar
and by Cauchy-Schwarz we again obtain the term
\frac{1}{6}\mathcal{I}
that can be absorbed by the lefthand side
and a term analogous to
II_{6}
but with
|\partial_{x_{n}}\eta_{m}|^{2}
instead of
|\nabla_{x}\eta_{m}|^{2}
but this does not change
anything in the estimate (
10.33
) and we obtain an identical bound for it.

Some of the remaining (solid integral) terms that are of the same type we estimate together. Firstly, we have

(10.34)

|I+II_{1}|\leq C\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\left|\nabla\eta_{m}\right|^{2}x_{n}\,dx_{n}dtdx.

Next, since
r|\nabla\zeta|\leq c
, if the derivative falls on the cutoff function
\zeta
we have

\displaystyle|II_{2}+III|

\displaystyle\lesssim\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\left|\nabla\eta_{m}\right||\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}|\frac{x_{n}}{r}\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\left(\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}|\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}|^{2}\frac{x_{n}}{r^{2}}\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx\right)^{1/2}\left(\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\left|\nabla\eta_{m}\right|^{2}x_{n}\,dxdtdx_{n}\right)^{1/2}.

By Fubini’s theorem, and the fact that

\iint_{\gamma_{a}(x,t)\cap\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}y_{n}^{-n-1}dydsdy_{n}\leq Cr
, the first integral on the right hand side can be controlled it by the nontangential maximal function:

(10.35)

\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}|\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}|^{2}\frac{x_{n}}{r^{2}}\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx\leq\frac{C}{r}\int_{\Delta_{2r}}\iint_{\gamma_{a}(x,t)\cap\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\left|\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}\right|^{2}y_{n}^{-n-1}dydsdy_{n}\\
\leq C\int_{\Delta_{2r}}\tilde{N}_{a}\left((\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\right)^{2}dxdt.

Hence,

\left|II_{2}+III\right|\leq C\left\|\tilde{N}_{a}\left((\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\Delta_{2r})}\left(\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\left|\nabla\eta_{m}\right|^{2}x_{n}\,dxdtdx_{n}\right)^{1/2}.

The Carleson condition for
|\nabla A_{\parallel}|^{2}x_{n}
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality imply

|II_{3}|\leq 2\left(\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\left|\partial_{m}A\right|\left|v_{j}+\eta_{j}\right|^{2}x_{n}\,dx_{n}dxdt\right)^{1/2}\left(\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\left|\nabla\eta_{m}\right|^{2}x_{n}\,dx_{n}dxdt\right)^{1/2}\\
\leq C\|\mu_{||}\|_{C}^{1/2}(1+\|x_{n}|\nabla A|\|_{L^{\infty}})\left((\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}\,{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)})\|_{L^{2}(\Delta_{2r})}^{2}+\|\tilde{N}_{a}(V{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Delta_{2r})})^{1/2}\right)\\
\cdot\left(\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\left|\nabla\eta_{m}\right|^{2}x_{n}\,dx_{n}dxdt\right)^{1/2}.

For the term
II_{4}
we use
r|\nabla\zeta|\leq c
.
It follows that

|II_{4}|\lesssim\|x_{n}|\nabla_{x}A_{\parallel}\|_{L^{\infty}}\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}|\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}||V+\vec{\eta}|\frac{x_{n}}{r^{2}}\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx.

An application of Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and (
10.35
) implies that

|II_{4}|\leq C\|x_{n}|\nabla_{x}A_{\parallel}|\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|\tilde{N}_{a}\left((\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\right)\right\|_{\Delta_{2r}}\\
\cdot\left(\left\|\tilde{N}_{a}\left(\vec{\eta}{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\right)\right\|_{\Delta_{2r}}+\left\|\tilde{N}_{a}\left(V{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\right)\right\|_{\Delta_{2r}}\right).

Again the constant in front of these terms can be made less than
\varepsilon
.

In a similar spirit we analyze the term
II_{8}
. We write it as

(10.36)

\displaystyle II_{8}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}\partial_{t}[(\eta_{m}-c_{m})^{2}]x_{n}\zeta\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\eta_{m}-c_{m})^{2}x_{n}(\partial_{t}\zeta)\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)}(\mbox{boundary terms})x_{n}\zeta\nu_{t}\,dS=:II_{9}+II_{10},

where the boundary terms only arise on the portion of
\partial\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\theta\hbar)
where

\zeta
is supported (which means on the graph of the function
\theta\hbar
).
\nu_{t}
denotes the co-normal
in the
t
-direction.
Note that on the graph of
\theta\hbar
,
\nu_{t}=-\frac{\partial_{t}\hbar}{J_{h}}
and
dS=J_{h}dxdt
, where
J_{h}
is defined above the computation for
II_{5}
. By (
10.12
), we have that
\left|\nu_{t}\right|dS\leq\frac{a^{-2}}{2x_{n}}dxdt
.
It follows that

|II_{10}|\lesssim\frac{1}{2a^{2}}\int_{\Delta(q,\tau)}|(\eta_{m}-c_{m})^{2}(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t)|\zeta\,dt\,dx\leq\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{I},

where the last estimate holds if
a
is chosen sufficiently large. We do so now. The solid integral term
II_{9}
can be bounded as follows. Since
|\partial_{t}\zeta|\lesssim r^{-2}
we split the area of integration into two parts, the first one for
x_{n}/r\leq\varepsilon
and its complement.
On the set where
x_{n}/r\leq\varepsilon
we get that the integral is bounded by

\varepsilon\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}|\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}|^{2}\frac{1}{r}\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx\leq\varepsilon\|\tilde{N}_{a}\left((\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)}\right)\|_{L^{2}(\Delta_{2r})}^{2}

using (
10.35
).
The remaining part of the integral is bounded by
\frac{C}{r}\displaystyle\iint_{\mathcal{K_{\varepsilon}}}|\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}|^{2}\,dX\,dt
.

Another term with a similar estimate is
V
; the fact that
\partial_{x_{n}}\zeta
vanishes on the set

(-\infty,r_{0}+r)\cup(r_{0}+2r,\infty)
means that it is supported away from the boundary of
\partial\Omega
and therefore,

(10.37)

|V|\leq\frac{C}{r}\iint_{\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)\cap\left\{x_{n}\geq r_{0}+r\right\}}|\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}|^{2}\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx\leq\frac{C}{r}\iint_{\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}}|\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}|^{2}\,dx_{n}\,dt\,dx.

We put together all terms and integrate in
\theta
. Observe that
\mathcal{S}(q,\tau,r,r_{0},\frac{1}{6}\hbar)\subset\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{r},\hbar)
, and the above analysis ultimately yields (
10.24
).
Thus the claim follows.

We only point out the differences in our calculation for (
10.25
). The function
w_{n}-c
for an arbitrary
c\in\mathbb{R}
solves the PDE

(10.38)

{\mathcal{L}}(w_{n}-c)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n-1}\partial_{i}((\partial_{n}a_{ij})w_{j}).

It follows that the calculation given above can be followed step by step. The only difference is that we cannot claim any smallness of certain numbers appearing in these inequalities since the Carleson measure we are estimating is that of
|\partial_{n}A_{\parallel}|^{2}x_{n}
, which is not assumed to be small. This is not
problematic since the terms that it modifies involve
\tilde{N}(W)
for which we obtain the desired estimates using
(
10.24
).
∎

Lemma 10.39
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an operator as in Lemma
10.23
and fix
a>0
sufficiently large as in Lemma
10.23
.
There exists
\kappa\in(0,1)
that depends only on
n
, constants
b>a
and
\gamma_{0}
that depend on
a
, and
C>0
that depends only on
n
and the ellipticity constants such that the following holds.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, if
\left[\|\mu_{||}\|_{C}+\left\|x_{n}\left|\nabla_{x}A_{\parallel}\right|\right\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}}\right]\Big(1+\|x_{n}|\nabla A_{\parallel}|\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}}\Big)
is sufficiently small (depending on
\varepsilon
), then for any
\vec{\eta}
that satisfies (
10.22
), for any
\gamma\in(0,\gamma_{0})
,
\beta>0
, there holds

(10.40)

\left|E_{1,\beta}\right|\leq C\gamma^{2}\left|\left\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}:M(\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}))(x,t)>\kappa\beta\right\}\right|.

where

(10.41)

E_{1,\beta}:=\\
\left\{(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}:\,\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta})>\beta,\varepsilon^{-1/2}[S_{b}(\vec{\eta})+{\varepsilon^{-1}A_{b}(\vec{\eta})}]\leq\gamma\beta,\varepsilon^{1/2}\left(\tilde{N}_{b}(\vec{\eta})+\tilde{N}_{b}(V)\right)\leq\gamma\beta\right\}.

Proof.

Let
b_{0}
and
\gamma_{0}
be as in Corollary
10.18
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
,
\gamma\in(0,\gamma_{0})
,
\beta>0
be fixed.
Observe that
E_{2,\beta}:=\left\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}:M(\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}))(x,t)>\kappa\beta\right\}
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
. We can assume that
E_{2,\beta}
is a nonempty proper subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
, as otherwise the estimate (
10.40
) is trivial.
By Vitali’s covering lemma, we can find a non-overlapping collection of parabolic balls
\left\{\Delta(x_{i},t_{i})\right\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

such that
2\Delta(x_{i},t_{i})\subset E_{2,\beta}
,
10\Delta(x_{i},t_{i})\cap E_{2,\beta}^{c}\neq\emptyset
for each
i
, and that
E_{2,\beta}\subset\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}5\Delta(x_{i},t_{i})
. Denote
\Delta_{i}:=5\Delta(x_{i},t_{i})
, and denote by
r_{i}
the radius of
\Delta_{i}
.
Set
F_{i}:=E_{1,\beta}\cap\Delta_{i}
. Note that
E_{1,\beta}\subset\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}F_{i}
as
E_{1,\beta}\subset E_{2,\beta}
.

Let us denote

\vec{\eta}_{L^{2}}(x,x_{n},t):=\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B((x,x_{n},t),x_{n}/2)}\left|\vec{\eta}(Y,s)\right|^{2}dYds\right)^{1/2},

where
B((x,x_{n},t),x_{n}/2)
is a parabolic Whitney ball centered at
(x,x_{n},t)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. Take
b\geq b_{0}
and let
\hbar:=\hbar_{\beta,a}(\vec{\eta}_{L^{2}})
be as in (
10.9
). By Corollary
10.18
,
(M_{\hbar}(\vec{\eta}_{L^{2}}))\big(x,\hbar(x,t),t\big)\geq\,C\beta
for
(x,t)\in F_{i}
. We claim that this is also true when
M_{\hbar}(\vec{\eta}_{L^{2}})
is replaced with the localized maximal function
M_{\hbar,6ar_{i}}(\vec{\eta}_{L^{2}})
at scale
\sim r_{i}
. In fact, since
2\Delta_{i}\cap E_{2,\beta}^{c}\neq\emptyset
, we have

(10.42)

M(\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}))(y,s)\leq\kappa\beta\quad\text{for some }(y,s)\in 2\Delta_{i}\cap E_{2,\beta}^{c}.

From this one deduces that

(10.43)

\vec{\eta}_{L^{2}}(x,x_{n},t)\leq 2^{n+1}\kappa\beta\quad\text{for }(x,t)\in\Delta_{i}\text{ and }x_{n}>2r_{i}.

In fact, suppose that (
10.43
) is false for some
(Z,\tau)=(z,z_{n},\tau)
with
(z,\tau)\in\Delta_{i}
and
z_{n}>2r_{i}
. Then
\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta})(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})>2^{n+1}\kappa\beta
for all
(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in S(Z,\tau)
, where
S(Z,\tau)
is defined as in (
10.7
), which is a parabolic ball centered at
(z,\tau)
of radius at least
2ar_{i}
. Therefore, since
a>1
,

\kappa\beta<\left(\frac{2a}{3+2a}\right)^{n+1}2^{n+1}\kappa\beta<\fint_{\Delta_{(3+2a)r_{i}}(y,s)}\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta})\,dx^{\prime}dt^{\prime}\leq M(\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}))(y,s),

a contradiction to (
10.42
).

We choose
\kappa\leq 2^{-n-1}
. In view of (
10.43
), for
(x,t)\in F_{i}
,
\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta})(x,t)=\tilde{N}_{a}^{2r_{i}}(\vec{\eta})(x,t)
. It also guarantees that
\hbar\leq 2r_{i}
on
\Delta_{i}
. Moreover, it implies that the “radius” of the boundary ball
R
constructed in Lemma
10.15
is bounded by
6ar_{i}
. Therefore, we have justified that

(10.44)

\text{ for }(x,t)\in F_{i},\ (M_{\hbar,6ar_{i}}(\vec{\eta}_{L^{2}}))\big(x,\hbar(x,t),t\big)\geq\,C\beta.

We define

(10.45)

\hbar_{i}(x,t):=\sup\left\{\hbar(x,t),\frac{1}{2}\dist_{p}\left((x,t),F_{i}\right)\right\},

and
\mathcal{S}_{i}:=\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{20r_{i}},\hbar_{i})
, where
\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{20r_{i}},\hbar_{i})
is defined as in Lemma
10.23
. One can easily check that
\hbar_{i}
satisfies (
10.11
), (
10.12
), and (
10.13
), and enjoys in addition the property

(10.46)

\text{for any }(x,t)\in 40a\,\Delta_{i}\setminus F_{i},\ \exists\,(y,s)\in F_{i}\text{ such that }\quad\mathcal{S}_{i}\cap\gamma_{a}(x,t)\subset\gamma_{b}(y,s)

for
b\geq b_{0}
sufficiently large.
Property (
10.46
) holds because we ensure that the graph of
h_{i}
is sufficiently far away from the boundary even when it is not above
F_{i}
.

We choose the vector
\vec{c}_{i}:=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B_{i}^{0}}\vec{\eta}
, where
B_{i}^{0}
is a ball with radius
\sim r_{i}
that is contained in
\mathcal{S}_{i}\cap\left\{(x,x_{n},t):(x,t)\in\Delta_{i},x_{n}>2r_{i}\right\}
. By (
10.43
),
\left|\vec{c}_{i}\right|\leq|\vec{\eta}_{L^{2}}|\leq 2^{n+1}\kappa\beta
. Then by (
10.44
), we have

C^{2}\beta^{2}\left|F_{i}\right|\leq\int_{F_{i}}(M_{\hbar,6ar_{i}}(\vec{\eta}_{L^{2}}))\big(x,\hbar(x,t),t\big)^{2}dxdt\\
\leq\int_{F_{i}}(M_{\hbar,6ar_{i}}(\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}_{i})_{L^{2}})\big(x,\hbar(x,t),t\big)^{2}dxdt+2^{2n+2}\kappa^{2}\beta^{2}\left|F_{i}\right|.

We take
\kappa\leq 2^{-n-1}
small enough so that
2^{2n+2}\kappa^{2}<C^{2}/2
, and so we can hide the last term to the left-hand side. Then

\frac{C^{2}}{2}\left|F_{i}\right|\leq\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\int_{F_{i}}(M_{\hbar,6ar_{i}}(\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}_{i})_{L^{2}})\big(x,\hbar(x,t),t\big)^{2}dxdt\\
\leq\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\int_{\Delta_{i}}(M_{\hbar_{i},6ar_{i}}(\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}_{i})_{L^{2}})\big(x,\hbar_{i}(x,t),t\big)^{2}dxdt.

Notice that we changed
\hbar
to
\hbar_{i}
in the last inequality; this is valid because
\hbar_{i}=\hbar
on
F_{i}
. By the
L^{2}
boundedness of the localized maximal function, we obtain that

(10.47)

C^{\prime}\left|F_{i}\right|\leq\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\int_{10\Delta_{i}}(\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}_{i})_{L^{2}}(x,\hbar_{i}(x,t),t)^{2}dxdt.

We claim that

(10.48)

\int_{10\Delta_{i}}(\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}_{i})^{2}_{L^{2}}(x,\hbar_{i}(x,t),t)dxdt\leq C\int_{1/6}^{6}\int_{20\Delta_{i}}\left|\vec{\eta}-\vec{c}_{i}\right|^{2}(x,\theta\hbar_{i}(x,t),t)dxdtd\theta.

This has been established (in the elliptic case) in
[
16
, Lemma 5.6]
. The proof in the parabolic case is analogous, as it only uses properties of the function
\hbar_{i}
which are as stated in Lemma
10.10
.
These imply that around a boundary point
(x,t)
there is a boundary parabolic ball of radius
\sim\hbar_{i}(x,t)
such that

\hbar_{i}(y,s)\sim\hbar_{i}(x,t)
for all
(y,s)
that belong to this boundary parabolic ball.
Hence it follows that by Lemma
10.23
we get that

(10.49)

C^{\prime}\left|F_{i}\right|\leq\frac{C}{\beta^{2}}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})\iint_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\left|\nabla\vec{\eta}\right|^{2}x_{n}\,dx_{n}dtdx+\frac{C}{\beta^{2}r}\iint_{\mathcal{K_{\varepsilon}}}|\vec{\eta}-\vec{c_{i}}|^{2}\,dX\,dt\\
+\frac{\varepsilon}{\beta^{2}}\left(\|\tilde{N}_{a}\left((\vec{\eta}-\vec{c_{i}}){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\right)\|_{L^{2}(40\Delta_{i})}^{2}+\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}\,{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}})\|_{L^{2}(40a\Delta_{i})}^{2}+\|\tilde{N}_{a}(V{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}})\|_{L^{2}(40\Delta_{i})}^{2}\right).

Our goal is to bound the right-hand side of (
10.49
) by
C\gamma^{2}\beta^{2}\left|\Delta_{i}\right|
, which will imply the desired estimate (
10.40
) by summing over
i
.
To this end, we write

\iint_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\left|\nabla\vec{\eta}\right|^{2}x_{n}\,dx_{n}dtdx\leq C\int_{40\Delta_{i}}\iint_{\gamma_{a}(x,t)}\left|\nabla\vec{\eta}\right|^{2}{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}(y,y_{n},s)\frac{dydy_{n}ds}{y_{n}^{n}}dxdt.

When
(x,t)\in F_{i}
, it holds trivially that
\iint_{\gamma_{a}(x,t)}\left|\nabla\vec{\eta}\right|^{2}{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}(y,y_{n},s)\frac{dydy_{n}ds}{y_{n}^{n}}\leq S_{b}(\vec{\eta})(x,t)^{2}
.
When
(x,t)\in 40\Delta_{i}\setminus F_{i}
, by (
10.46
), we can find some
(x_{0},t_{0})\in F_{i}
so that

\iint_{\gamma_{a}(x,t)}\left|\nabla\vec{\eta}\right|^{2}{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}(y,y_{n},s)\frac{dydy_{n}ds}{y_{n}^{n}}\leq S_{b}(\vec{\eta})(x_{0},t_{0})^{2}
. Hence, by the definition of
E_{1,\beta}
, we conclude that

(10.50)

\iint_{\gamma_{a}(x,t)}\left|\nabla\vec{\eta}\right|^{2}{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}(y,y_{n},s)\frac{dydy_{n}ds}{y_{n}^{n}}\leq\varepsilon\gamma^{2}\beta^{2}\quad\text{for all }(x,t)\in 40\Delta_{i},

and so

(10.51)

\iint_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\left|\nabla\vec{\eta}\right|^{2}x_{n}\,dx_{n}dtdx\leq C\varepsilon\gamma^{2}\beta^{2}\left|\Delta_{i}\right|.

The terms
\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}\,{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}})\|_{L^{2}(40\Delta_{i})}^{2}
and
\|\tilde{N}_{a}(V{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}})\|_{L^{2}(40\Delta_{i})}^{2}
are controlled similarly. We note that for any
(x_{0},t_{0})\in 40\Delta_{i}
, there exists
(x_{0},t_{0})\in F_{i}
such that
\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}(x,t)\leq\tilde{N}_{b}(\vec{\eta})(x_{0},t_{0})
due to (
10.46
). Therefore, by the definition of
F_{i}
we get that

(10.52)

\varepsilon\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}\,{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}})\|_{L^{2}(40\Delta_{i})}^{2}+\varepsilon\|\tilde{N}_{a}(V{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}})\|_{L^{2}(40\Delta_{i})}^{2}\leq C\gamma^{2}\beta^{2}\left|\Delta_{i}\right|.

For the term
\frac{1}{r_{i}}\iint_{\mathcal{K_{\varepsilon}}}|\vec{\eta}-\vec{c_{i}}|^{2}\,dX\,dt
, we use Poincaré inequality in variables
x
and
t
simultaneously to get that

\frac{1}{r_{i}}\iint_{\mathcal{K_{\varepsilon}}}|\vec{\eta}-\vec{c_{i}}|^{2}\,dX\,dt\leq Cr_{i}\iint_{\mathcal{K_{\varepsilon}}}\left(\left|\nabla\vec{\eta}\right|^{2}+r_{i}^{2}\left|\partial_{t}\vec{\eta}\right|^{2}\right)dX\,dt

\leq C\left(\varepsilon^{-1}\iint_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\left|\nabla\vec{\eta}\right|^{2}x_{n}+\varepsilon^{-3}\iint_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\left|\partial_{t}\vec{\eta}\right|^{2}x_{n}^{3}\right)\,dx_{n}dtdx,

where in the last step we recalled that
x_{n}>\varepsilon r_{i}
in
\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}
. So this term is again bounded by a bound similar to (
10.51
).

Finally, the term
\varepsilon\|\tilde{N}_{a}\left((\vec{\eta}-\vec{c_{i}}){\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\right)\|_{L^{2}(40\Delta_{i})}^{2}
is bounded by the sum of
\varepsilon\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}\,{\mathds{1}}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}})\|_{L^{2}(40\Delta_{i})}^{2}
, which is treated in (
10.52
), and
C\varepsilon\left|\vec{c}_{i}\right|^{2}\left|\Delta_{i}\right|
, which, since
\left|\vec{c}_{i}\right|\leq|\vec{\eta}_{L^{2}}|
, has the bound given in (
10.43
).
∎

The following good-
\lambda
estimate for
w_{n}
can be established using (
10.25
) in a similar way.

Lemma 10.53
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
and
a>0
as in Lemma
10.39
.
There exists
\kappa\in(0,1)
that depends only on
n
, constants
b>a
and
\gamma_{0}
that depend on
a
, and
C>0
that depends only on
n
and the ellipticity constants such that the following holds.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, for any
w_{n}
that satisfies (
10.38
), for any
\gamma\in(0,\gamma_{0})
,
\beta>0
, there holds

\left|\left\{\tilde{N}_{a}(w_{n})>\beta,\varepsilon^{-1/2}[S_{b}(w_{n})+\varepsilon^{-1}A_{b}(w_{n})]\leq\gamma\beta,\varepsilon^{1/2}\tilde{N}_{b}(w_{n})+M_{0}\,\tilde{N}_{b}(W)\leq\gamma\beta\right\}\right|\\
\leq C\gamma^{2}\left|\left\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}:M(\tilde{N}_{a}(w_{n}))(x,t)>\kappa\beta\right\}\right|,

where
M_{0}=(\left\|\mu\right\|_{C}+\|x_{n}|\nabla A_{\parallel}|\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2})^{1/2}(1+\|x_{n}|\nabla A_{\parallel}|\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2})^{1/2}
.

We can now establish Theorem
10.1
. Recall that we have assumed a priori that
\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty
. Since
\nabla_{x}u=V+\vec{\eta}
and
V
already enjoys the bound (
10.21
), it follows that
\|\tilde{N}(\vec{\eta})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty
. Consider any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
such that for
\left[\|\mu_{||}\|_{C}+\left\|x_{n}\left|\nabla_{x}A_{\parallel}\right|\right\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}}\right]\Big(1+\|x_{n}|\nabla A_{\parallel}|\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}}\Big)<\delta(\varepsilon)<\infty
Lemma
10.39
holds. Then

\left\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta})\right\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}^{p}=p\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu^{p-1}\left|\left\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}:\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta})(x,t)>\nu\right\}\right|d\nu\\
\leq p\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu^{p-1}\left|E_{1,\nu}\right|d\nu+p\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu^{p-1}\left|\left\{\varepsilon^{-1/2}[S_{b}(\vec{\eta})+{\varepsilon^{-1}A_{b}(\vec{\eta})}]>\gamma\nu\right\}\right|d\nu\\
+p\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu^{p-1}\left|\left\{\varepsilon^{1/2}\left(\tilde{N}_{b}(\vec{\eta})+\tilde{N}_{b}(V)\right)>\gamma\nu\right\}\right|d\nu,

where
E_{1,\nu}
is defined as in (
10.41
).
By Lemma
10.39
and change of variables, one has

\left\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta})\right\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}^{p}\leq C\gamma^{2}p\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu^{p-1}\left|\left\{M(\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}))>\kappa\nu\right\}\right|d\nu\\
+(\gamma\varepsilon^{1/2}/2)^{-p}p\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu^{p-1}\left|\left\{S_{b}(\vec{\eta})>\nu\right\}\right|d\nu+(\gamma\varepsilon^{3/2}/2)^{-p}p\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu^{p-1}\left|\left\{A_{b}(\vec{\eta})>\nu\right\}\right|d\nu\\
+(\gamma\varepsilon^{-1/2}/2)^{-p}p\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu^{p-1}\left|\left\{\tilde{N}_{b}(\vec{\eta})>\nu\right\}\right|d\nu+(\gamma\varepsilon^{-1/2}/2)^{-p}p\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu^{p-1}\left|\left\{\tilde{N}_{b}(V)>\nu\right\}\right|d\nu\\
=\kappa^{-p}\gamma^{2}p\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu^{p-1}\left|\left\{M(\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}))>\nu\right\}\right|d\nu\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\\
+C(\gamma,\varepsilon)[\left\|S_{b}(\vec{\eta})\right\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\left\|A_{b}(\vec{\eta})\right\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\|\tilde{N}_{b}(V)\|_{L^{p}}^{p}]+(\gamma\varepsilon^{-1/2}/2)^{-p}\|\tilde{N}_{b}(\vec{\eta})\|_{L^{p}}^{p}\\
=\kappa^{-p}\gamma^{2}\|M(\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta}))\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+(\gamma\varepsilon^{-1/2}/2)^{-p}\|\tilde{N}_{b}(\vec{\eta})\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+C(\gamma,\varepsilon)[\left\|S_{b}(\vec{\eta})\right\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\left\|A_{b}(\vec{\eta})\right\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\|\tilde{N}_{b}(V)\|_{L^{p}}^{p}].

As the maximal function
M
is
L^{p}
bounded for
p>1
the first term of the last line is further bounded by

C(p)\kappa^{-p}\gamma^{2}\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta})\|_{L^{p}}^{p}
.
Choosing
\gamma\in(0,1)
sufficient small so that
C(p)\kappa^{-p}\gamma^{2}<1/4
, as well as
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
so small such that
(\gamma/2)^{-p}\varepsilon^{p/2}\|\tilde{N}_{b}(\vec{\eta})\|_{L^{p}}^{p}<1/4\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta})\|_{L^{p}}^{p}

one can hide
\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta})\|_{L^{p}}^{p}
on the left-hand side and obtain the estimate:

\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\vec{\eta})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}^{p}\leq C(\gamma,\varepsilon)[\left\|S_{b}(\vec{\eta})\right\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\left\|A_{b}(\vec{\eta})\right\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\|\tilde{N}_{b}(V)\|_{L^{p}}^{p}].

As
\gamma,\,\varepsilon
are fixed now we drop the dependence of the constant on them. Writing
\nabla_{x}u=\vec{\eta}+V
it follows that

\|\tilde{N}_{a}(\nabla_{x}u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}^{p}\leq C\left[\left\|S_{b}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\left\|A_{b}(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\left\|S_{b}(V)\right\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\left\|A_{b}(V)\right\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\|\tilde{N}_{b}(V)\|_{L^{p}}^{p}\right],

and hence

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla_{x}u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}^{p}\leq C\left[\left\|S(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\left\|A(\nabla u)\right\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+\|\nabla_{x}f\|_{L^{p}}^{p}\right],

given (
10.21
). Note that (
10.21
) does not contain an estimate for
\left\|A(V)\right\|_{L^{p}}

but that follows form Lemma 4.4 of
[
15
]
where the Cacciopoli inequality for second gradient is shown.
The final ingredient in the proof (
10.2
) is the estimate for
\|\tilde{N}(\partial_{x_{n}}u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}
.
This relies on Lemma
10.53
. Tracking the constant
M_{0}
, we obtain that

\|\tilde{N}(\partial_{x_{n}}u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C(\left\|\mu\right\|^{1/2}_{C}+\|x_{n}|\nabla A_{\parallel}|\|_{L^{\infty}})(1+\|x_{n}|\nabla A_{\parallel}|\|_{L^{\infty}})\|\tilde{N}(\nabla_{x}u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}

+C\|{S}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)},

which gives (
10.2
).∎

11.
Localization on bounded Lipschitz cylinders

In this section we introduce a localization technique that will allow us to establish solvability of the Regularity problem on Lipschitz cylinders of the form
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
for a bounded Lipschitz domain

\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
using previously established solvability on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. We note that this localization is not needed in the case when
\mathcal{O}
is an unbounded Lipschitz domain
of the form
\{(x^{\prime},x_{n}):\,x_{n}>\phi(x^{\prime})\}
for a Lipschitz function
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
, as in such a case we can consider a pullback map
\rho:\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
and transfer the parabolic PDE from
\Omega
to

\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
directly without any localization. We will return to this point when we discuss the map
\rho

is more detail where we will show how to solve the unbounded case.

In what follows, we borrow ideas from Section 5.2 of
[
22
]
where a similar decomposition was considered. Recalling the definition of the Lipschitz domain

\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
from Definition
2.10
we thus restrict ourselves to the case

\diam(\mathcal{O})<\infty
.

A simple compactness argument implies that there exists
s_{0}<1
such that the
\ell
-cylinders
s_{0}{\mathbb{Z}}_{j}
still cover
\partial\mathcal{O}
. Thus by making the scale
r_{0}
and diameter
d
smaller if necessary (at the expense of increased
N
) we may without loss of generality assume that the union of
(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}
covers
\partial\mathcal{O}
.

By rescaling the PDE, it may also be assumed that the
\ell
-cylinders
{\mathbb{Z}}_{j}
in the definition above have diameter
d=1
. Let
N,\,C_{0}
be as above and hence
there are
N
such
\ell
-cylinders
(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}
needed to cover the boundary
\partial\mathcal{O}
.

It follows that there exists a partition of unity
(\varphi_{j})_{j=1}^{N}
such that each
\varphi_{j}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, is supported in

(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}
, and altogether,

\sum_{j=1}^{N}\varphi_{j}(x)=1,\qquad\mbox{ for all }\qquad x\in\left\{x\in\mathcal{O}:\,\mathrm{dist}\left(x,{\partial\mathcal{O}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\right\}.

As each
\varphi_{j}
is smooth, and there are
N
of them, it follows that
\|\nabla\varphi_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq M
for some
M>0
for all
j
. This completes
the description of the partition of the spatial component of our domain
\Omega
.

Denote by
\Omega_{i}:=\mathcal{O}\times(i,i+1]

and let
\Delta_{i}=\partial\mathcal{O}\times(i,i+1]
for
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. There exists a nonnegative
C^{1}
function
\psi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, supp
\psi\subset[-1,2]
such that for

\psi_{i}(\cdot)=\psi(\cdot+i),\qquad|\psi^{\prime}|\lesssim 1,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi=1,\qquad\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\psi_{i}\equiv 1.

Clearly,
\|D^{1/2}_{t}\psi\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim 1
, for each
1<r<\infty
.

Consider now an arbitrary
f\in\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)
for some
p>1
, and the solution to
\mathcal{L}u=0
with
u\big|_{\partial\Omega}=f
.
Set

f_{\Delta_{i}}:=\fint_{{\Delta}_{i}}f.

Finally, let
\Psi_{k}:=\sum_{i\geq k}\psi_{i}
and note that
\psi_{i}=\Psi_{i+1}-\Psi_{i}
and

\Psi_{k}\equiv 1
on
[i+2,\infty)
.

For all
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
j\in\{1,2,\dots,N\}
we denote by
w_{ij}
the solution of the boundary value problem

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}w_{ij}=0,&\text{in }\Omega,\\
w_{ij}=(f-f_{\Delta_{i}})\psi_{i}\varphi_{j}&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\end{cases}

If
w:=\sum_{i,j}w_{ij}
then
v:=u-w
solves
\mathcal{L}v=0

with boundary data

\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}f_{\Delta_{i}}\psi_{i}=\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}f_{\Delta_{i}}(\Psi_{i+1}-\Psi_{i})=\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(f_{\Delta_{i-1}}-f_{\Delta_{i}})\Psi_{i}.

Thus we can write
v=\sum_{i,j}v_{ij}
where
v_{ij}
solves

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}v_{ij}=0,&\text{in }\Omega,\\
v_{ij}=(f_{\Delta_{i-1}}-f_{\Delta_{i}})\Psi_{i}\varphi_{j}&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

It follows that we have decomposed the solution
u
to
\mathcal{L}u=0
with
u\big|_{\partial\Omega}=f
into

u=\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(v_{ij}+w_{ij}),

such that
w_{ij}\big|_{\partial\Omega}
is supported only on
(\partial\mathcal{O}\cap(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j})\times(i-1,i+2)
and
v_{ij}\big|_{\partial\Omega}
is supported in
(\partial\mathcal{O}\cap(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j})\times(i-1,\infty)
.

Let us also define
\tilde{v}_{ij}
, such that

\tilde{v}_{ij}
solves

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}\tilde{v}_{ij}=0,&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\tilde{v}_{ij}=(f_{\Delta_{i-1}}-f_{\Delta_{i}})\Psi_{i}(1-\Psi_{i+20})\varphi_{j}&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{cases}

To motivate this, observe first that

v_{ij}\big|_{\partial\Omega}(P,\tau)=\tilde{v}_{ij}\big|_{\partial\Omega}(P,\tau)
for
\tau\leq i+15
and therefore
\tilde{N}(\nabla v_{ij})(P,\tau)=\tilde{N}(\nabla\tilde{v}_{ij})(P,\tau)
for all
\tau\leq i+10
, using the assumption on the diameter of the cylinders.
However,
\tilde{v}_{ij}
is much more similar to
w_{ij}
, as both have compactly supported boundary data and
the half derivative of

\Psi_{i}(1-\Psi_{i+20})
has faster decay than the half derivative of
\Psi_{i}
.

Let us study the properties of
N(\nabla v_{ij})
and
N(\nabla w_{ij})
. Without loss of generality, let

v
be either
v_{ij}
or
w_{ij}
for some
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. It follows that
v\equiv 0
for
t\leq i-1
. Thus

\tilde{N}(\nabla v)(P,\tau)\equiv 0
for all
\tau\leq i-5
.

When
i-5<\tau<i+10
we may study
\tilde{N}(\nabla v)(P,\tau)
by considering the
(R_{L})_{p}
boundary value problem with datum
supported in a single coordinate patch. We address that later, and this is the heart of the matter. In this time range we may use

\tilde{v}_{ij}
instead of
v_{ij}
, as the solutions coincide when
\tau<i+15
.

Finally, for
\tau\geq i+10
we will use the fact that after summing in
j
, the solutions
\nabla v_{i}=\sum_{j}\nabla v_{ij}
decay exponentially.
This is a consequence of the following lemma from
[
22
]
.

Lemma 11.1

(
[
22
, Lemma 5.10]
)
.

Let
p\geq 1
and
assume that the Dirichlet problem for the adjoint PDE
(D)_{p^{\prime}}^{*}
is solvable. (When
p=1
we assume

(D)_{q}^{*}
for some
q>1
). Let

v
be a solution to
\mathcal{L}v=0
and
suppose that
v
is constant on
\partial\mathcal{O}\times(i+2,\infty)
.
There exists an
\alpha>0
such that for all
k>i+9
we have that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{N}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{k})}\lesssim e^{-\alpha|i-k|}\|{\tilde{N}}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i+3})}.

Notice that the assumption that
(D)_{p^{\prime}}^{*}
is solvable is harmless because under the conditions of the matrix
A
in Theorem
1.6
, the parabolic measure satisfies
\omega_{\mathcal{L}^{*}}\in A_{\infty}(d\sigma)
for the adjoint operator, which is a consequence of the paper
[
19
]
(see also
[
13
,
15
]
for small Carleson case). The
A_{\infty}
condition implies that there is some
p_{0}\in(1,\infty)
such that
(D)_{p^{\prime}}^{*}
is solvable for all
p\in(0,p_{0})

(and thus
p^{\prime}\in(p_{0}^{\prime},\infty)
). We are going to use this fact several times.

Applying Lemma
11.1
to
v_{i}=\sum_{j}v_{ij}
and
w_{i}=\sum_{j}w_{ij}
we have for all
k>i+9
:

(11.2)

\int_{\Delta_{k}}{\tilde{N}}(\nabla v_{i})^{p}\lesssim e^{-\alpha p|i-k|}\sum_{j}\|{\tilde{N}}(\nabla v_{ij})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i+3})}^{p},\quad\int_{\Delta_{k}}{\tilde{N}}(\nabla w_{i})^{p}\lesssim e^{-\alpha p|i-k|}\sum_{j}\|{\tilde{N}}(\nabla w_{ij})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i+3})}^{p}.

With this in place it remains to establish estimates for
\|{\tilde{N}}(\nabla{v}_{ij})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{k})}=\|{\tilde{N}}(\nabla\tilde{v}_{ij})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{k})}

and
\|{\tilde{N}}(\nabla{w}_{ij})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{k})}
for
k=i-5,i-4,\dots,i+9
.

Let us study the boundary data of
\tilde{v}_{ij}
and
w_{ij}
. We start with
w_{ij}
. We recall Proposition 2.4 of
[
22
]
, which is stated in
greater generality than we consider here (for chord-arc domains).

Proposition 11.3
.

Let
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
1
-sided chord-arc domain. Assume that for some
p>1

f\in\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)
.
There exists
h\in L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
with
\|h\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}
for which the following holds:

Consider any parabolic ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
of radius
r
centered at the boundary
\partial\Omega
, let
\Delta=B\cap\partial\Omega
and
\varphi
be a
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R})
cutoff function supported on
3B
with
\|\partial_{t}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim r^{-2}
and
\|\nabla_{x}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim r^{-1}
.
Then we have the following estimate:

\|(f-\textstyle\fint_{\Delta}f)\varphi\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{p}(32\Delta)}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(32\Delta)}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.

Applying this result to

(1/2)\Delta_{ij}:=((1/4){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}\cap\partial\mathcal{O})\times(i,i+1)
, (and also defining

\Delta_{ij}:=((1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}\cap\partial\mathcal{O})\times(i-1,i+2)
),
we see that

\|(f-\textstyle\fint_{(1/2)\Delta_{ij}}f)\psi_{i}\varphi_{j}\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{ij})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{ij})},

and hence

(11.4)

\|(f-\textstyle\fint_{\Delta_{i}}f)\psi_{i}\varphi_{j}\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{ij})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{ij})}+|\fint_{(1/2)\Delta_{ij}}f-\fint_{\Delta_{i}}f|,

where we use the fact that
D^{1/2}_{t}(\psi_{i}\varphi_{j})=\varphi_{j}D^{1/2}_{t}\psi_{i}
has
L^{p}
norm of size
O(1)
. A similar statement holds for boundary data of
\tilde{v}_{ij}
where we get that

(11.5)

\|(\textstyle\fint_{\Delta_{i+1}}f-\fint_{\Delta_{i}}f)\Psi_{i}(1-\Psi_{i+20})\varphi_{j}\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim|\fint_{\Delta_{i+1}}f-\fint_{\Delta_{i}}f|,

using the fact that
D^{1/2}_{t}(\Psi_{i}(1-\Psi_{i+20})\varphi_{j})=\varphi_{j}D^{1/2}_{t}(\Psi_{i}(1-\Psi_{i+20}))
also has
L^{p}
norm
of the size
O(1)
for all
p>1
.

It is further shown in
[
22
]
that the difference of averages on the right-hand side of (
11.5
) enjoy estimates in term of the sharp maximal function of
f
. Actually, the way
h
in Proposition
11.3
was defined in
[
22
]
used
f^{\sharp}
with the bound
\|M(f^{\sharp})\|_{L^{p}}\leq\|h\|_{L^{p}}

and hence by (5.17) of
[
22
]
:

(11.6)

\|(\textstyle\fint_{\Delta_{i+1}}f-\fint_{\Delta_{i}}f)\Psi_{i}(1-\Psi_{i+20})\varphi_{j}\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{i})},

with analogous estimate for

(11.7)

\|(f-\textstyle\fint_{(1/2)\Delta_{i}}f)\psi_{i}\varphi_{j}\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{i})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{ij})}.

Recall now the following theorem from
[
22
]
.

Theorem 11.8

(
[
22
, Theorem 6.52]
)
.

Assume that
\omega\in A_{\infty}(d\sigma)
, where
\omega
is the parabolic measure of the operator
\mathcal{L}^{*}=\partial_{t}+\mathrm{div}(A^{T}\nabla\cdot)
on a Lipschitz cylinder
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
.
Then for any
p\in(1,\infty]
the following holds. There exists
C(p)>0
such that for any
homogeneous
(1,1/2,p)
-atom associated to a parabolic ball
\Delta\subset\partial\Omega

the weak solution
u
of the equation
\mathcal{L}u=-\partial_{t}u+\mathrm{div}(A\nabla u)=0
with boundary datum
f
satisfies the estimate

(11.9)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{1}(\partial\Omega\backslash 2\Delta)}\leq C(p).

Without going into the details of what a
(1,1/2,p)
-atom is, suffice to say that it is a localised function
f
with support in a boundary ball
\Delta
, and that
has an appropriately defined scaling of
L^{p}
norms of
\nabla f
and
D^{1/2}_{t}f
. Given (
11.6
)
and (
11.7
), the functions of the left-hand side are localised and while not
(1,1/2,p)
-atoms, yet are multiples of atoms. Hence we can apply Theorem
11.8
to both the
w_{ij}
and the
\tilde{v}_{ij}
, respecting the right scaling which takes into account the size of the support of the boundary balls.

Starting with (
11.7
),
the function
(f-\textstyle\fint_{(1/2)\Delta_{i}}f)\psi_{i}\varphi_{j}

is supported in

\Delta_{ij}=((1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}\cap\partial\mathcal{O})\times(i-1,i+2)
and hence (
11.9
) will give a bound on

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla w_{ij})\|_{L^{1}(\partial\Omega\backslash 2\Delta_{ij})}
, where

2\Delta_{ij}=({\mathbb{Z}}_{j}\cap\partial\mathcal{O})\times(i-3,i+4)
.
Given that our atoms are defined on balls of size
O(1)
, which simplifies the matter of scaling, we therefore obtain that

(11.10)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla w_{ij})\|_{L^{1}(\partial\Omega\backslash 2\Delta_{ij})}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{i})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{ij})},

and similarly, for
\tilde{v}_{ij}
whose boundary data is supported on

((1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}\cap\partial\mathcal{O})\times(i-1,i+21),

(11.11)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla\tilde{v}_{ij})\|_{L^{1}(\partial\Omega\backslash[({\mathbb{Z}}_{j}\cap\mathcal{O})\cap(i-4,i+25)])}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{i})}.

It remains to consider the key estimates at the points near the support of the boundary data of
\tilde{v}_{ij}
and
w_{ij}
, addressed in the following result.

Theorem 11.12
.

Let
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
be as above and assume that the coefficients of the matrix
A
defining the operator
\mathcal{L}=-\partial_{t}+\mathrm{div}(A\nabla\cdot)
satisfy either (1) or (2) of Theorem
1.6
.
Then there exists
p>1
such that the following holds.

For any boundary ball
\Delta
of size
O(1)
such that
\Delta\subset[(1/2){\mathbb{Z}}_{j}\cap\partial\mathcal{O}]\times(\tau-11,\tau+11)
, where
{\mathbb{Z}}_{j}

is one of the
\ell
-cylinders defining the Lipschitz domain
\mathcal{O}
, and for any
f\in\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)
supported in
\Delta
and any solution
v
of
\mathcal{L}v=0
in
\Omega
with
v=f
on
\partial\Omega
,
we have that

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(3\Delta)}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.

Note that an important aspect of the above Lemma is that fact that
3\Delta
belongs to one of the coordinate patches that define the boundary
\partial\Omega
. Thus the support of both
f
and
\tilde{N}(\nabla v)
is localized.

Assuming for the moment this theorem holds, we will pull these pieces together to obtain solvability of the
L^{p}
Regularity problem on the bounded Lipschitz cylinder
\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
.
Here
p>1
is the smaller of
p
as in Theorem
11.12
and
\omega\in B_{p}(d\sigma)
where
\omega

is the parabolic measure of the adjoint operator
\mathcal{L}^{*}
. Consider such
p>1
.

Firstly, thanks to Theorem
11.12
, (
11.6
), and (
11.7
), we have that

(11.13)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla w_{ij})\|_{L^{p}(3\Delta_{ij})}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{i})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{ij})},

and similarly, for
\tilde{v}_{ij}
:

(11.14)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla\tilde{v}_{ij})\|_{L^{p}((2{\mathbb{Z}}_{j}\cap\mathcal{O})\cap(i-10,i+50)])}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{i})}.

Next, we have the
L^{1}
estimates (
11.10
)-(
11.11
) but these can be improved to
L^{p}
thanks to the following reverse Hölder result
from
[
22
]
.

Theorem 11.15
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and suppose that
(D_{\mathcal{L}^{*}})_{p^{\prime}}
is solvable
and suppose that
u
is a weak solution of
\mathcal{L}u=0
with
u=0

on
\tilde{\Delta}:=80\Delta
where
\Delta=\Delta_{r}(P_{0},\tau_{0})
, with
r\lesssim\mbox{diam}(\mathcal{O})
.
Then

\Big(\fint_{\Delta}{\tilde{N}}(\nabla u)^{p}\Big)^{1/p}\lesssim\fint_{\tilde{\Delta}}{\tilde{N}}(\nabla u).

In particular, consider a cover of the set
\mathcal{G}=(\Delta_{i-5}\cup\Delta_{i-4}\cup\dots\Delta_{i+9})\setminus 3\Delta_{ij}=(\partial\mathcal{O}\times(i-5,i+10])\setminus 3\Delta_{ij}
.
Because
w_{ij}
on the boundary is supported in
\Delta_{ij}
each point in this set is at least a distance
r\sim O(1)
away from this support. Furthermore, the area of this set is also of size
O(1)
and therefore there will be a fixed finite number
K
that only depends on the geometry of
\mathcal{O}
, but not on
w_{ij}
, such that

\mathcal{G}
can be covered by at most
K
parabolic boundary balls
B_{k}
of size
r/80
with
80B_{k}\cap 2\Delta_{ij}=\emptyset
. Hence by the result above, on each
B_{k}
:

\int_{B_{k}}{\tilde{N}}(\nabla w_{ij})^{p}\lesssim\left(\int_{80B_{k}}{\tilde{N}}(\nabla w_{ij})\right)^{p}\leq\left(\int_{\partial\Omega\setminus 2\Delta_{ij}}{\tilde{N}}(\nabla w_{ij})\right)^{p}\lesssim\|h\|^{p}_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{i})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|^{p}_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{ij})},

by (
11.10
).
Summing over all
k\leq K
yields

\int_{(\Delta_{i-5}\cup\Delta_{i-4}\cup\dots\Delta_{i+9})\setminus 3\Delta_{ij}}{\tilde{N}}(\nabla w_{ij})^{p}\lesssim\|h\|^{p}_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{i})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|^{p}_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{ij})},

and after combining this estimate with (
11.13
) and summing over all
j\leq N
we get that:

(11.16)

\int_{\Delta_{i-5}\cup\Delta_{i-4}\cup\dots\Delta_{i+9}}{\tilde{N}}(\nabla w_{i})^{p}\lesssim\|h\|^{p}_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{i})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|^{p}_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{i})}.

Recall that
w_{i}=\sum_{j\leq N}w_{ij}
.
By an analogous argument we get for
\tilde{v}_{i}=\sum_{j\leq N}\tilde{v}_{ij}
(and hence also for
{v}_{i}
as they coincide of this portion of the domain):

(11.17)

\int_{\Delta_{i-5}\cup\Delta_{i-4}\cup\dots\Delta_{i+9}}{\tilde{N}}(\nabla{v}_{i})^{p}=\int_{\Delta_{i-5}\cup\Delta_{i-4}\cup\dots\Delta_{i+9}}{\tilde{N}}(\nabla\tilde{v}_{i})^{p}\lesssim\|h\|^{p}_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{i})}.

We are ready to add up the estimates to obtain a bound for the
L^{p}
norm of
u
. Fix
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}

and consider
\tilde{N}(\nabla u)
on
\Delta_{i}
. Since
\tilde{N}
is sub-additive, clearly

\tilde{N}(\nabla u)(P,\tau)\leq\sum_{k}\left[\tilde{N}(\nabla v_{k})+\tilde{N}(\nabla w_{k})\right](P,\tau),

and therefore

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}\leq\sum_{k}\left[\|\tilde{N}(\nabla v_{k})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}+\|\tilde{N}(\nabla w_{k})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}\right]

\leq\sum_{k=i-9}^{i+5}\left[\|\tilde{N}(\nabla v_{k})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}+\|\tilde{N}(\nabla w_{k})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}\right]+\sum_{k<i-9}\left[\|\tilde{N}(\nabla v_{k})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}+\|\tilde{N}(\nabla w_{k})\|_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}\right]

\lesssim\sum_{k=i-9}^{i+5}\left[\|h\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}\right]+\sum_{k<i-9}e^{-\alpha|i-k|}\left[\|h\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}\right],

using (
11.16
), (
11.17
) and (
11.2
). We raise both sides to the
p
-th power. It follows that

\hskip-42.67912pt\|\tilde{N}[\nabla u]\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}\lesssim\sum_{k=i-9}^{i+5}\left[\|h\|^{p}_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|^{p}_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}\right]

\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left(\sum_{k<i-9}e^{-\alpha|i-k|}\left[\|h\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}\right]\right)^{p}.

For the last term we use Hölder’s inequality for series. We split the exponential into two terms

e^{-\alpha|i-k|}=e^{-\alpha|i-k|/p^{\prime}}e^{-\alpha|i-k|/p}

to obtain

\left(\sum_{k<i-9}e^{-\alpha|i-k|}\left[\|h\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}\right]\right)^{p}\leq\left(\sum_{k<i-9}\left(e^{-\alpha|i-k|/p^{\prime}}\right)^{p^{\prime}}\right)^{p/p^{\prime}}\times

\qquad\qquad\qquad\sum_{k<i-9}e^{-\alpha|i-k|}\left[\|h\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}\right]^{p}

\leq C(p,\alpha)\sum_{k<i-9}e^{-\alpha|i-k|}\left[\|h\|^{p}_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|^{p}_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{k})}\right].

Now we sum over all indices
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Since

\|\tilde{N}[\nabla u]\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}=\sum_{i}\|N[\nabla u]\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Delta_{i})}
we obtain after rearranging the summation order:

(11.18)

\|\tilde{N}[\nabla u]\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(1+\sum_{k>9}e^{-\alpha k})\left[\|h\|^{p}_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{i})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}f\|^{p}_{L^{p}(16\Delta_{i})}\right]

We use the finite overlap of the balls
32\Delta_{i}
which allows us to claim that

\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\|h\|^{p}_{L^{p}(32\Delta_{i})}\lesssim\|h\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)},

with a similar statement holding for
D^{1/2}_{t}f
.
For both
h
and
D^{1/2}_{t}f
we have bounds on their
L^{p}
norms by
\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}
. In summary, we have that

(11.19)

\|\tilde{N}[\nabla u]\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|f\|^{p}_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)},

proving solvability of the Regularity problem for this particular
p>1
. Hence, with the exception of the proof of Theorem
11.12
we are done, as this argument extends the solvability result stated in Theorem
1.6
to
bounded Lipschitz cylinders. The second part of the Theorem
11.12
claiming duality of ranges of solvability then follows from Lemma
3.20
.
∎

11.1.
Proof of Theorem
11.12
.

In the section above we have reduced matters to solvability of Regularity problem for data localized to a parabolic ball which additionally sits inside one of the coordinate patches that describes the boundary
\partial\mathcal{O}

times the time direction. In the local coordinates of this particular patch on (
8{\mathbb{Z}}
) we may assume the following:

\phi:\{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}:\,|x^{\prime}|\leq 8\}\to\mathbb{R}
is a Lipschitz function with Lipschitz constant
\ell
with
\phi(0)=0

and in the coordinates
(x^{\prime},x_{n},t)
the portion of domain
\Omega
that belongs to
8{\mathbb{Z}}\times\mathbb{R}
can be written
as

\Omega\cap(8{\mathbb{Z}}\times\mathbb{R})=\{(x^{\prime},x_{n},t):\,,|x^{\prime}|\leq 8,\,\phi(x^{\prime})<x_{n}<8(\ell+1),\,t\in\mathbb{R}\},

with
\partial\Omega\cap(8{\mathbb{Z}}\times\mathbb{R})
being the graph of function
\phi
times
\mathbb{R}
.
Let us now choose a new function
\tilde{\phi}:\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\to\mathbb{R}
with Lipschitz constant at most
3\ell
having the following properties:

\tilde{\phi}(x^{\prime})={\phi}(x^{\prime})\quad\mbox{for all }|x^{\prime}|\leq 6,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\tilde{\phi}(x^{\prime})=0\quad\mbox{for all }|x^{\prime}|\geq 8.

This can be done trivially by multiplying
\phi
by a cutoff function.

We similarly extend the coefficients of our equation. Let us write the parabolic PDE for a solution
v
to
\mathcal{L}v=0

on
\Omega=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}

with boundary datum
f
supported in the ball

\Delta=[(1/2)Z_{j}\cap\partial\mathcal{O}]\times(-11,11),

where we have without loss of generality shifted the support of our datum to
|t|<11
. In these local coordinates the PDE has the form

-\partial_{t}v+\mathrm{div}(A\nabla v)=0,\qquad\mbox{where $A$ in uniformly elliptic in }[8{\mathbb{Z}}\cap\mathcal{O}]\times\mathbb{R},

and satisfies the Carleson condition as in Theorem
1.6
.

We would like to extend the PDE to the domain
\tilde{\Omega}:=\{(x^{\prime},x_{n},t):\,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\,x_{n}>\tilde{\phi}(x^{\prime}),\,t\in\mathbb{R}\}
such that
the PDE will coincide with the original PDE in the set
[4{\mathbb{Z}}\cap\mathcal{O}]\times\mathbb{R}
, and so that the new matrix
\tilde{A}

will be uniformly elliptic and satisfy the Carleson condition of Theorem
1.6
with respect to the boundary of
the domain
\partial\tilde{\Omega}
.
This again is trivial, as we may consider a partition of unity
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
subordinate to the
cover of
\{(x^{\prime},x_{n}):\,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\,x_{n}>\tilde{\phi}(x^{\prime})\}
by the sets
6{\mathbb{Z}}\cap\mathcal{O}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus[4{\mathbb{Z}}\cap\mathcal{O}]
with new matrix
\tilde{A}
defined by

\eta_{1}(x^{\prime},x_{n})A(x,x_{n},t)+\eta_{2}(x^{\prime},x_{n})I,\qquad\mbox{for all }\{(x^{\prime},x_{n},t):\,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\,x_{n}>\tilde{\phi}(x^{\prime}),\,t\in\mathbb{R}\}.

When
|x^{\prime}|\leq 4
and
x_{n}<4(\ell+1)
this preserves the property that
\tilde{A}=A
, while away from
|x^{\prime}|\geq 8
or
x_{n}>8(\ell+1)
the matrix is simply the identity matrix and thus trivially satisfies both ellipticity and Carleson condition.

Hence it is possible to consider now the solution
u
to the PDE

(11.20)

-\partial_{t}u+\mathrm{div}(\tilde{A}\nabla u)=0\qquad\mbox{on }\tilde{\Omega},\quad\mbox{ with }u\big|_{\partial\tilde{\Omega}}=f.

Observe that while the solution
v
is only defined in our coordinates
on the set
(8{\mathbb{Z}}\cap\mathcal{O})\times\mathbb{R}
, the solution
u
is defined on the whole set
\tilde{\Omega}
.
Let us establish first solvability of the PDE (
11.20
).

Consider now a bijective bi-Lipschitz map
\Psi:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\to\{(x^{\prime},x_{n}):\,x_{n}>\tilde{\phi}(x^{\prime})\}
.
The natural choice here is the map due to Dahlberg, Kenig, Nečas, Stein
(see for example
[
9
]
or
[
46
]
) defined as

(11.21)

\Psi(x^{\prime},x_{n})=(x^{\prime},c_{0}x_{n}+(\theta_{x_{n}}*\tilde{\phi})(x^{\prime})),

where
(\theta_{s})_{s>0}
is smooth compactly supported approximate
identity and
c_{0}
can be chosen large enough (depending only on

\|\nabla\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n-1})}
) so that
\Psi
is
one to one.

Extend this map to

(11.22)

\rho(x,x_{n},t)=(\Psi(x^{\prime},x_{n}),t),

a map from
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
to
\tilde{\Omega}
, the domain on which we consider (
11.20
).

Then

U=u\circ\rho,\quad\text{solves the parabolic PDE}\quad\mathcal{L}_{1}U=0\text{ on }{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}

where

\mathcal{L}_{1}=-\partial_{t}+\text{div}(B\nabla\cdot),

where
B
is a new matrix obtained from the original matrix
\tilde{A}
.
Also,
U=\widetilde{f}
on
\partial\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\right)
where
\widetilde{f}=f\circ\rho|_{x_{n}=0}
.

This type of mapping has been studied extensively, in the parabolic setting in
[
36
]
. However, our map is simpler than the one in
[
36
]

as it is constant in
t
. Crucially, as shown in
[
36
]
, the new matrix
B
as above inherits the Carleson condition that
\tilde{A}
satisfies with perhaps a larger constant (as the Carleson norm also depends on
\|\nabla\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n-1})}
). Furthermore as
\Psi
is
t
-independent, the above change of variables does not introduce any first order (drift) term.

It follows that the PDE for
U
is of the type we have considered in Theorem
1.6
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}

for which the solvability has been established earlier.
Note that the matrix
B
inherits its ellipticity from
\tilde{A}
and hence
A
, and that it also satisfies Carleson condition with norm that only depends on the Carleson condition of the original matrix
A
and
\ell
. We can then apply Theorem
1.6
for
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
to the operator
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, which implies that there exists a uniform
p_{0}=p_{0}(A,\lambda,\Lambda,\ell,n)>1
such that for all
1<p<p_{0}

the
L^{p}
Regularity problem for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{1}
is solvable on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
and for some
C=C(A,\lambda,\Lambda,\ell,n,p)>0
, we have that

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla U)\|_{L^{p}(\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}))}\leq C\|\widetilde{f}\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}))}.

As
U=u\circ\rho
where
\rho
is bi-Lipschitz the same estimate also holds for
u
on the domain
\tilde{\Omega}
. Thus

(11.23)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla u)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\tilde{\Omega})}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\tilde{\Omega})}.

We now want to deduce the solvability of the Regularity problem for
v
from solvability of the related PDE for
u
. Recall that we know that on the set
(4{\mathbb{Z}}\cap\mathcal{O})\times\mathbb{R}
the two PDEs (for
u
and
v
)
we consider coincide and hence we have for
w=u-v
the following:

-\partial_{t}w+\mathrm{div}(\tilde{A}\nabla w)=0,\quad\mbox{on}\quad[4{\mathbb{Z}}\cap\mathcal{O}]\times\mathbb{R}.

We want to further restrict the domain on which we consider
w
. Firstly, clearly
w\equiv 0
for
t<-11
as
f
is not supported there. Secondly, as we may use Theorem
11.8
directly on
v
on the domain

\Omega
. It follows that

(11.24)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{1}(\partial\Omega\setminus 2\Delta)}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.

Hence we have all required estimates for
t>2\times 11=22
from above as we may combine the estimate
(
11.24
) with Theorem
11.15
and the exponential decay (Lemma
11.1
) to conclude that

(11.25)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega\cap\{t\geq 22\})}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.

There remains only the interval
-11<t<22
to consider.

Consider first the set
\mathcal{S}=(\partial\Omega\setminus 3\Delta)\cap\{-11<\tau<22\}
. When
(x,t)\in\mathcal{S}
, we have
f\equiv 0
(and even on an enlarged) portion of boundary), and hence an argument similar to the one following Theorem
11.15
applies.

\mathcal{S}
can be covered by finite collection of balls
B_{k}
with
80B_{k}\cap 2\Delta=\emptyset

and hence we may again use Theorem
11.15
and the estimate
(
11.24
) to obtain

(11.26)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{S})}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.

We also consider

\tilde{\Omega}^{s}:=(s{\mathbb{Z}}\cap\mathcal{O})\times\mathbb{R}\qquad\mbox{for }3\leq s\leq 4,

thus for each such
s
the domain
\tilde{\Omega}^{s}
is an unbounded cylinder and on
\tilde{\Omega}^{s}
the PDE given above for
w
holds as both
u
and
v
solves PDEs with identical coefficients.

Considering the boundaries of
\tilde{\Omega}^{s}
for
3\leq s\leq 4
we see that

(x,t)\in\partial\tilde{\Omega}^{s}\cap\partial\tilde{\Omega},\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad w(x,t)=0,

which follows from the fact that
u=v=f
on the
\partial\tilde{\Omega}
portion of the boundary. Given the definition of
\tilde{\Omega}^{s}
, its lateral boundary has two more components:

(11.27)

\{(x^{\prime},x_{n}):\,|x^{\prime}|=s,\,\phi(x^{\prime})<x_{n}<s(\ell+1)\}\times\mathbb{R}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\{(x^{\prime},s(\ell+1)):\,|x^{\prime}|<s\}\times\mathbb{R}.

To gain understanding of the function
w
on (
11.27
) we look the domain

\tilde{\Omega}_{0}=\tilde{\Omega}^{4}\setminus\overline{\tilde{\Omega}^{3}}=

[\{(x^{\prime},x_{n}):\,3<|x^{\prime}|<4,\,\phi(x^{\prime})<x_{n}<4(\ell+1)\}\cup\{(x^{\prime},x_{n}):\,|x^{\prime}|<4,\,3(\ell+1)<x_{n}<4(\ell+1)\}]\times\mathbb{R}.

x
x_{n}
x_{n}=\phi(x)
\phi
4\mathbb{Z}\cap\mathcal{O}
3\mathbb{Z}\cap\mathcal{O}
\tilde{\Omega}_{0}=\text{the shaded region}\times\mathbb{R}

Figure 2.
Sketch of domains in the
(x,x_{n})
plane.

Our goal is to establish that

\nabla w\in L^{2}(\Omega_{0}).

Given what we know about
u
and
v
we clearly have
\nabla w=\nabla u-\nabla v=0
when
t\leq-11
.
Also since (
11.23
) and (
11.25
) holds when
t\geq 22
we obtain by using
Lemma
3.10
and the exponential decay (Lemma
11.1
)
that

\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{0}\cap\{t\geq 22\})}\leq\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{0}\cap\{t\geq 22\})}+\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{0}\cap\{t\geq 22\})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.

On the component of the domain
\Omega_{0}

\{(x^{\prime},x_{n}):\,3<|x^{\prime}|<4,\,\phi(x^{\prime})<x_{n}<4(\ell+1)\}\times(-11,22)
we see that the boundary component (that coincides with
\partial\tilde{\Omega}
) belongs to the set
\mathcal{S}
and thus we get that
\nabla u\in L^{2}
, by
Lemma
3.10
when close to the boundary, and by its interior equivalent when inside the domain. The argument for
v
is similar - instead of (
11.26
) we use (
11.23
).

Finally the component of
\{(x^{\prime},x_{n}):\,|x^{\prime}|<4,\,3(\ell+1)<|x_{n}|<4(\ell+1)\}\times(-11,22)
of
\Omega_{0}
is in the interior of
\tilde{\Omega}
at the height of at least
O(1)\sim(\ell+1)
away from the boundary. It follows that for any
(x^{\prime},x_{n},\tau)
in this component we can find points
(q,\tau)\in\mathcal{S}
such that
(x^{\prime},x_{n},\tau)\in\Gamma_{a}(q,\tau)
, provided the slope
a
that defines nontangential cone
\Gamma_{a}
is sufficiently small. As this can always be assumed (change of slope only changes constants in the estimates but not the estimates themselves),

N(\nabla u)(q,\tau)
controls the
L^{2}
norm near the point
(x^{\prime},x_{n},\tau)
. In summary, we have that

\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{0})}\leq\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{0})}+\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{0})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.

We prove below (Lemma
11.30
) that
D^{1/2}_{t}w\in L^{2}(\Omega_{0})
with
\|D^{1/2}_{t}w\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{0})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}
. It then follows that
w
has trace in

\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega_{0})
and in particular as
\partial\tilde{\Omega}^{3}\setminus\partial\tilde{\Omega}\subset\partial\Omega_{0}
it also has trace in
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\tilde{\Omega}^{3})
as
w=0
on the remaining portion of its boundary
\partial\tilde{\Omega}^{3}\cap\partial\tilde{\Omega}
.

Recalling the section
2.2
where we have introduced energy solutions, there exists a unique energy solution to the PDE
L\tilde{w}=0
in
\tilde{\Omega}^{3}
such that Tr
\tilde{w}=
Tr
w
. Due to uniqueness established there
w=\tilde{w}
in
\tilde{\Omega}^{3}
and thus
w
itself is an energy solution on this domain
enjoying the estimate

\displaystyle\|w\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}}=\bigg(\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega}_{3})}^{2}+\|H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}v\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega}_{3})}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\lesssim\|\mbox{Tr }w\|_{\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\tilde{\Omega}^{3})}

\lesssim\|\nabla w\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{0})}+\|D^{1/2}_{t}w\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{0})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.

In particular, as
w=0
on
4\Delta
,
we obtain using Theorem
11.15
that

(11.28)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla w)\|_{L^{p}(3\Delta)}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.

Finally, as
v=u-w
and
u
has required estimates on
3\Delta
from (
11.23
), we get also that

(11.29)

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(3\Delta)}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.

This combined with (
11.26
) yields

\|\tilde{N}(\nabla v)\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{L}^{p}_{1,1/2}(\partial\Omega)}.
This proves the claim of Theorem
11.12
(modulo the following lemma):

Lemma 11.30
.

Assume that
\mathcal{O}_{1}\subset\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
are two bounded Lipschitz domains
such that the overlap of the boundaries
\partial\mathcal{O}_{1}\cap\partial\mathcal{O}
is a set of positive measure.
Assume that
r=\mbox{diam}(\mathcal{O})
and fix some
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

Then there exists
C>0
such that for any solution of the PDE
\mathcal{L}v=0
on
\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
vanishing on the set

(11.31)

\{(x,t)\in\partial\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}:\,\dist(x,\partial\mathcal{O}_{1})<\alpha r,\,t\in\mathbb{R}\}

the following estimate holds:

\|D^{1/2}_{t}v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{1}\times\mathbb{R})}\leq C\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})}.

To prove this we consider a cutoff function
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O})
such that

\varphi=1
on the set
\{(X,t)\in\mathcal{O}:\,\mbox{dist}(X,\mathcal{O}_{1})<\alpha r/2\}

and vanishes on the set
\{(X,t)\in\mathcal{O}:\,\mbox{dist}(X,\mathcal{O}_{1})\geq\alpha r\}
.
Also
|\nabla\varphi|\lesssim r^{-1}
.
Notice that this together with the condition (
11.31
) guarantees that
v\varphi=0
on
\partial\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}

and
v\varphi=v
on
\mathcal{O}_{1}\times\mathbb{R}
.

We clearly have

(11.32)

\iint_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\times\mathbb{R}}|D^{1/2}_{t}(v)|^{2}\leq\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}}D^{1/2}_{t}(v)^{2}\varphi^{2}=\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}}(D^{1/2}_{t}H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}v)H_{t}v\varphi^{2}.

Here
H_{t}
is the Hilbert transform in the time variable,
H_{t}^{2}=-I
. Since
D^{1/2}_{t}H_{t}D^{1/2}_{t}v=\partial_{t}v
we have after using the PDE v satisfies:

\iint_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\times\mathbb{R}}|D^{1/2}_{t}(v)|^{2}\leq\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{div}(A\nabla v)H_{t}v\varphi^{2}=-\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}}A\nabla v\cdot H_{t}(\nabla v)\varphi^{2}-2\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}}A\nabla v\cdot\nabla\varphi(u\varphi).

Recall that
H_{t}
is an isometry on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and hence

\int_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|H_{t}(\nabla v\varphi)|^{2}\,dt=\int_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|\nabla v\varphi|^{2}\,dt\leq\int_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|\nabla v|^{2}\varphi^{2}\,dt.

Hence the righthand side above has a bound (by Cauchy Schwarz and taking into account the support of
\varphi
):

(11.33)

\lesssim\iint_{\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}|\nabla v|^{2}+r^{-2}\iint_{\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}|v|^{2}.

For the second term we use Sobolev inequality. On a fixed time slice
t=t_{0}
, since

v
vanishes on
A
(which is a set of positive measure), we get:

r^{-2}\int_{\mathcal{O}}|v|^{2}\leq C\int_{\mathcal{O}}|\nabla v|^{2}.

Integrating in
t
and putting all inequalities together then yields:

\iint_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\times\mathbb{R}}|D^{1/2}_{t}(v)|^{2}\lesssim\iint_{\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}}|\nabla v|^{2},

which was our claim.

To establish that
D^{1/2}_{t}w\in L^{2}(\Omega_{0})
we take
\mathcal{O}_{1}\times\mathbb{R}=\Omega_{0}

and
\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}=\tilde{\Omega}^{4+\varepsilon}\setminus\overline{\tilde{\Omega}^{3-\varepsilon}}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
is taken to be a small enlargement of
\Omega_{0}
. Clearly the argument we gave on
\Omega_{0}
also works on this slightly enlarged domain. Observe that these two domains share the boundary on a piece of
\partial\tilde{\Omega}
where
w=0
and thus the assumption of our Lemma is satisfied. From this
D^{1/2}_{t}w\in L^{2}(\Omega_{0})
follows.
∎

Appendix A
Solvability of the Dirichlet problem for a block form matrix

In this section we prove solvability of the Dirichlet problem
\mathcal{L}u=0
for a matrix
A
in the block form.
Here as before
\mathcal{L}u=-\partial_{t}+\mathrm{div}(A\nabla\cdot)
and
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. We work with the operator
\mathcal{L}
, not
\mathcal{L}^{*}
, to maintain consistency with the rest of the paper, even though for our Regularity result we need solvability of the
L^{p}
Dirichlet problem for
\mathcal{L}^{*}
for all
p\in(1,\infty)
. However, the simple change of variables
(x,x_{n},t)\mapsto(x,x_{n},-t)
transforms the time-forward PDE into a time-backward one, so proving the result for
\mathcal{L}
will suffice.

Our objective is to establish solvability of the
L^{p}
Dirichlet problem for
\mathcal{L}
for all
p
close to
1
, as that automatically implies solvability for all
q>p
. Thus, without loss of generality, we fix some
1<p<2
. Recall the definition of the
p
-adapted square function (introduced originally in
[
18
]
) which is a modification of the usual square function defined earlier in (
2.24
)

(A.1)

S_{p,a}(w)(q,\tau):=\left(\iint_{\gamma_{a}(q,\tau)}\left|\nabla w(X,t)\right|^{2}\left|w(X,t)\right|^{p-2}x_{n}^{-n}dXdt\right)^{1/p}\quad\text{for }(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega,

where for simplicity we only work in the upper-half space and thus we use the nontagential cones
\gamma_{a}(q,\tau)
as in Section
10
. When clear, we drop the subscript
a
and only write
S_{p}(w)
.
(
A.1
) follows the convention that if both
|w(X,t)|=|\nabla w(X,t)|=0
the expression

\left|\nabla w(X,t)\right|^{2}\left|w(X,t)\right|^{p-2}
is considered to be zero.

Since
2-p>0
,
\left|u(X,t)\right|^{2-p}\leq N(u)^{2-p}(q,\tau)
for all
(X,t)\in\gamma(q,\tau)
.
With Hölder’s inequality, this gives

(A.2)

\displaystyle\|S_{2}(u)\|_{L^{p}}^{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}\left(\iint_{\gamma(q,\tau)}|\nabla u(X,t)|^{2}\left|u(X,t)\right|^{p-2}\left|u(X,t)\right|^{2-p}x_{n}^{-n}\,dX\,dt\right)^{p/2}dq\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}N(u)^{\frac{4-2p}{p}}\left(\iint_{\gamma(q,\tau)}|\nabla u(X,t)|^{2}\left|u(X,t)\right|^{p-2}x_{n}^{-n}\,dX\,dt\right)^{p/2}dq\,d\tau

\displaystyle\leq
