\displaystyle\varepsilon\int_{\partial\Omega}N(u)^{p}dq\,d\tau+C_{\varepsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega}S_{p}(u)^{p}dq\,d\tau=\varepsilon\|N(u)\|_{L^{p}}^{p}+C_{\varepsilon}\|S_{p}(u)\|_{L^{p}}^{p},

for all
\varepsilon>0
. Here
N(u)
is the sup-version of the nontangential maximal function.
Observe that this inequality holds trivially with
\varepsilon=0
when
p=2
and thus we can consider any
1<p\leq 2
. We prove an analog of Lemma
8.5
by integrating by parts in the quantity
\|S_{p}(u)\|_{L^{p}}^{p}
.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=-\partial_{t}+L
be a parabolic operator with matrix
A
in the block form (
8.1
), and let
u
be a bounded nonnegative energy solution to
\mathcal{L}u=0
in the infinite strip
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,r)\times\mathbb{R}
. Then for any
r>0
, we have

(A.4)

\lambda\int_{0}^{\frac{r}{2}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}u^{p-2}x_{n}\,dxdtdx_{n}+\frac{2C(p)}{r}\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\partial\Omega}|u|^{p}dxdtdx_{n}\\
\leq C(p)\int_{\partial\Omega}|u(x,r,t)|^{p}dxdt+C(p)\int_{\partial\Omega}|u(x,0,t)|^{p}dxdt,

where
C=C(p)>0
depending only on
p
.

The assumption that
u
is nonnegative
will make it much easier to handle expressions like the first term of (
A.4
). In particular, by Harnack, if
u(X,t)>0
then
u(Y,s)>0
for all
s>t
, and that means that
u^{p-2}(Y,s)
is well-defined. Furthermore, the assumption that
u\geq 0
is jsutified since an
L^{p}
function
f
on the boundary can be split into its positive and negative parts and the corresponding solutions
u^{+}
,
u^{-}
can be considered separately.
We postpone the proof until we establish the following corollary.

Corollary A.5
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=-\partial_{t}+L
be a parabolic operator with matrix
A
in the block form (
8.1
), and let
u
be a bounded nonnegative energy solution to
\mathcal{L}u=0
in
\Omega
. Then

(A.6)

\lambda\iint_{\Omega}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}u^{p-2}x_{n}\,dXdt\leq C\int_{\partial\Omega}|u(x,0,t)|^{p}dxdt,

where
C
depends only
p>1
.

Proof.

Consider a bounded
f\in\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
\|f\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}<\infty
as otherwise the claim is trivial. Let
u
be the energy solution of the equation
\mathcal{L}u=0
in
\Omega
with boundary datum
f
and consider also the energy solutions
for
1\leq k\in\mathbb{N}:

(A.7)

\mathcal{L}u_{k}=0\mbox{ on }\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,k)\times\mathbb{R},\quad u_{k}(x,0,t)=f(x,t),\quad u_{k}(x,k,t)=0,\quad\mbox{for all }(x,t)\in\partial\Omega.

For simplicity we extend
u_{k}
to
\Omega
by defining
u_{k}(x,x_{n},t)=0
when
x_{n}>k
, which extends each
u_{k}
continuously.
We now consider the limit of
u_{k}
, as
k\to\infty
. For each
k\geq 1
,
the Lax-Milgram lemma (c.f. subsection
2.2
) used on the domain
\Omega^{k}=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,k)\times\mathbb{R}
gives us

(A.8)

\|u_{k}\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}}=\|\nabla u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|H_{t}D_{t}^{1/2}u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\|\mbox{Tr }u_{k}\|_{\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega^{k})}=C\|f\|_{\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)},

where the constant
C\in(0,\infty)
depends on the coercivity constant of the
sesquilinear form (
2.13
)
in
\Omega^{k}
(which is uniform in
k
), and
\|A\|_{L^{\infty}}=\Lambda
. Therefore, this
bound is uniform in
k
. This uniformity and the fact that
\text{Tr}(u_{k})=f
, implies that a weak convergence argument
yields a sub-sequence convergent to some
v
with
\|v\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)}

and
\text{Tr}(v)=f
. This sub-sequence (which we somewhat imprecisely also call
(u_{k})
) is therefore strongly convergent to
v
in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})

by a standard functional analysis argument.
It follows that the
L^{2}
averages of
u_{k}
converge locally and uniformly to
the
L^{2}
averages of
v
in
C_{\rm loc}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
. Additionally, because each
u_{k}
has uniform interior Hölder continuity estimates, the convergence of
u_{k}\to v
also holds in
C^{\alpha}_{\rm loc}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R})
for some
\alpha>0
.

Taking the limit in
k
, it now follows that
v
must be a weak solution to
\mathcal{L}v=0
in
\Omega
. Since
\text{Tr}(v)=f
and
\|v\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}}<\infty
, it must hold that
u=v
since energy solutions are unique. Consider now
u_{k}-u
, a solution in
\Omega^{k}
enjoying the bound

(A.9)

\|u_{k}-u\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}}\leq C\|\mbox{Tr }(u_{k}-u)\|_{\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega^{k})}=C\|\mbox{Tr }u\|_{\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\{k\}\times\mathbb{R})}.

The trace
\|\mbox{Tr }u\|_{\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\{k\}\times\mathbb{R})}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, otherwise
\|u\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}}=\infty
, which is false. Thus the weak convergence
\nabla u_{k}\to\nabla u
in
L^{2}
upgrades to strong convergence.

Let
K
be any compact subset of
\Omega
on which
u\neq 0
. It follows that

u_{k}^{p-2}\to u^{p-2}
in
L^{\infty}(K)
. Note that the fact that
p<2
plays no role as
u\neq 0
and therefore, for some
k\geq k_{0}
, the solutions
u_{k}
are also bounded away from zero since
u_{k}\to u
uniformly on
K
.
Together with
\nabla u_{k}\to\nabla u
in
L^{2}
on
K
, we have

\lambda\iint_{K}\left|\nabla u_{k}\right|^{2}u_{k}^{p-2}x_{n}\,dXdt\to\lambda\iint_{K}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}u^{p-2}x_{n}\,dXdt.

We use (
A.4
) for each
u_{k}
with
r
so large such that
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,r/2)\times\mathbb{R}
. This means we need to only consider
k\geq r/2
. We obtain that

(A.10)

\lambda\iint_{K}\left|\nabla u_{k}\right|^{2}u_{k}^{p-2}x_{n}\,dXdt\leq\lambda\int_{0}^{\frac{k}{2}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\left|\nabla u_{k}\right|^{2}u_{k}^{p-2}x_{n}\,dxdtdx_{n}\\
\leq C(p)\left[\int_{\partial\Omega}|u_{k}(x,k,t)|^{p}dxdt+\int_{\partial\Omega}|u_{k}(x,0,t)|^{p}dxdt\right]=C(p)\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{p}dxdt.

Taking the limit
k\to\infty
on both sides therefore yields that

\lambda\iint_{K}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}u^{p-2}x_{n}\,dXdt\leq C(p)\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{p}dxdt,

on all compact subset
K\subset\Omega
on which
u\neq 0
. Taking the supremum over all of these sets gives

(A.11)

\lambda\iint_{\Omega\setminus{\{u\neq 0\}}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}u^{p-2}x_{n}\,dXdt\leq C\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{p}dxdt.

From this, our claim follows, since we use the convention that
\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}u^{p-2}=0
when both
u
and
\nabla u
vanish thus we must have
\left|\nabla u(X,t)\right|^{2}u(X,t)^{p-2}=0
when
t<t_{0}
for some
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
and
u>0
when
t>t_{0}
.
∎

We are ready to prove Lemma
A.3
. Pick an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
. Because
u
is an energy solution in the strip
\Omega^{r}=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,r)\times\mathbb{R}
the quantity below must be finite:

\iint_{\Omega^{r}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}(u+\varepsilon)^{p-2}x_{n}\,dXdt\leq\varepsilon^{p-2}r\|u\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}(\Omega^{r})}<\infty.

Hence using ellipticity we can write

\lambda\iint_{\Omega^{r}}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}(u+\varepsilon)^{p-2}x_{n}\,dXdt\leq\frac{1}{p-1}\iint_{\Omega^{r}}A\nabla u\cdot\nabla[(u+\varepsilon)^{p-1}]x_{n}\,dXdt,

and similarly for the localized version of this expression.

We recall the proof of Lemma
8.5
where a similar calculation is performed. Let
Q_{r}
,
\zeta
be as in that proof, then a calculation as in (
8.8
)-(
8.9
) gives

(A.12)

\int_{0}^{r}\int_{Q_{2r}}A\nabla u\cdot\nabla[(u+\varepsilon)^{p-1}](\zeta^{2}x_{n})dxdtdx_{n}\\
=\frac{r}{p}\int_{Q_{2r}}\partial_{n}\left((u+\varepsilon)^{p}\right)(x,r,t)\zeta(x,t)^{2}dxdt+\frac{1}{p}\int_{Q_{2r}}(u(x,0,t)+\varepsilon)^{p}\zeta(x,t)^{2}dxdt\\
-\frac{1}{p}\int_{Q_{2r}}(u(x,r,t)+\varepsilon)^{p}\zeta(x,t)^{2}dxdt-\frac{1}{p}\int_{0}^{r}\int_{Q_{2r}}x_{n}A_{\parallel}\nabla_{x}[(u+\varepsilon)^{p}]\cdot\nabla_{x}(\zeta^{2})dX\,dt\\
-\int_{0}^{r}\int_{Q_{2r}}\divg(A\nabla u)(u+\varepsilon)^{p-1}\zeta^{2}x_{n}\,dX\,dt.

For the last term we use the PDE
u
satisfies to obtain that it equals to

\frac{1}{p}\int_{0}^{r}\int_{Q_{2r}}(u+\varepsilon)^{p}\partial_{t}(\zeta^{2})x_{n}\,dX\,dt
(c.f. the calculation for
I_{1}
in the proof of the proof of Lemma
8.5
).
Observe that on the right-hand side of (
A.12
) all the powers are
p>1
and thus the limit
\varepsilon\to 0+
exists. Taking that limit, and using monotone convergence, we conclude that
(
A.12
) gives:

(A.13)

\int_{0}^{r}\int_{Q_{2r}}A\nabla u\cdot\nabla[u^{p-1}](\zeta^{2}x_{n})dxdtdx_{n}\\
=\frac{r}{p}\int_{Q_{2r}}\partial_{n}\left(u^{p}\right)(x,r,t)\zeta(x,t)^{2}dxdt+\frac{1}{p}\int_{Q_{2r}}u(x,0,t)^{p}\zeta(x,t)^{2}dxdt\\
-\frac{1}{p}\int_{Q_{2r}}u(x,r,t)^{p}\zeta(x,t)^{2}dxdt-\frac{1}{p}\int_{0}^{r}\int_{Q_{2r}}x_{n}A_{\parallel}\nabla_{x}[u^{p}]\cdot\nabla_{x}(\zeta^{2})dX\,dt\\
+\frac{1}{p}\int_{0}^{r}\int_{Q_{2r}}u^{p}\partial_{t}(\zeta^{2})x_{n}\,dX\,dt.

Now as in the proof of Lemma
8.5
we consider a partition of unity of the boundary of
\partial\Omega
and sum over it. This yields

(A.14)

\int_{0}^{r}\int_{\partial\Omega}A\nabla u\cdot\nabla[u^{p-1}]\,x_{n}\,dX\,dt\\
=\frac{r}{p}\int_{\partial\Omega}\partial_{n}\left(u^{p}\right)(x,r,t)dxdt+\frac{1}{p}\int_{\partial\Omega}u(x,0,t)^{p}dxdt-\frac{1}{p}\int_{\partial\Omega}u(x,r,t)^{p}dxdt.

Finally, for any fixed
r_{0}>0
, we integrate (
A.14
) in
r
variable over
[0,r_{0}]
and then divide both sides by
r_{0}
. This yields

\lambda(p-1)\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\left(x_{n}-\frac{x_{n}^{2}}{r_{0}}\right)\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}u^{p-2}x_{n}\,dXdt\leq\frac{1}{p}\int_{\partial\Omega}u(x,r_{0},t)^{p}dxdt+\frac{1}{p}\int_{\partial\Omega}u(x,0,t)^{p}dxdt\\
-\frac{2}{p\,r_{0}}\int_{0}^{r_{0}}\int_{\partial\Omega}u(x,x_{n},t)^{p}dxdtdx_{n}.

Truncating the integral on the left-hand side to
[0,\frac{r_{0}}{2}]
(and replacing
r_{0}
by
r
) we obtain (
A.4
) as desired with
C(p)=2(p-1)/p
.∎

From Lemma
A.3
together with (
A.2
) for
u_{k}
defined earlier by
(
A.7
), we see that for any
\varepsilon>0,

(A.15)

\|S^{k/2}_{2}(u_{k})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq\varepsilon\|N^{k/2}(u_{k})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+C_{\varepsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{p}\,dxdt,

where the superscript
k/2
denotes truncation of the corresponding cones at the height
k/2
and the constant
C_{\varepsilon}
is independent of
k
. However, Lemma
A.3
also gives us a second estimate that removes these truncations. The second term of the left-hand side of (
A.4
)
implies that
\|u_{k}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega^{k})}\leq Ck\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{p}\,dxdt
.
Consider now any
(q,\tau)\in\partial\Omega
and the expression

\iint_{\gamma(q,\tau)\cap\{x_{n}\in[k/2,\infty)\}}|\nabla u_{k}|^{2}x_{n}^{-n}dX\,dt.

As
u_{k}=0
for
x_{n}>k
the term is nonzero only for
x_{n}\in[k/2,k]
. Using boundary Cacciopoli (as
u_{k}
vanishes on the boundary
x_{n}=k
) it follows that

\iint_{\gamma_{a}(q,\tau)\cap\{x_{n}\in[k/2,\infty)\}}|\nabla u_{k}|^{2}x_{n}^{-n}dX\,dt\leq C\iint_{\gamma_{\tilde{a}}(q,\tau)\cap\{x_{n}\in[k/3,k)\}}|u_{k}|^{2}x_{n}^{-2-n}dX\,dt,

for some
\tilde{a}>a
. It then follows that

\left(\iint_{\gamma_{a}(q,\tau)\cap\{x_{n}\in[k/2,\infty)\}}|\nabla u_{k}|^{2}x_{n}^{-n}dX\,dt\right)^{1/2}\leq C\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{\gamma_{\tilde{a}}(q,\tau)\cap\{x_{n}\in[k/3,k)\}}|u_{k}|^{2}dX\,dt\right)^{1/2}\\
\leq C\sup_{\gamma_{\tilde{a}}(q,\tau)\cap\{x_{n}\in[k/3,k)\}}|u_{k}|.

Hence the square function at the point
(q,\tau)
above height
k/2
can be estimated by the nontangential maximal function at the same point above the height
k/3
with cones of slightly wider aperture.
We claim that we have

(A.16)

\int_{\partial\Omega}\left(\sup_{\gamma_{\tilde{a}}(q,\tau)\cap\{x_{n}\in[k/3,k)\}}|u_{k}|\right)^{p}dq\,d\tau\leq C\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{p}\,dxdt.

Assuming this holds then, combining it with (
A.15
), yields

(A.17)

\|S_{2}(u_{k})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq\varepsilon\|N(u_{k})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+C_{\varepsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{p}\,dxdt,

for a constant independent of
k
. It remains to show (
A.16
). The region
\gamma_{\tilde{a}}(q,\tau)\cap\{x_{n}\in[k/3,k)\}
lays inside a slightly larger region
T_{k}(q,\tau):=\{(x,x_{n},t):\,\|x-q,t-\tau\|<2\tilde{a}k,\,\,\,x_{n}\in(k/4,k)\}
,
from which, by boundary Hölder continuity (Lemma
3.12
),

\left(\sup_{\gamma_{\tilde{a}}(q,\tau)\cap\{x_{n}\in[k/3,k)\}}|u_{k}|\right)^{p}\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{T_{k}(q,\tau)}|u_{k}|^{p}.

We integrate this over
\partial\Omega
. The right-hand side becomes the solid integral

Ck^{-1}\|u_{k}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega\cap\{x_{n}\in(k/4,k)\})}.

The factor
k^{-1}
comes from the average over
T_{k}(q,\tau)
. That is, the average in the
x_{n}
variable takes place over an interval
(k/4,k)
of length
\sim k
and the integration over
\partial\Omega
removes the average from all remaining variables. Since
\|u_{k}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega^{k})}\leq Ck\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{p}\,dxdt
it follows that (
A.16
) holds.

Next, we establish the inequality
\|N(u_{k})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|S_{2}(u_{k})\|_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}
. Working with
u_{k}
allows us to use the a decay that we don’t have available for
u
, namely, that each
u_{k}\to 0
as
x_{n}\to\infty
- in fact
u_{k}=0
for
x_{n}>k
. This decay was one of the ingredients needed in Section
10
. With the
w
of defined in that section replaced by
u_{k}
, we construct the function
\hbar_{\nu,a}
as in (
10.9
) and claim that Lemma
10.10
holds as stated, noting the continuity of
u_{k}
. Observe that the nature of this stopping-time construction forces
\hbar_{\nu,a}<k
everywhere. Next we claim Lemma
10.15
holds as stated for the set

(A.18)

\big\{(x,t):\,N_{a}(u_{k})(x,t)>\nu\mbox{ and }S_{b}(u_{k})(x,t)\leq\gamma\nu\big\}

with
(x,t)\in 2R
and such that

(A.19)

\big|u_{k}\big(z,\hbar_{\nu,a}(w)(z,\tau),\tau\big)\big|>\nu/{2}\,\,\text{ for all }\,\,(z,\tau)\in R.

This requires a new argument as
S_{b}(u_{k})
only controls
u_{k}
in spatial variables but not in the
t
variable.
For such a point
(x,t)
in (
A.18
), part (iii) of Lemma
10.10
gives the existence of a point
(y,y_{n},s)\in\gamma_{a}(x,x_{n},t)
on the graph of
\hbar
with
u_{k}(y,y_{n},s)=\nu
.
We consider an interior parabolic cylinder
Q_{y_{n}/2}(y,y_{n},s)
. There exists
b=b(a)>0
such that

3/2Q_{y_{n}/2}(y,y_{n},s)\subset\gamma_{b}(x,t)
and hence (
A.19
) will follow if we prove that for all points inside
Q_{y_{n}/2}(y,y_{n},s)
the function
u_{k}>\nu/2
.

Using interior Hölder inequality Lemma
3.3
(or if
Q_{y_{n}/2}(y,y_{n},s)
intersects the hyperplane
\{x_{n}=k\}
the boundary Hölder) we always have

\sup_{(X_{1},t_{1}),(X_{2},t_{2})\in Q_{y_{n}/2}(y,y_{n},s)}|u_{k}(X_{1},t_{1})-u_{k}(X_{2},t_{2})|\leq C\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{3/2Q_{y_{n}/2}(y,y_{n},s)}|u_{k}-c|.

Considering a fixed time slice

B^{t_{0}}=3/2Q_{y_{n}/2}(y,y_{n},s)\cap\{t=t_{0}\}
we have by Sobolev inequality

\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B^{t_{0}}}|u_{k}-c|\,dX\leq Cy_{n}\,\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B^{t_{0}}}|\nabla u_{k}|\,dX,

which holds when
c
is an average of
u_{k}
over
B^{t_{0}}
. Let
\eta
be a smooth nonnegative cutoff
function in spatial variables, supported in
B^{t_{0}}
and satisfying
\iint_{B^{t_{0}}}\eta\,dX=1
and
|\nabla\eta|\leq y_{n}^{-1}\eta
. Define an average

v_{av}(t_{0})=\iint_{B^{t_{0}}}u_{k}(X,t_{0})\eta(X)\,dX.

With such choice of
c
, the Sobolev inequality holds, and therefore we have that

(A.20)

\sup_{(X_{1},t_{1}),(X_{2},t_{2})\in Q_{y_{n}/2}(y,y_{n},s)}|u_{k}(X_{1},t_{1})-u_{k}(X_{2},t_{2})|\\
\leq C\left[y_{n}\fint_{s-(y_{n}/2)^{2}}^{s+(y_{n}/2)^{2}}\,\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B^{t_{0}}}|\nabla u_{k}|\,dX\,dt_{0}+\sup_{t_{0},t_{0}^{\prime}\in(s-(y_{n}/2)^{2},s+(y_{n}/2)^{2})}|v_{av}(t_{0})-v_{av}(t_{0}^{\prime})|\right].

The first term in the last line is bounded by
CS_{b}(x,t)\leq C\gamma\lambda
(by Cauchy-Schwarz) and hence we can pick
C\gamma<1/4
.

We claim that the second term enjoys a similar estimate. Calculating as in
[
11
]
, we multiply the equation
\mathcal{L}u=0
by
\eta
and integrate it over the region

\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[t_{0},t_{0}^{\prime}]
, extending the function
u_{k}\eta
by zero outside the support of
\eta
.
Because
\eta
is a
function of the spatial variables only, we have

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t_{0}^{\prime}\}}u_{k}\eta\,dY-\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t_{0}\}}u_{k}\eta\,dY=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[t_{0},t_{0}^{\prime}]}(\partial_{t}u_{k})\eta\,dY\,dt

=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[t_{0},t_{0}^{\prime}]}\mbox{\rm div}(A\nabla u_{k})\eta\,dY\,dt=-\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times[t_{0},t_{0}^{\prime}]}(A\nabla u_{k})\nabla\eta\,dY\,dt.

The term
\nabla\eta
in the right-hand side integral is bounded by

y_{n}^{-1}
, and the length of the interval
[t_{0},t_{0}^{\prime}]
is bounded by
y_{n}^{2}
, therefore

\left|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{t_{0}^{\prime}\}}u_{k}\eta\,dY-\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\{t_{0}\}}u_{k}\eta\,dY\right|\lesssim C\left[y_{n}\fint_{s-(y_{n}/2)^{2}}^{s+(y_{n}/2)^{2}}\,\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.675pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.02336pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-2.53589pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern-1.5mu{-}\mkern-7.5mu{-}$}}\kern-1.7709pt}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int_{B^{t_{0}}}|\nabla u_{k}|\,dX\,dt_{0}\right],

which again has a bound by
CS_{b}(x,t)\leq C\gamma\lambda
. It follows that for sufficiently small
\gamma>0

we get that

\sup_{(X_{1},t_{1}),(X_{2},t_{2})\in Q_{y_{n}/2}(y,y_{n},s)}|u_{k}(X_{1},t_{1})-u_{k}(X_{2},t_{2})|\leq\lambda/2,

and hence
u_{k}>\lambda-\lambda/2=\lambda/2
on
Q_{y_{n}/2}(y,y_{n},s)
.
Hence we have Lemma
10.15
for the set (
A.18
).∎

With similar modification we therefore have Corollary
10.18
as well. We now state a version of Lemma

10.23
.

Lemma A.21
.

Let
\Omega={\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
and let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a block-form operator as above with
\mathcal{L}u_{k}=0
as in (
A.7
) extended by zero for
x_{n}>k
.

For a fixed (sufficiently large
a>0
), consider and an arbitrary function
\hbar:{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that it satisfies
the estimates (
10.11
), (
10.12
) and
\hbar\in[0,k)
.
Then
we have the following:

For all arbitrary parabolic surface balls
\Delta_{r}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
of radius
r
such that at least one point of
\Delta_{r}

the inequality
\hbar(x,t)\leq 2r
holds we have the following estimate for any
c\in\mathbb{R}
:

(A.22)

\int_{1/6}^{1}\int_{\Delta_{r}}\big|u_{k}\big(x,\theta\hbar(x,t),t\big)-c\big|^{2}\,dx\,dt\,d\theta\leq C\iint_{\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{r},\hbar)}\left|\nabla u_{k}\right|^{2}x_{n}\,dx_{n}dtdx\\
+\frac{C}{r}\iint_{\mathcal{K}_{1}}|u_{k}-c|^{2}\,dX\,dt,

for some
C\in(0,\infty)
that only depends on
a,\Lambda,n
but not on
k
,
u_{k}
,
c
or
\Delta_{r}
.
Here,
\mathcal{S}(\Delta_{r},\hbar)
and
\mathcal{K}_{1}
as are in Lemma

10.23
.
The cones used to define the nontangential
maximal functions in this lemma have vertices on
\partial\Omega
.

This is much simpler version of the previous Lemma for gradients, but there a new minor issue to be addressed since
u_{k}
only satisfies the PDE for
0<x_{n}<k
. In fact,
as
\theta\leq 1
and
\hbar<k
then
\theta\hbar<k
and hence we are integrating inside the region where our PDE holds. It follows that the proof of Lemma
A.21
is analogous, but simpler, since
\mathcal{L}u_{k}=0
.
Hence the claim simplifies to just (
A.22
).

Next we prove the following lemma.

Lemma A.23
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
and
u_{k}
be as in the previous lemma and fix
a>0
sufficiently large as above.
There exists
\kappa\in(0,1)
that depends only on
n
, constants
b>a
and
\gamma_{0}
that depend on
a
, and
C>0
that depends only on
n
and the ellipticity constants such that the following holds.

For any
\gamma\in(0,\gamma_{0})
,
\beta>0
, there holds

(A.24)

\left|\left\{(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}:\,{N}_{a}(u_{k})>\beta,S_{b}(u_{k})\leq\gamma\beta\right\}\right|\\
\leq C\gamma^{2}\left|\left\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}:M({N}_{a}(u_{k}))(x,t)>\kappa\beta\right\}\right|.

Proof.

We only sketch the proof, highlighting the differences from the proof of Lemma
10.39
.
Let

(A.25)

E_{1,\beta}:=\left\{(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}:\,{N}_{a}(u_{k})>\beta,S_{b}(u_{k})\leq\gamma\beta\right\}.

We proceed is in Lemma
10.39
but skip defining the average
\vec{\eta}_{L^{2}}
as we now work with
N
instead of
\tilde{N}
. Hence in the proof whenever we see
\vec{\eta}_{L^{2}}
we replace it by
u_{k}
. Proceeding exactly as in the proof of Lemma
10.39
we arrive to (
10.48
). The argument given previously for (
10.50
). applies as well to
u_{k}
and in particular it is useful for the estimate
(
A.20
), leading to the claim that:

(A.26)

\left|\int_{20\Delta_{i}}\left|u_{k}-c\right|^{2}(x,\hbar_{i}(x,t),t)dxdt-\int_{20\Delta_{i}}\left|u_{k}-c\right|^{2}(x,\theta\hbar_{i}(x,t),t)dxdt\right|\leq C\gamma^{2}\beta^{2}|\Delta_{i}|,

for
\theta\in[1/6,1]

which allows us to replace (
10.48
) by

(A.27)

\int_{10\Delta_{i}}|u_{k}-c|^{2}(x,\hbar_{i}(x,t),t)dxdt\leq C\int_{1/6}^{1}\int_{20\Delta_{i}}\left|u_{k}-c\right|^{2}(x,\theta\hbar_{i}(x,t),t)dxdtd\theta+C\gamma^{2}\beta^{2}|\Delta_{i}|.

We then proceed analogously (with fewer terms to handle) until we reach

\frac{1}{r_{i}}\iint_{\mathcal{K}_{1}}|u_{k}-c|^{2}\,dX\,dt.

Previously we used the area function to take care of variations in time variable. Instead,
(
A.20
) allows us to bound the oscillation of
u
in terms of truncated version of
S_{b}(u_{k})
. This concludes the argument.
∎

Under the assumption that
\|N(u_{k})\|_{L^{p}}<\infty
we then can conclude that, for all
1<p<\infty
, there exists
C=C(n,p,\lambda,\Lambda)>0
such that

(A.28)

\|N(u_{k})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\|S_{2}(u_{k})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}.

Let us address briefly why the apriori bound
\|N(u_{k})\|_{L^{p}}<\infty
is justified.

Let
f:\partial\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be a compactly supported Lipschitz function, a class of functions that are dense in
L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
,
p<\infty
. Moreover, as
f
has a compact Lipschitz extension into
\Omega
,
f
has a well-defined trace in the space
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)
. Splitting
f=f^{+}-f^{-}
into positive and negative parts, each of which are Lipschitz, we can again consider separately energy solutions with data
f^{+}
and
f^{-}
.

Let
u_{k}
be a solution of (
A.7
) with such nonnegative boundary datum (we call again
f
). Let
\Delta

be ball containing the support of
f
.
As
f
is bounded, so is
u_{k}
by the maximum principle. Hence we see that
for all
(p,\tau)\in 3\Delta
we have
N(u_{k})(p,\tau)\leq K<\infty
. This ensures
L^{p}
integrability of
N(u_{k})
on the set
3\Delta
.
Away from
3\Delta
, as
u_{k}
is also nonnegative, the decay of
u_{k}
is comparable to the decay of the corresponding Green’s function ((
3.8
)) by the comparison principle.

Furthermore, the set
3\Delta\times(0,k)
is bounded in the domain
\Omega^{k}
and by the maximum principle
u_{k}
is bounded on the boundary of this set. We pick a pole
(Y,s)
with time
s
some distance before the beginning of the support of
f
and, as
G>0
on
\partial(3\Delta\times(0,k))
, there exists a constant
M>0
for which

0<u_{k}(x,x_{n},t)\leq MG((x,x_{n},t),(Y,s)),\qquad\mbox{for all }t>s,\,x_{n}\in(0,k),\,\mbox{ and }\,(x,t)\in\partial\Omega)\setminus 3\Delta,

Here,
G
is the Green’s function of the whole space given by (
3.8
).
The Green’s function
G
has sufficient decay - exponential in the spatial variables and polynomial in
t
of order at
least
C(1+t)^{-1}
-
which makes it
t
-integrable in
L^{p}
for
p>1
on an interval
(0,\infty)
),
and thus ensures that
N(u_{k})
will be integrable in
L^{p}
on sets of type

\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,k)\times(0,\infty)
.
While this may be a crude bound, it shows that (
A.28
) holds for all
u_{k}
with a
C
that only depends on
n,p,\lambda
and
\Lambda
but not on
k
.

With (
A.28
) established for non-negative
u_{k}
and with Lipschitz, nonnegative, compactly supported data, it will follow that for any
1<p\leq 2
, there exists
C>0
independent of
k
, such that

\|N(u_{k})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\|S_{2}(u_{k})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq C\varepsilon\|N(u_{k})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+CC_{\varepsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{p}\,dxdt,

where we have used (
A.17
). Choosing
\varepsilon>0
such that
C\varepsilon<1/2
by finiteness of

\|N(u_{k})\|_{L^{p}}<\infty
implies that

\|N(u_{k})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq 2C\|S_{2}(u_{k})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq 2CC_{\varepsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{p}\,dxdt,

and hence
u_{k}
enjoys both square and nontangential maximal function bounds.

Recall that we have already shown that
u_{k}\to u
locally uniformly on compact subsets of
\Omega
, where
u
solves the PDE
\mathcal{L}u=0
in
\Omega
with datum
f
. Taking limits of notangential maximal functions truncated to these compact subsets yields the same bound for
u
, namely that

\|N(u)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq 2CC_{\varepsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{p}\,dxdt.

Similar square function bounds follow by using
\nabla u_{k}\to\nabla u
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
,
and obtaining that, in every compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
,

\int_{K\cap\gamma(p,\tau)}|\nabla u_{k}|^{2}x_{n}^{-n}dX\,dt\to\int_{K\cap\gamma(p,\tau)}|\nabla u|^{2}x_{n}^{-n}dX\,dt,

for all boundary points
(p,\tau)
. Integrating and taking the supremum over all such
K
yields

\|S_{2}(u)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq 2C_{\varepsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega}f^{p}\,dxdt.

Finally, applying this to
f^{+}
and
f^{-}
separately gives:

\|N(u)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\leq\|N(u^{+})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}+\|N(u^{-})\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}

\leq 2CC_{\varepsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega}(f^{+})^{p}\,dxdt+2CC_{\varepsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega}(f^{-})^{p}\,dxdt\leq 2CC_{\varepsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega}|f|^{p}\,dxdt,

with an analogous bound for
\|S_{2}(u)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}
.
This shows the solvability of the
L^{p}
Dirichlet problem for
\mathcal{L}
on
\Omega
. The compactly supported Lipschitz functions are dense in
L^{p}(\partial\Omega)
so there exists a unique continuous extension of this solution operator
f\mapsto u=u(f)
onto all of
L^{p}
that satisfies the required bound

\|N(u)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\approx\|S_{2}(u)\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}\lesssim\|f\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\partial\Omega)}
. This solution operator is close to the energy solutions in the following sense. If
f\in L^{p}(\partial\Omega)\cap\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)
, then
u
obtained as

u=u(f)
is also an energy solution with its energy norm
\|u\|_{\dot{\mathsf{E}}}
controlled by the
\dot{H}{\vphantom{H}}^{1/4}_{\partial_{t}-\Delta_{x}}(\partial\Omega)
norm of
f
and Tr
u=f
.
